**Summary.** The paper introduces a configuration-based framework to unify the understanding of superradiant phase transitions in Dicke lattices. By classifying stable configurations based on lattice symmetry, it maps out complex, multi-stable phase diagrams for both open (dissipative) and closed quantum systems. This provides a comprehensive picture connecting local atomic/photonic arrangements to global thermodynamic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful structural tool (configuration-based understanding) to organize complex many-body dynamics in open quantum systems like cavity QED arrays, unifying disparate findings on multistability and phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a unified, configuration-based understanding of how multistability arises and relates different superradiant phases in Dicke lattices.

**Main result.** The authors classify superradiant phases based on lattice symmetry configurations, demonstrating that the system can exhibit up to four stable phases for $N=4$ sites. They also contrast the behavior between dissipative (nonequilibrium) and closed (equilibrium) systems regarding universality classes.

**Method.** The analysis employs a mean-field approach derived from the Lindblad master equation for the open system, supplemented by stability analysis via linearization around steady states. For equilibrium, ground state energy minimization is used.

**Model / system.** The model is the Dicke lattice, consisting of coupled atom-resonator units on a 1D lattice ($N$ sites). The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian and dissipation (Lindblad equation), incorporating photon hopping ($\xi$).

**Key observables.** Superradiant phases (NP vs. SRP), local order parameters ($R_j, I_j$), coexistence of stable steady states, and critical exponents ($\gamma_{open}, \gamma_{closed}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon hopping strength ($\xi$), dissipation rate ($\kappa$), coupling strengths ($g$), and lattice size ($N=4, 5, 6$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mean-field approximation for correlations ($\langle AB angle \simeq \langle A angle \langle B angle$) and sometimes uses the Discrete Truncated Wigner Approximation (DTWA).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate representative superradiant configurations for $N=5$ and $N=6$, and show scaling behavior of on-site fluctuations ($\Delta_j$) near critical couplings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and then develops a configuration classification scheme. It analyzes the dissipative case to map out the phase diagram, followed by an extension to the closed lattice to compare equilibrium vs. nonequilibrium universality classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The emergence of multiple superradiant phases in Dicke lattice models has attracted considerable attention in the quantum optics community. However, a unified understanding of the origin of multistability and its relation to different superradiant phases is still lacking. Here, we develop a configuration-based understanding to classify the superradiant phases in Dicke lattices. We show that photon hopping naturally organizes the possible superradiant configurations according to the lattice symmetry, providing a unified interpretation of the nonequilibrium phase diagram and the emergence of multistability. For the dissipative four-site Dicke lattice, we obtain the complete phase diagram and identify the coexistence of up to four stable superradiant phases. The proposed classification is further extended to five- and six-site lattices. Moreover, we demonstrate that the same configuration-based understanding also applies to the closed Dicke lattice, where the ground state uniquely selects one of the allowed configurations. Finally, we show that different configurations may belong to either same or distinct nonequilibrium universality classes in the dissipative Dicke lattice, while they share the same equilibrium universality class in the closed Dicke lattice. Our results provide a unified picture for understanding equilibrium and nonequilibrium superradiant phase transitions in Dicke lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the statistics of work done on monitored Ising chains under quantum quenches and stochastic measurements. It demonstrates that frequent monitoring drives the system's work distribution towards Gaussian behavior, while controlled jumps reveal distinct linear versus sublinear growth rates based on causal correlations. This offers deep insights into how measurement backaction shapes non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a quantitative framework for understanding how measurement backaction fundamentally alters non-equilibrium thermodynamics, moving beyond simple average energy calculations to characterize fluctuations in work.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how work statistics evolve in monitored quantum systems subjected to both quantum quenches and continuous measurement processes.

**Main result.** Frequent monitoring drives the work distribution towards an essentially Gaussian form, while controlled measurements reveal distinct growth regimes depending on whether energy increments are causally disconnected or correlated within a light cone.

**Method.** The analysis employs deriving trajectory-resolved generating functions for work statistics and evaluating them using fermionic Gaussian-state formalisms.

**Model / system.** The system is the transverse-field Ising chain, whose dynamics are governed by stochastic quantum jumps (unraveling of a Lindblad master equation) or controlled deterministic projections.

**Key observables.** Work distribution P(W), work generating function G(u), mean work <W>, and variance σ²_W.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement rate (γ), number of detection events, transverse field strength (h), and the causal separation between measurement jumps (inside/outside light cone).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Central Limit Theorem to explain the approach to Gaussianity for large numbers of weakly correlated increments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition of P(W) from discrete peaks to Gaussian shapes as monitoring strength increases, and compare work statistics under isolated quenches versus monitored dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes work statistics across different measurement protocols: stochastic jumps (showing crossover), controlled deterministic jumps (highlighting causality effects), and the combined effect of quenching with continuous observation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate work statistics in monitored transverse-field Ising chains subject to both a quantum quench of the transverse field and either stochastic quantum jumps or controlled measurement sequences. For generalized measurements, we derive a trajectory-resolved generating function for work statistics in the two-point energy measurement scheme. Evaluating it using a fermionic Gaussian-state formalism, we show that, under stochastic jump dynamics, the work distribution crosses over from a comb-like structure to an essentially Gaussian form with shrinking sub-Gaussian tails, as the number of detection events grows. For controlled jump protocols, the energy added by each jump is constant when successive jumps are causally disconnected but decreases and then saturates when they lie within each other's light cone, yielding linear and sublinear growth of the average work, respectively. For monitored quenches, continuous observation washes out the fine structure of the isolated-quench distribution and again drives the statistics toward Gaussian behavior.

</details>

### [Coupling effect of nearest-neighbor interacting qubit chains to a single qubit system](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00307v1)

**Authors:** Manuel Allan L. Orongan, Lemuel John F. Sese, Rayda P. Gammag  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00307v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `Rydberg arrays` **1/5** · `correlated cavity matter` **1/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5** · `spintronics-quantum-optics interface` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper develops a theorem to filter out Hamiltonian terms that do not affect local system dynamics. By testing this theorem on various qubit chain models, the authors demonstrate its predictive power. Crucially, they show that when the underlying commutation relations break down, 'emergent couplings' can generate entanglement between subsystems that never directly interact.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful theoretical tools for simplifying complex open quantum systems by identifying irrelevant couplings, while simultaneously revealing how non-trivial dynamics (like entanglement) can arise from seemingly 'irrelevant' or non-commuting interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a rigorous theorem identifying Hamiltonian terms that do not affect local dynamics or observables, and to analyze when this theorem breaks down.

**Main result.** The study shows that while certain interactions can be ignored for local dynamics based on commutation relations, non-commuting intermediate interactions can generate entanglement between non-directly coupled subsystems via an emergent coupling mechanism.

**Method.** The analysis uses partial tracing over environment degrees of freedom applied to the von Neumann equation, and employs the interaction picture formalism for time evolution calculations.

**Model / system.** The models involve qubit chains interacting with a central local system, incorporating Ising interactions, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) coupling, and transverse magnetic fields on environment qubits.

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrix ($\hat{ho}_0(t)$), Concurrence ($C(\hat{ho}_{02})$), and the presence/absence of specific Hamiltonian terms in $\hat{ho}_0(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths (e.g., $G_{12}$ vs $G_{01}$) and external field strengths ($\omega_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theorem relies on specific commutation relations $[\hat{H}, \hat{H}_{E2}] = 0$; the entanglement analysis assumes initial unbiased probability amplitudes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 analysis shows that concurrence generation is maximized when the transverse field strength ($\omega_1$) is comparable to the coupling strengths, and vanishes if interactions are too weak or non-commuting terms are absent.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces a theorem for identifying irrelevant Hamiltonian terms. It then applies this theorem to simple qubit chain models (Ising/DM) where it holds true. Finally, it examines a third system where the commutation relation fails, demonstrating emergent coupling and calculating entanglement using Concurrence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This study establishes a theorem that provides sufficient criteria for identifying terms in any time-independent Hamiltonian that have no influence on local dynamics, local observables, or any local phenomena, without any approximation. The usefulness of this theorem is demonstrated by predicting the behavior of three systems. The first system consists of multiple qubit chains with Ising interactions. The second system is formed by a single qubit chain, differentiated by the substitution of Dzyalonshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction for the last Ising interaction. The predictions were verified by analytically deriving the reduced dynamics of both systems. A third system was also considered, namely, a short qubit chain with a transverse magnetic field on the intermediary environment qubit. This transverse magnetic field signals that the commutation relation required by our theorem no longer holds. The third system's local dynamics were also derived analytically, demonstrating that all Hamiltonian constituents contributed to the reduced dynamics. The physical consequences of our theorem's inapplicability were also explored using this third system by analyzing the entanglement dynamics between the qubits that do not directly interact. The results showed that the entanglement is caused by an \textit{emergent coupling} between the two non-directly interacting subsystems. This \textit{emergent coupling} arises from the non-commutativity of the intermediate interactions connecting the two subsystems. When searching for these \textit{emergent couplings}, our theorem can eliminate intermediate interactions that will not produce them.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines polaron-polaritons formed by interacting excitons coupled to light within a semiconductor microcavity. By incorporating dissipative coupling, the authors show that many-body correlations lead to the emergence of exceptional points (EPs) in the energy spectrum. This identifies dissipative polariton systems as a versatile platform for exploring non-Hermitian physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it merges concepts from open quantum systems (dissipation/non-Hermiticity) with many-body physics (exciton interactions), providing a tunable platform for studying non-Hermitian dynamics in AMO and quantum optics contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how combining strong quantum correlations with dissipation shapes collective excitations, specifically focusing on polaron-polaritons.

**Main result.** The interplay between many-body correlations and non-Hermitian coupling generates anomalous dispersion relations and the emergence of exceptional points (EPs) in the polaron-polariton spectrum.

**Method.** The analysis employs finite-temperature Green's function formalism, solving for the polariton spectrum by finding the zeros of the determinant of the inverse propagator matrix.

**Model / system.** The system models exciton-polaritons in a microcavity semiconductor exhibiting strong coupling and biexciton resonance. It involves coupled light-matter interactions ($\hat{H}_{lm}$) and inter-species correlations ($\hat{H}_{int}$).

**Key observables.** Exceptional Points (EPs), anomalous dispersion relations, polaron-polariton energy branches (Lower, Middle, Upper), and level attraction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipative coupling strength ($\Omega_{Im}$), relative decay rates ($\gamma_x, \gamma_c$), and the biexciton resonance detuning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed under the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA) and assumes a strong coupling regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate dissipative coupling polariton branches showing curvature changes and EPs; other figures map polaron-polaritons vs. momentum/detuning for varying dissipative coupling strength, demonstrating level attraction leading to EP coalescence.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the Hamiltonian, uses Green's functions to incorporate interactions via a self-energy ($\Sigma$), solves the resulting determinant equation to find the spectrum, and analyzes how non-Hermitian terms generate EPs across different polaron-polariton branches.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how strong correlations and dissipation combine to shape collective quantum excitations is a central challenge in many-body physics. We investigate the effect of dissipative light-matter coupling on strongly interacting exciton-polaritons in the presence of a biexciton resonance, which gives rise to polaron-polariton quasiparticles. We show that the interplay between many-body correlations and non-Hermitian coupling generates anomalous dispersion relations and exceptional points in the polaron-polariton spectrum. The location and coexistence of exceptional points are controlled by the dissipative coupling and the relative decay rates of the excitonic and photonic constituents, allowing them to emerge across different polaron-polariton branches. These results identify dissipative polaron-polaritons as a versatile platform for exploring non-Hermitian many-body physics with tunable light-matter quasiparticles.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using a photonic interferometer with birefringent quartz to simulate arbitrary single-qubit open dynamics. By treating the photon's frequency as an effective environment, they derive a controllable mechanism that allows simulating non-Markovian and general decoherence processes. This establishes polarization-frequency engineered interferometers as a versatile tool for quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally accessible optical protocol—'optical reservoir engineering'—to simulate complex quantum dissipation channels beyond simple Markovian decay.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to simulate arbitrary single-qubit open-system dynamics using a controllable photonic platform.

**Main result.** The setup successfully simulates general qubit decoherence, including non-Markovian effects like coherence revivals, by engineering the coupling between polarization and frequency degrees of freedom.

**Method.** The simulation involves analyzing the partial trace over the continuous frequency environment degree of freedom after a photon passes through a birefringent quartz plate within an interferometer.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single photon in an open Mach-Zehnder interferometer containing a birefringent quartz plate. The qubit dynamics are governed by coupling polarization (the system) to the frequency spectrum (the environment).

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrix elements ($ho_{00}, ho_{01}$), mean fidelity, and output photon probabilities ($p_j$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frequency distribution parameters ($\mu, \sigma$), quartz plate thickness ($\ell$), initial populations, and relative phase ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The frequency environment is modeled using a Gaussian distribution, and the analysis relies on partial tracing over this continuous spectrum.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (MZI with quartz), show probability distributions vs. $\mu$ or $\sigma$, and compare simulated qubit evolution (e.g., amplitude damping) against theoretical benchmarks like depolarization channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the derivation of the output state by tracing out frequency, derives measurable quantities ($\eta_\lambda, \kappa_A$), and then applies this formalism to benchmark simulations for various open dynamics regimes (depolarizing, non-Markovian).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a photonic platform for simulating arbitrary single-qubit open-system dynamics using a single photon in an open Mach-Zehnder interferometer. A birefringent quartz plate induces a coupling between the polarization and frequency degrees of freedom. By treating the latter as an effective environment, we analytically derive the reduced polarization dynamics. We show that the resulting evolution is characterized by a controllable interplay between populations and coherence, instead of the usual dephasing caused by quartz plates. By adjusting the photon frequency distribution and interferometric parameters, we demonstrate that target single-qubit states can be efficiently reproduced through a tunable optical protocol expected to work under accessible experimental conditions. The simulator is benchmarked against paradigmatic open-system evolutions, including depolarization and non-Markovian dynamics, achieving high accuracy. Our results establish polarization-frequency engineered photonic interferometers as a versatile protocol for simulation of open quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the Mott transition in 1D fermionic chains coupled to dissipative baths. It demonstrates that dissipation fundamentally alters the phase diagram, splitting the single transition into two distinct critical points: an LL-to-Dissipative Phase (DP) BKT transition, followed by a DP-to-MI transition. This shows that environmental coupling can generate novel critical behavior in strongly correlated matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how environmental coupling (dissipation) can qualitatively change fundamental quantum phase transitions in low-dimensional strongly correlated systems, which is highly relevant to experimental realizations in cold atoms or superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how coupling a one-dimensional Mott transition system to local dissipative baths fundamentally alters the nature of the quantum phase transition.

**Main result.** Dissipation splits the conventional Mott transition into two distinct critical phenomena: an LL $	o$ DP BKT transition, followed by a new DP $	o$ MI commensurate-incommensurate transition for bath exponents $s < 3/2$.

**Method.** The analysis combines bosonization techniques with exact integration of bath degrees of freedom to derive an effective field theory and analyze the resulting critical behavior using Renormalization Group (RG) methods.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional interacting fermionic model exhibiting a Mott transition between a Luttinger liquid (LL) and a Mott insulator (MI), with dissipation introduced via coupling to local harmonic oscillator baths.

**Key observables.** Compressibility ($\kappa$), superfluid stiffness ($ho_s$), critical exponents ($eta, 
u, z$), and the DC conductivity $\sigma(\omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bath exponent $s$ (characterizing the bath spectrum $J(\omega) \sim |\omega|^s$) is crucial, with thresholds at $s=1$ and $s=3/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on bosonization, exact integration of the bath degrees of freedom, and perturbative RG techniques applied to the effective action.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the phase diagram showing LL $	o$ DP $	o$ MI for $s < 3/2$, contrasting with direct LL $	o$ MI for $s > 3/2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the effective action via bosonization and bath integration, then analyzing the resulting critical points using RG flow equations to determine how the transition splits into two distinct universality classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how dissipation modifies quantum phase transitions is a central challenge in many-body physics. A paradigmatic example is the one-dimensional Mott transition, which in isolated systems separates a conducting Luttinger liquid (LL) from a Mott insulator (MI). Here, we study the fate of this transition in the presence of dissipative baths locally coupled to the density. Using bosonisation and an exact integration of the bath degrees of freedom, we show that dissipation fundamentally reshapes the phase diagram for bath exponents $s<3/2$, where $s$ characterises the low-energy bath spectrum. Rather than undergoing a direct LL-MI transition, the system develops an intermediate dissipative phase (DP) that is compressible and gapless, yet has zero superfluid stiffness. As a result, the conventional Mott transition splits into two distinct critical phenomena: a Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition from the LL to the DP, followed by a new commensurate-incommensurate transition from the DP to the MI. We derive an effective field theory for the latter transition and characterize its universality. For $1<s<3/2$, the critical exponents vary continuously with the bath exponent as $β=ν=1/z=s-1$, while for $s<1$ the transition is governed by $β=ν=1/z=0$ and the doping vanishes sharper than any power law. State-of-the-art Monte Carlo simulations quantitatively support our predictions. These results demonstrate that dissipation can qualitatively alter the nature of the Mott transition and generate novel critical behaviour in strongly correlated one-dimensional systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the robustness of quantum correlations, specifically Quantum Discord, in a double quantum dot system driven by external biases. Using advanced theoretical techniques like the Quantum Langevin equation, the authors show that discord persists robustly in the non-equilibrium steady state. This establishes that environmental coupling and asymmetry can be engineered to control and maintain nonclassical quantum features in solid-state devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems and non-equilibrium thermodynamics, which are central to modern AMO physics and mesoscopic quantum transport studies, providing quantitative tools (Discord) beyond simple entanglement measures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum correlations, specifically Quantum Discord, behave during non-equilibrium electronic transport processes.

**Main result.** Quantum discord remains finite in the steady state over a broad parameter range, demonstrating that nonequilibrium transport provides an effective means of controlling nonclassical correlations.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Quantum Langevin Equation formalism to derive the exact reduced density matrix, allowing for the calculation of time-dependent and steady-state quantum and classical correlations.

**Model / system.** The system is a Double Quantum Dot (DQD) coupled to fermionic reservoirs (Left and Right), modeled by a total Hamiltonian including system, bath, and interaction terms. The analysis covers transient dynamics under thermal bias.

**Key observables.** Quantum Discord ($D(ho_{AB})$), Classical Correlation ($C(ho_{AB})$), Total Mutual Information ($I(ho_{AB})$), and their ratio $D/C$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System-reservoir coupling strength ($\Gamma$), spectral bandwidth ($W_\alpha$), thermal bias ($\Delta T$), and reservoir asymmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism uses the quantum Langevin equation to retain non-Markovian memory effects, avoiding Markovian approximations. The calculation of classical correlation relies on projective measurements on one subsystem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate time evolution (transient dynamics) showing convergence to a steady state, and steady-state dependence on coupling strength, bandwidth, and thermal bias, highlighting the role of asymmetry.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the non-equilibrium dynamics by first establishing the theoretical framework using Langevin equations. It then examines how initial states affect transient behavior before focusing on the robust, parameter-dependent steady-state values for quantum discord versus classical correlation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum discord captures quantum correlations beyond entanglement and can remain finite even when the entanglement vanishes. We investigate the transient nonequilibrium dynamics and steady-state behavior of quantum discord and classical correlations in a double quantum dot (DQD) system coupled to fermionic reservoirs. By employing a quantum Langevin equation formalism, we obtain the exact reduced density matrix of the system, enabling a comprehensive analysis of its quantum and classical correlations under nonequilibrium conditions. The influence of system-reservoir coupling strength, spectral bandwidth, thermal bias, and varying initial state on both the transient dynamics and steady-state correlations is systematically analyzed. Quantum discord remains finite in the nonequilibrium steady state over a broad parameter range. Although thermal gradients reduce the overall magnitude of correlations, quantum discord persists and exhibits greater resilience. These results demonstrate that nonequilibrium electronic transport, together with the environmental spectral properties and reservoir asymmetry, provides an effective means of controlling nonclassical correlations in mesoscopic systems and establishes quantum discord as a robust hallmark of open fermionic quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates predicting how much a time-evolved quantum state remains in its low-energy manifold after a quench in a Heisenberg spin chain. It compares measurement-based shadow learning against coherent Quantum CNNs, finding that no single method is universally superior; the best approach depends critically on whether the system dynamics are weakly, moderately, or strongly perturbed.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how to extract complex, non-local many-body information (like subspace purity) from quantum measurements using machine learning techniques, providing crucial insights into the limitations of current quantum characterization methods for dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is the supervised prediction of subspace overlaps ($\mathcal{O}_K$) between time-evolved quantum states and specified low-energy eigenspaces following a local quench.

**Main result.** The optimal learning strategy for predicting these overlaps is highly dependent on the quench regime: physics-informed QCNNs are robust, while measurement-based shadow methods excel in moderate quench regimes but fail in weak/strong quenches.

**Method.** The study compares two quantum information extraction strategies—measurement-based (using classical CNNs on randomized measurements) and coherent quantum learning (using Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks)—to learn the overlap observable.

**Model / system.** The system is a 10-qubit one-dimensional Heisenberg spin chain, whose dynamics are studied after being subjected to a local perturbation (quench). The Hamiltonian governs the time evolution $|\psi(t)angle = e^{-iHt} |\psi_0angle$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the subspace overlap $\mathcal{O}_K$, quantified by the coefficient of determination ($R^2$) when predicting it from quantum measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quench strength ($h$), which defines five dataset configurations (D1-D5) spanning weak, moderate, and strong quench regimes; measurement shot budgets ($	ext{Shots}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dominant limitation observed during hardware validation is the resource cost and noise associated with arbitrary state preparation circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate distributions of $\mathcal{O}_K$ across different quench strengths, compare $R^2$ scores between shadow CNNs and QCNNs across regimes, and show predicted vs. true overlaps for various models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem setup (Heisenberg chain, subspace overlap), details two competing learning paradigms (Shadow vs. QCNN), presents results showing regime-dependent performance trade-offs, validates findings on noisy hardware, and concludes with a comparison of resource costs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Predicting the overlap of quantum states with specified low-energy subspaces is a key diagnostic for quantum many-body dynamics, with direct applications in state preparation, subspace-based algorithms, and the study of thermalization. We study the supervised prediction of subspace overlaps O_K between time-evolved states and K-dimensional low-energy eigenspaces of a 10-qubit Heisenberg spin chain following a local perturbation. We compare two quantum information extraction strategies: measurement-based learning, in which classical shadow features are processed by convolutional neural networks, and coherent quantum learning, in which quantum convolutional neural networks process the state directly. We further introduce physics-informed variants for both approaches, including Hamiltonian-aware shadows and QCNN gates aligned with the Heisenberg exchange structure. Across five dataset configurations spanning weak, moderate, and strong quench regimes, physics-informed QCNNs achieve stable performance, with mean test-set coefficients of determination R^2 = 0.753-0.846. Shadow-based methods show stronger regime dependence: they outperform QCNNs in the moderate-quench regime, reaching R^2 = 0.886, but underperform in weak and strong quenches at default shot budgets, where the best shadow results are R^2 = 0.615 and 0.672, respectively. Hardware validation on Quantinuum and IBM noise models shows that arbitrary state preparation is the dominant limitation, requiring approximately 2,044 two-qubit gates and causing near-complete depolarization before inference. These results identify a regime-dependent tradeoff between measurement-based and coherent quantum learning, with shadow methods excelling when the target remains locally accessible and physics-informed QCNNs providing more robust performance across dynamical regimes.

</details>

**Authors:** Danh-Phuong Nguyen, Christophe Mora, Cristiano Ciuti

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes a one-dimensional electronic chain coupled to an optical cavity field and subject to electron interactions. It establishes that the light-matter coupling fundamentally alters the low-energy physics, leading to a 'strange Luttinger liquid' description. The authors map out phase diagrams showing how this cavity interaction modifies known quantum phases, including those hosting Majorana-like zero modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it merges concepts from quantum optics (cavity QED) with strongly correlated electron physics (Luttinger liquids), providing a mechanism to engineer unconventional quantum states in 1D systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the low-energy physics of a one-dimensional electronic chain coupled to a quantized vacuum field, aiming to characterize deviations from standard Luttinger liquid theory.

**Main result.** The coupling to the cavity field creates a 'strange Luttinger liquid' in the non-interacting limit, breaking conventional velocity relations; furthermore, electron-electron interactions lead to a phase diagram featuring Majorana-like zero modes whose boundaries are shifted by the cavity field.

**Method.** The analysis employs bosonization techniques for low-energy effective descriptions and utilizes exact diagonalization (ED) for finite interaction strengths to map out quantum phase diagrams.

**Model / system.** The system is a tight-binding electronic chain embedded in an optical cavity, described by a Hamiltonian incorporating light-matter coupling via the Peierls substitution and electron-electron interactions ($U$).

**Key observables.** Low-energy excitation spectrum, local density of states (LDOS), charge susceptibilities ($\chi$), and dressed bosonic velocities ($	ilde{v}_S, 	ilde{v}_N, 	ilde{v}_J$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), light-matter coupling strength ($g$), cavity frequency ($\omega_c$), and filling level (half-filling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving low-energy effective Hamiltonians via perturbation theory in the coupling $g$ and assuming a large but finite chain length.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate electronic phase diagrams showing transitions between Luttinger liquid, Charge Density Wave (CDW), and Phase Separation (PS) phases as functions of interaction strength and cavity coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing the non-interacting case ($U=0$) to establish the 'strange' nature of the LL. It then extends this analysis using ED for finite $U$, mapping out phase diagrams, and finally investigating how interactions modify the low-energy description derived from cavity coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a one-dimensional electronic chain coupled to a homogeneous quantized vacuum field and electron-electron interactions. In the absence of the latter, we derive a low-energy effective description in the presence of light-matter coupling, which we identify as a strange Luttinger liquid. Although it retains a formal resemblance to conventional Luttinger liquid theory, the coupling to the quantum field qualitatively modifies the low-energy sector and breaks the standard velocity relation underlying Luttinger universality. For finite electron-electron interactions, we recover a phase diagram featuring several phases as a function of interaction strength and hopping amplitude, including a phase hosting Majorana-like zero modes. Using exact diagonalization, we compute observables that characterize the phase boundaries and show that the cavity field significantly shifts them. We also study the fate of Majorana-like states under the influence of the cavity field, highlighting their modification by light-matter coupling. Finally, we investigate whether the strange Luttinger liquid description identified in the noninteracting regime continues to hold when electron-electron interactions are introduced.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a comprehensive diagnostic toolkit to study how periodically driven quantum systems approach thermal equilibrium. By comparing local subsystem equilibration (via relative entropy) with global energy absorption statistics (via work theorems), the authors show that these two distinct measures reveal the same underlying dynamical crossover, providing a unified picture of non-equilibrium physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, quantitative methodology for analyzing non-equilibrium steady states in driven quantum matter, directly relevant to understanding energy flow and thermalization processes beyond standard equilibrium assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize Floquet thermalization in periodically driven quantum systems by combining two complementary diagnostics: local subsystem equilibration and global work statistics.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that both local (subsystem) and global (work statistics) indicators track the same dynamical crossover—from prethermal plateaus towards eventual heating—establishing a unified framework for characterizing non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Method.** The analysis employs theoretical tools including calculating relative entropy for subsystem thermalization and analyzing work distributions via characteristic functions and fluctuation theorems for global energy absorption.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an N-site open Ising spin chain subjected to periodic driving (Floquet dynamics). The Hamiltonian involves interaction ($H_J$) and a time-dependent driving term ($H_x$).

**Key observables.** Subsystem relative entropy $S(ho_A || ho_A^eta)$, Work Distribution Function $P_{	ext{TPM}}^n(W)$, Fluctuation Theorem deviation $	ext{CFT}_n$, and the effective inverse temperature $eta_n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\omega$), subsystem size ($A$), and the choice of reference Hamiltonian ($H_J, H_{	ext{eff}}, H_{	ext{ave}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison relies on identifying a consistent ansatz thermal state $ho_{eta n}$ using one of the derived effective Hamiltonians, which is shown to be most robustly provided by $H_{ave}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the stroboscopic evolution of coherence quantities and relative entropy across different driving frequencies ($\omega=3.0, 10.0, 20.0$), comparing results for initial thermal vs. pure states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the physical model and diagnostics (subsystem/work statistics), compares their behavior across varying driving frequencies to map out dynamical regimes (prethermalization, heating), and concludes by establishing a unified theoretical framework connecting local and global equilibration measures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Floquet thermalization in a periodically driven quantum non-integrable Ising chain by combining two operational diagnostics: subsystem thermalization and work statistics. For generic interacting Floquet systems, stroboscopic dynamics lead to heating toward an infinite-temperature state at low driving frequencies, to a prethermal state during the crossover regime, and then to a finite-temperature state at high driving frequencies. We show that reduced density matrices of small subsystems provide a precise measure of local equilibration and clearly resolve prethermal plateaus. In parallel, we analyze the statistics of work performed over a Floquet cycle using both the characteristic function of work with and without the two-point measurements, and the related fluctuation theorem, which captures coherent contributions and deviations from thermal equilibrium. By comparing these two diagnostics within the same Floquet setting, we demonstrate that work statistics encode the same dynamical crossover that governs subsystem thermalization. Our results establish a unified and experimentally accessible framework for characterizing Floquet thermalization, prethermal regimes, and coherent energy absorption in interacting quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors experimentally investigate how surface charge accumulation on an optical nanofiber degrades the excitation of Rydberg atoms in a hybrid quantum system. They demonstrate that spectral splitting is caused by DC electric fields from these charges, which are generated via enhanced collisional ionization. The results provide crucial guidelines for designing and operating robust fiber-integrated quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it addresses a critical practical limitation—surface noise—in scaling up integrated quantum technologies utilizing Rydberg atoms near solid-state waveguides. It provides quantitative methods to characterize and mitigate environmental decoherence sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how uncontrolled surface electric fields, arising from charge accumulation on dielectric nanostructures, degrade Rydberg excitation processes in hybrid quantum systems.

**Main result.** Spectral deformations and splitting observed in the Rydberg excitation spectrum are strongly attributed to DC energy level shifts caused by accumulated surface charges. Applying an external oscillating electric field can suppress these spectral features.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of time-dependent Rydberg spectra with theoretical modeling using master equations incorporating static electric field shifts.

**Model / system.** The system involves laser-cooled $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms excited to high-lying Rydberg states near an optical nanofiber (ONF). The model incorporates DC energy level shifts ($\delta_{DC}$) derived from surface charges, which are generated primarily through enhanced Rydberg-ground state collisional ionization.

**Key observables.** Time evolution of the Rydberg excitation spectrum; spectral splitting/deformation magnitude; remaining MOT population ($N_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg states (e.g., $35D_{5/2}, 40D_{5/2}$); electric field strengths ($\sim 1-2.5 	ext{ V/cm}$); collisional ionization rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{coll}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the observed spectral changes are due to static DC shifts from surface charges, which can be mitigated by external AC fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups, time evolution of spectra showing deformation (Figs. 2, 3), comparison with model predictions based on field strength (Fig. 5), and the dependence of spectral features on laser polarization alignment (Figs. 11, 12).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform and observing time-dependent spectral changes to modeling these shifts using DC electric fields derived from charge generation mechanisms like collisional ionization, culminating in guidelines for mitigation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid quantum platforms based on highly excited Rydberg atoms coupled to nanophotonics devices offer a promising route toward scalable quantum networks and integrated quantum technologies. However, the close proximity of Rydberg atoms to dielectric nanostructures makes these systems particularly susceptible to uncontrolled surface electric fields that can lead to a degradation of the excitation process. Here, we experimentally investigate Rydberg excitation of laser-cooled $^{87}$Rb atoms via the evanescent field of an optical nanofiber in the presence of fiber-guided red- and blue-detuned light fields as used to trap ground state atoms in fiber-based dipole traps. We observe a time evolution of the Rydberg excitation spectrum when both the dipole trapping fields are on and the additional spectral features that appear can be suppressed by applying an external oscillating electric field to the system, strongly indicating that surface charge accumulation is responsible for the observed spectral feature. The experimental results are reproduced qualitatively by a model that incorporates DC energy level shifts arising from electric fields generated by charges deposited on the nanofiber surface. We identify Rydberg-ground state collisional ionization, which is enhanced by the dipole trapping fields, as the dominant mechanism for charge generation. These results provide new insight into charge dynamics at dielectric nanophotonic interfaces and establish practical guidelines for mitigating surface charge-induced electric fields in fiber-integrated Rydberg quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes that any local active dynamics can be simulated using either a time-periodic Hamiltonian (Brownian pump) or a static Hamiltonian coupled to two baths at different temperatures (Brownian ratchet). This demonstrates that steady heat currents are sufficient resources to generate and stabilize complex, non-equilibrium collective behaviors in many-body spin systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical framework showing how non-equilibrium thermodynamics—specifically steady heat currents—can serve as the fundamental physical resource to power complex computation or collective behavior, bridging statistical mechanics with information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that two simple, physically realizable driving mechanisms—the Brownian pump (time-periodic) and the Brownian ratchet (static)—can universally simulate any local active dynamics found in spin systems.

**Main result.** The authors prove rigorous theorems showing that both pumps and ratchets can approximate arbitrary probabilistic cellular automata (PCA) dynamics, translating abstract rule-based updates into concrete thermodynamic driving protocols.

**Method.** The work involves constructing specific Hamiltonians for the pump and ratchet mechanisms and analyzing their time evolution in various parameter limits to show equivalence with PCA update rules.

**Model / system.** The models are based on many-body spin systems, specifically using probabilistic cellular automata (PCAs) as the target dynamics. The physical realization involves coupling spins to thermal baths at different temperatures or applying time-periodic driving fields.

**Key observables.** Magnetization ($\langle m angle$), steady heat current, apparent critical field ($h_c$), and simulation closeness ($\delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature difference ($\delta T = T_\ell - T_f$), coupling strengths ($J, K$), time-period $	au$, and bath temperatures ($T_\ell, T_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The constructions are robust up to noise that can be made arbitrarily weak by tuning energy scales; the analysis often relies on specific limits of parameters (e.g., $\Gamma_f \gg \Gamma_\ell$ or $1 \ll 	au \ll e^{eta J}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between time-dependent pumping, static ratcheting, and cellular automaton dynamics; phase diagrams show ferromagnetic stability regions dependent on temperature differences and external fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from analyzing simple biased diffusion to constructing the full many-body ratchet/pump Hamiltonian. It then proves theorems for simulating continuous-time (ratchet) and discrete-time (pump) PCAs, culminating in tests of memory retention using symmetry-breaking fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active systems can exhibit a broad range of phenomena forbidden in equilibrium. Their dynamics are often specified by abstract local update rules, and it is generally unclear when the same behavior can arise from physically natural driving. Here we show that two simple driving mechanisms can universally simulate any local active dynamics in spin systems. The first is the familiar setting of a time-periodic Hamiltonian coupled to a cold bath, which we call a "many-body Brownian pump." As a second mechanism, we promote the Brownian ratchet, traditionally a mechanism for transport, to a "many-body Brownian ratchet": a static Hamiltonian coupled to a hot bath and a cold bath, where the resulting steady heat current can be harnessed not only to drive transport but also to generate local active dynamics. Using probabilistic cellular automata as an explicit model, we prove that for any continuous-time (or discrete-time) local active dynamics, there is always a many-body Brownian ratchet (or pump) that approximates the dynamics, up to noise that can be made arbitrarily weak by tuning energy scales and other parameters. As a concrete demonstration, we construct a simple ferromagnetic Ising ratchet on a bilayer lattice. When the two layers are coupled to baths at different temperatures, this model serves as a robust classical memory even under a symmetry-breaking field, something impossible in equilibrium. More broadly, our work shows that ratchets can use steady heat currents to autonomously generate and stabilize novel collective behavior, realizing a new static setting for nonequilibrium many-body dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method combining Quantum Error Detection (QED) and Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC) to estimate expectation values from noisy quantum circuits. By optimizing which symmetries are measured, the technique systematically reduces estimation variance compared to using PEC or QED in isolation. This is demonstrated successfully when simulating dynamics for models like the Fermi-Hubbard model.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource estimation for simulating strongly correlated electron models (like the FH model) under realistic noise conditions, providing tailored error mitigation techniques crucial for near-term quantum simulation hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve quantum estimation accuracy and reduce variance when estimating expectation values of noisy quantum circuits, surpassing standard Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC) by integrating Quantum Error Detection (QED). This is achieved through optimizing the selection of symmetry measurements.

**Main result.** Combining QED with PEC provides more accurate and lower-variance estimates than either method alone, especially when symmetries are optimized. The approach successfully mitigates errors in simulating complex models like the Fermi-Hubbard model on a lattice.

**Method.** The authors combine PEC (using inverse channels $\Lambda^{-1}$) with QED (by post-selecting on error-free measurements) and treat the selection of symmetry measurement configurations as a classical optimization problem to suppress noise impact.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general noisy quantum circuits, specifically demonstrating utility for GHZ-state output distributions and simulating the time-dynamics of a generalized superfast encoded Fermi-Hubbard model on lattices.

**Key observables.** Total Square Error (TSE), expectation values $\langle O_{	ext{ideal}} angle$ vs. $\langle O_{	ext{noisy}} angle$, sampling cost ($C_{	ext{PEC+QED}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\lambda$), lattice size ($2 	imes 2$ up to larger systems), and the set of chosen symmetry generators $C$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimization framework assumes that carefully selecting symmetries can systematically suppress noise, although noisy measurements themselves introduce limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the combination of QED (detecting errors via symmetries) and PEC (handling undetectable noise), showing performance comparisons for GHZ states and lattice models.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the combined estimator, deriving cost functions based on error detection probabilities ($p_{	ext{no-det}}$), and then applies this optimization procedure to benchmark systems like GHZ state preparation and the Fermi-Hubbard model dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that combining quantum error detection (QED) with probabilistic error cancellation (PEC) gives more accurate and lower-variance estimates than PEC alone, provided that the symmetry measurements required for QED are carefully chosen. Because noisy symmetry measurements can negate the benefits of the PEC+QED approach, we cast the selection of measurement configurations as a classical optimization problem that systematically suppresses the impact of noise. Applying optimized PEC+QED to GHZ-state output distributions and to simulating the time-dynamics of a generalized superfast encoded Fermi-Hubbard model, we find consistent improvements over PEC. For GHZ states, the optimization over symmetry measurement configurations is essential for achieving an advantage. For the Fermi-Hubbard model, PEC+QED improves observable estimation on a $2 \times 2$ lattice and for larger systems the mitigation overheads can be reduced by measuring only subsets of stabilizers. Our results demonstrate the importance of circuit-specific tailoring of QEM techniques and that fault-tolerant design principles may already provide value for near-term devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes asymmetric light scattering from a two-atom system incorporating optical gain. Using advanced quantum diagrammatic techniques, the authors demonstrate that the resulting optical response is fundamentally non-reciprocal—a feature absent in classical models. This highlights how quantum interference effects dictate directional properties when gain and loss are present.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes beyond standard semiclassical treatments in quantum optics by rigorously demonstrating fundamental non-reciprocal effects arising purely from quantum interference and gain/loss mechanisms in open systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the directionality and asymmetry of light scattering from a two-atom system containing gain, focusing on how this response deviates from classical predictions.

**Main result.** The fully quantum analysis reveals non-reciprocity in the optical response (forward power depending on the side of incidence), which contradicts classical linear response theory. This asymmetry is linked to interference effects involving phase differences and inter-atom emission.

**Method.** The authors employ a full quantum mechanical, diagrammatic perturbation theory approach using time-ordered Green's functions to calculate vacuum expectation values of quadratic field fluctuations.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two identical three-level atoms forming a dimer, where one atom is incoherently pumped (gain). The interaction is analyzed under weak probe fields ($\Omega_0$) and involves considering both single-atom and collective scattering processes.

**Key observables.** Differential scattered power $W(	heta)$, the ratio of forward/backward scattered powers ($W^G/W^L$), and the dependence on illumination geometry (frontal vs. side lighting).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump rate ($P$), interatomic distance ($k_R$), probe detuning ($\Delta = \omega - \omega_0$), and the relative phase factors associated with side incidence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions in the interaction Hamiltonian, integrating out fast dynamics of auxiliary states, and assuming specific limits for time evolution (e.g., $\gamma t \gg 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate angular distributions ($W(	heta)$) for both frontal and side illumination geometries, comparing quantum predictions against classical models using log-ratio plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical model and Hamiltonian to calculating scattering power via diagrammatic expansion. It systematically compares results for different illumination geometries (frontal vs. side) and contrasts the fully quantum findings with those derived from classical linear response theory, highlighting points of divergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the the scattering of light by a binary system of identical atoms in which one of them is incoherently pumped. This system belongs to the kind of non-parity symmetric optical systems in which gains and losses are partially compensated. We carry out a fully quantum analysis of the directionality of the radiation scattered from the atoms when the incident light strikes the system either perpendicular or alongside the interatomic axis. We find that, generally, while the degree of asymmetry depends on the pump rate, the preferred direction for emission depends on the interatomic distance and the detuning of the probe field with respect to the resonant frequency. On physical grounds, for the case of frontal illumination, the asymmetry is the result of the interference of the photons emitted from different atoms. On the contrary, for side lighting, the asymmetry with respect to the side of incidence is caused by both the phase difference between the probe field photons that strike each atom and the interference between the photons emitted from different atoms. Further, for side lighting too, our quantum approach demonstrates that the forward scattered power depends on the side of incidence, which reveals the lack of reciprocity in the quantum optical response of the system. This result conflicts with what is obtained within a classical approach.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a first-principles quantum framework unifying how far-field spatial coherence is determined by both incident quantum light scattering and intrinsic thermal emission from dissipative objects. By using the Modified Langevin Noise Formalism, it shows that the outgoing coherence results from an algebraic sum of filtering (scattering) and projection (thermal emission) mechanisms. This has profound implications for designing lossy quantum optical components.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a unified quantum electrodynamic treatment of light propagation through realistic, lossy media, connecting fundamental principles like unitarity directly to macroscopic phenomena like the van Cittert-Zernike theorem and thermal cloaking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a first-principles framework that unifies the scattering of quantum light and intrinsic thermal emission from finite, dissipative objects to describe far-field spatial coherence.

**Main result.** The outgoing far-field spatial coherence separates into an algebraic superposition of two geometry-driven mechanisms: elastic scattering (a non-unitary filter) and thermal emission (deriving van Cittert-Zernike theorem).

**Method.** The analysis employs the Modified Langevin Noise Formalism (MLNF) to calculate outgoing correlation dyadics, analyzing asymptotic limits for far-field correlations.

**Model / system.** The system involves an arbitrary dispersive and absorbing magnetodielectric object interacting with incident quantum light fields. The theoretical foundation is built upon coupled radiation-matter operators within the MLNF framework.

**Key observables.** Far-field spatial coherence ($\mu^2_{	ext{out}}$), outgoing correlation dyadic ($	ext{C}_{	ext{out}}$), and thermal shadow structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T_{em}$), scattering cross-sections ($\sigma_{	ext{sca}}, \sigma_{	ext{abs}}$), and the dimensionless parameter $\Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes rigorous description of material dissipation (Kirchhoff's law) and relies on statistical independence between incident radiation and object excitations in some derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate coherence generation across four thermodynamic limits: transparent, global equilibrium, cold vacuum, and cold scatterer, demonstrating the structured nature of the outgoing correlation dyadic.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the general input-output relationship using MLNF, then decomposing the resulting far-field correlation into scattering and thermal components, and finally analyzing specific physical limits (thermal/coherent illumination) to derive quantitative coherence bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Far-field spatial coherence dictates the interference properties of scattered light and thermal emission. Traditionally, these phenomena are treated through disjointed paradigms: classical scattering descriptions assume cold objects lacking quantum fluctuations, idealized quantum scattering schemes ignore dissipation, and semiclassical fluctuational electrodynamics relies on phenomenological noise currents, precluding the consistent treatment of incident quantum states. Here, we develop a first-principles framework based on the modified Langevin noise formalism to unify the scattering of quantum light and the intrinsic thermal emission of finite dissipative objects. We demonstrate that the outgoing far-field spatial coherence separates into an algebraic superposition of two geometry-driven mechanisms, coupled by the global unitarity of the radiation-matter dynamics. The first mechanism, elastic scattering, acts as a non-unitary spatial filter, mode-selectively attenuating and reshaping incident quantum correlations. The second mechanism, thermal emission, originates from localized material dissipation and projects the object's absorption profile into the far field, providing a quantum-vectorial derivation of the macroscopic van Cittert-Zernike theorem. Applying this framework across optical regimes, we determine operational bounds for lossy quantum photonics. Under chaotic thermal illumination, we analytically demonstrate thermal cloaking at equilibrium and show that a passive sink casts a structured thermal shadow geometrically identical to a primary emitter. Under coherent illumination, we derive a thermodynamic phase diagram bounding macroscopic phase correlations, demonstrating that subwavelength nanostructures undergo substantial coherence degradation compared to bulk objects. Finally, under spatially entangled illumination...

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an intramolecular spinterface mechanism to explain the chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect in isolated donor-chiral-bridge-acceptor complexes. Using a Lindblad master equation, the authors show that photoexcited current generates an effective magnetic field at the donor interface. This internal field breaks symmetry and allows for robust, measurable spin polarization signatures compatible with experimental EPR data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a quantum mechanical framework for understanding spin selectivity in molecular electronics using open quantum system techniques (Lindblad dynamics) to model non-equilibrium charge transport phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To explain the chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect observed in donor-chiral-bridge-acceptor complexes without relying on an external metal interface.

**Main result.** The authors propose and model an intramolecular spinterface-like mechanism where a current-induced solenoidal field breaks symmetry, enabling robust CISS-like spin filtering in isolated molecular systems.

**Method.** The study employs a two-electron Lindblad master equation approach to simulate the time evolution of the system's density matrix under photoexcitation and charge transfer.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a D–$\chi$B–A complex. The model incorporates Hamiltonian terms for donor, bridge (tight-binding chain), Zeeman splitting, and exchange interactions, all evolving via the Lindblad master equation to capture dissipation.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization ($P$), measured in time-resolved EPR studies, which is compared against theoretical predictions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Solenoidal coupling strength ($\alpha_0$), temperature ($T$), and spin-mixing rates ($\gamma_m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on the charge current generating an effective solenoidal field at the donor–bridge interface, breaking symmetry without intrinsic SOC on the bridge.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system schematic, the singlet-triplet state manifold, and show how polarization depends nonmonotonically on spin-mixing rates ($\gamma_m$) and saturates with increasing solenoidal coupling strength ($\alpha_0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of CISS in isolated complexes, develops a detailed Lindblad model incorporating current-induced fields (the spinterface), calculates steady-state polarization by solving the master equation, and analyzes the dependence on key physical parameters like temperature and coupling strength.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect has been invoked to explain recent reports of differences in the time-resolved EPR signals between chiral and achiral molecules. However, the microscopic origin of these differences and their connection to CISS remains contested, particularly since these systems lack a metal interface. Here we introduce an intramolecular spinterface-like mechanism that naturally arises within donor-chiral bridge-acceptor (D--$χ$B--A) complexes and quantitatively reproduces experimentally reported observed spin polarization in time-resolved EPR studies. In our two-electron Lindblad model, the photoexcited charge-transfer electron traversing the chiral bridge exchanges with the residual donor electron, which acts as a localized magnetic moment analogous to an induced magnetic moment on an electrode surface. The resulting through-bridge charge current produces an effective solenoidal field at the donor--bridge interface, breaking spin degeneracy and directional symmetry, thus enabling spin-selective transport without invoking intrinsic spin-orbit coupling on the bridge. We show that the interplay between this current-induced field, donor thermalization (which breaks time-reversal symmetry), and bridge spin mixing yields tens-of-percent polarization over realistic experimental conditions and charge-transfer time scales, matching reported CISS signatures in triads and DNA hairpins. By explicitly resolving the dependence on solenoidal coupling strength, temperature, and spin-mixing rates, the model identifies the regime in which internal spinterfaces can generate robust CISS-like spin filtering. These findings demonstrate that CISS-like signals in isolated D--$χ$B--A complexes are fully compatible with a spinterface mechanism, providing a unified conceptual framework for interpreting both device-based and molecule-internal CISS platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an advanced impurity solver for nonequilibrium Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) by combining weak-coupling expansion with Tensor Cross Interpolation (TCI). This technique efficiently calculates complex high-dimensional integrals, allowing accurate simulation of time evolution in strongly correlated electron systems like the Hubbard model. It is particularly valuable because it remains numerically controlled away from half-filling where other methods fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful numerical tool that extends DMFT calculations into non-equilibrium regimes where standard Monte Carlo methods fail due to the sign problem, offering insights into real-time quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating nonequilibrium quantum many-body systems where computational complexity grows exponentially with real time.

**Main result.** The developed solver accurately reproduces known quench dynamics, showing that the dynamical transition at half-filling evolves into a smooth crossover when doping occurs.

**Method.** It implements an impurity solver for Nonequilibrium Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) using a weak-coupling expansion combined with the Tensor Cross Interpolation (TCI) algorithm to handle high-dimensional integrals efficiently.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to lattice models, specifically the Hubbard model, analyzed via its mapping onto an impurity problem solved on the Keldysh contour in the complex time plane. The Hamiltonian involves interaction terms U(t).

**Key observables.** Two-time Green's functions (lesser/greater), spectral functions ($	ext{Im} G^R_\sigma(\omega)$), and quench dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength U, filling fraction (e.g., half-filled vs 3/4-filled), maximum real time $t_{	ext{max}}$, and expansion order $n_{	ext{max}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the weak-coupling expansion and assumes that the integrands possess a low-rank structure amenable to tensor decomposition via TCI.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate convergence checks showing error reduction with increasing expansion order ($n_{	ext{max}}$) or bond dimension ($\chi$), and compare TCI results against exact solutions for Green's functions in both time and frequency domains.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the mathematical framework by defining the Kadanoff-Baym contour, detailing iterative procedures for transient vs. steady states, and finally presenting the weak-coupling expansion solved via TCI to evaluate high-dimensional integrals.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating nonequilibrium quantum many-body systems remains a major challenge due to the exponential growth of the computational complexity with real time. Here we implement a nonequilibrium impurity solver based on the weak-coupling expansion and the tensor cross interpolation (TCI), and apply it to nonequilibrium dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). The method approximates the integrands of the high-dimensional integrals arising in the weak-coupling expansion in a tensor-train form, enabling efficient evaluations without stochastic sampling and thereby mitigating the sign problem affecting continuous-time quantum Monte Carlo (CT-QMC) methods. Benchmark calculations for an exactly solvable nonequilibrium impurity model agree well with the exact results and reveal a low-rank structure of the integrands. When applied to interaction-quench problems in the half-filled Hubbard model, the method reproduces fast thermalization at a critical interaction strength with accuracy comparable to CT-QMC. Away from half filling, where the sign problem becomes even more severe, the present approach remains well controlled, revealing a crossover instead of a sharply defined fast thermalization point in the 3/4-filled case. The solver can also be applied to steady-state DMFT problems, yielding accurate spectral functions in the metallic regime without analytic continuation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the successful realization and characterization of optically active single hole spins in ZnSe quantum wells by utilizing acceptor impurities instead of traditional p-doping. By combining DFT calculations, time-resolved spectroscopy, and photon correlation measurements, the authors confirm nitrogen as a promising host impurity for developing solid-state spin qubits and single-photon sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses solid-state quantum emitters for qubits. The combination of theoretical defect engineering (DFT) with precise optical characterization of spin states makes it highly relevant to quantum information research in semiconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to create a promising platform for optically active spin qubits and single-photon sources in ZnSe by overcoming historical p-doping limitations.

**Main result.** The authors successfully isolated and characterized a single-hole spin bound to an acceptor impurity, identifying nitrogen as the most likely candidate based on spectroscopic and theoretical evidence.

**Method.** A combination of first-principles DFT calculations (to predict defect levels) and advanced optical spectroscopy techniques (PL, time-resolved measurements, magnetospectroscopy) was employed.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-hole spin localized at an acceptor impurity within a ZnSe quantum well. The physics involves charge state transitions ($|A^-angle \leftrightarrow |A^0angle$) and the formation of an exciton complex ($|A^0Xangle$).

**Key observables.** Antibunching ($g^{(2)}(0)$), effective hole g-factor (0.7), optical resonance linewidth ($\approx 26.7 	ext{ GHz}$), and localization energy ($\sim 34 	ext{ meV}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fast radiative recombination lifetime ($\sim 244 	ext{ ps}$); the dependence of emission intensity on above-band vs. resonant pump power.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed acceptor state originates from residual nitrogen background rather than intentionally introduced dopants, and that the system can be modeled using steady-state rate equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate formation energy comparisons for various impurities; PL spectra show distinct exciton components; time decay fits confirm fast emission lifetimes; and $g^{(2)}(	au)$ measurements demonstrate antibunching.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical challenge (p-doping limitations) to proposing an optical activation method. It then uses DFT calculations to identify the impurity, followed by detailed spectroscopic characterization of the bound exciton and spin properties using time-resolved and magnetic measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconductor hole spins offer a pathway to extended coherence times by decoupling from nuclear magnetic noise, while their spin-orbit coupling enables fast all-electrical control. In ZnSe, however, realizing this potential has been limited by p-doping challenges. Here, we circumvent this limit by optically activating acceptors within the ZnSe quantum well. We isolate a single-hole spin bound to a shallow acceptor, confirmed by antibunching and accessed via the fast (244 ps) radiative recombination of a bound exciton. Magnetic and Raman spectroscopy of the ground state reveal an effective hole g-factor of 0.7 and an optical resonance linewidth of 26.7 GHz. Complementary first-principles simulations, together with the experimental results, provide evidence that points toward nitrogen as the most likely acceptor impurity. These results introduce a promising new platform for optically active spin qubits and single-photon sources in ZnSe.

</details>
