**Summary.** This paper resolves the Wigner's Friend paradox by reformulating quantum mechanics on a hybrid space combining continuous real space and discrete p-adic space. It argues that wavefunction collapse is not an external postulate but a dynamical outcome of non-local Hamiltonians acting within this structure. This approach successfully explains definite measurement outcomes for all observers without invoking consciousness or observer intervention.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a mathematically rigorous, non-observer-dependent resolution to a foundational problem in quantum mechanics by embedding it within advanced number theory ($\mathbb{Q}_p$).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve the Wigner's Friend paradox by developing a quantum mechanical framework that incorporates discrete microscopic space.

**Main result.** Wavefunction collapse is shown to be a dynamical consequence of the Schrödinger equation on a hybrid $\mathbb{R} 	imes \mathbb{Q}_p$ space, eliminating observer-dependent assumptions and resolving the conflict between different observers' descriptions of reality.

**Method.** The authors model quantum mechanics on $L^2(\mathbb{R} 	imes \mathbb{Q}_p)$, using non-local Hamiltonians derived from p-adic analysis to explain wavefunction localization during measurement interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is described by QM on the hybrid space $\mathbb{R} 	imes \mathbb{Q}_p$, where $\mathbb{Q}_p$ models discrete Planck-scale space. The dynamics are governed by a Schrödinger equation whose non-locality forces wavefunction collapse onto compact supports during measurement.

**Key observables.** Wavefunction localization onto compact supports, definite pointer readings for both Wigner and his Friend.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The p-adic number field $\mathbb{Q}_p$, the Planck–Bronstein scale (setting the discrete nature of space).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that QM holds on $L^2(\mathbb{R} 	imes \mathbb{Q}_p)$, and observers are treated as classical apparatuses, not rational agents.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the framework by defining dynamics on $\mathbb{R} 	imes \mathbb{Q}_p$, applying this to toy models (particle in a box), and then using the resulting collapse mechanism to resolve the paradox for both temporal orderings of Wigner's Friend scenario.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a resolution of the Wigner's Friend paradox within a framework of quantum mechanics (QM) on the hybrid space RxQ_{p}, where Q_{p} denotes the field of p-adic numbers, regarded as a model of discrete microscopic space at the Planck-Bronstein scale. In this framework, wavefunction collapse is not an independent postulate but a dynamical consequence of the Schrödinger equation with non-local Hamiltonians: wavefunctions localize onto compact supports during measurement interactions, producing definite pointer readings without the intervention of observers or the exchange of information between subsystems. We model both Wigner and his Friend as classical apparatuses and show that each produces a definite reading through independent applications of the collapse mechanism, thereby eliminating the conflict between their descriptions of reality. The framework is consistent with the principal no-go theorems in finite- and infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces associated with extended Wigner's Friend scenarios -- including those of Frauchiger-Renner, Brukner, Bong et al., and Guérin et al. -- since it requires no agents capable of recording or reasoning about outcomes, thereby vacating the observer-dependent assumptions that drive those theorems. We illustrate the collapse mechanism explicitly through a toy model of a particle in a box, comparing the standard description with the new one. The non-locality intrinsic to QM on L2(RxQ_{p}) permits realism at the cost of locality, and the Absoluteness of Observed Events holds in our framework without requiring observer independence.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors analyze the multi-layer extended Hubbard model to find exact eigenstates corresponding to interlayer triplet superconductivity. They show that these states are mathematically stable and physically realized only in bilayer and trilayer systems due to specific algebraic constraints (RSGA). This work advances understanding of pairing mechanisms by demonstrating how triplet pairs can dominate over singlet pairs when interlayer interactions are present.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an analytical understanding of exotic superconducting phases (triplet pairing) in layered correlated materials by mapping out precise conditions ($\lambda=2, 3$) under which exact solutions exist, linking symmetry constraints directly to physical realizability.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates the existence and properties of exact interlayer triplet-pairing eigenstates within the multi-layer extended Hubbard model, generalizing prior studies on $\eta$-pairing states.

**Main result.** Exact triplet-pair eigenstates are found to exist only in bilayer ($\lambda=2$) and trilayer ($\lambda=3$) systems when interlayer interactions ($V$) are present. These states demonstrate off-diagonal long-range order (ODLRO) but cannot coexist with singlet pairs if $V 
eq 0$.

**Method.** The analysis relies on constructing specialized triplet-pairing operators ($\zeta^\pm_\sigma$) and applying symmetry-generating algebra (SGA) and restricted spectrum-generating algebra (RSGA) to find exact solutions.

**Model / system.** The system is the multi-layer extended Hubbard model defined by a Hamiltonian including intralayer hopping ($t$), interlayer hopping ($t_\perp$), on-site interaction ($U$), and interlayer interaction ($V$).

**Key observables.** Off-diagonal long-range order (ODLRO), stability of eigenstates measured via fidelity $F(t)$, and correlation functions for singlet ($C_S$) and triplet ($C_T$) pairs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of layers ($\lambda$), the interaction strengths ($U, V$), and the hopping parameters ($t, t_\perp$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of exact eigenstates is restricted by the requirement that the Hamiltonian must satisfy the conditions for a Restricted Spectrum-Generating Algebra (RSGA), limiting applicability to $\lambda=2$ and $\lambda=3$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the multi-layered lattice structure and hopping terms; Figure 2 plots the fidelity $F(t)$ to demonstrate state stability under time evolution for different layer numbers ($\lambda$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the model and pairing operators, derives conditions based on symmetry algebra (RSGA), establishes the existence of exact eigenstates for $\lambda=2, 3$, and uses quench dynamics simulations to confirm their stability against interlayer interactions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

$η$-pairing symmetry generalizes the pairing mechanisms in superconductivity but is broken in the presence of interlayer interactions. In this work, we extend this approach to triplet pairs. We propose interlayer triplet-pairing operators for the multi-layer extended Hubbard model. We find that a set of exact condensate-pair eigenstates can be constructed, which exhibit off-diagonal long-range order. In contrast to the $η$-pairing mechanism, this originates from restricted spectrum generating algebra and is only available for bilayer and trilayer systems in the presence of interlayer Hubbard interactions. Nevertheless, the system also retains the original on-site $η$-pairing symmetry in the absence of interlayer interactions. Consequently, both singlet and triplet pairs coexist in the eigenstates of the multi-layer Hubbard model. We employ quench dynamics to demonstrate the results through numerical simulations. Our findings open avenues for the study of exact condensate-pair states in strongly correlated systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a cyclic control method using parameter-space expansion to enable fast, high-fidelity controlled-Z gates on superconducting qubits. By adding an extra degree of freedom, the technique overcomes the speed-fidelity trade-off caused by short-timescale waveform distortions. This results in significantly lower coherent errors even when operating at very fast gate speeds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally validated engineering solution for high-speed quantum gate operations that circumvents fundamental limitations imposed by realistic hardware noise models (STDs), which is crucial for scaling up quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the inherent speed-fidelity trade-off when implementing fast quantum gates, specifically CZ gates, due to short-timescale waveform distortions (STDs).

**Main result.** A novel cyclic control strategy based on parameter-space expansion successfully suppresses distortion-induced coherent errors while maintaining gate speed, fundamentally breaking the conventional speed-fidelity limitation.

**Method.** The authors employ a Parameter-Space Expansion (PSE) technique by introducing an auxiliary degree of freedom to restore controllability and satisfy necessary symmetry constraints for fast cyclic evolution.

**Model / system.** The work is performed on superconducting qubits, specifically using tunable transmon qubits coupled via tunable couplers in a 72-qubit processor architecture. The dynamics are analyzed by reducing the system's evolution to an effective two-level subspace $\{|11angle, |20angle\}$ for the CZ gate.

**Key observables.** Coherent error rate (measured via cross-entropy benchmarking), leakage into $|20angle$ state, and conditional phase accumulation ($	ext{CPhase}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate duration ($	au$), distortion amplitude ($\alpha$), and the auxiliary control parameters $(\Delta_1, \Delta_2)$ used in the PSE protocol.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that leakage and CPhase errors can be independently diagnosed and suppressed by optimizing pulse shapes within the expanded parameter space.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare simulated error rates vs. filter cutoff frequency (showing robustness), demonstrate gate error rates vs. duration ($	au$) showing the trade-off mitigation, and show XEB results quantifying fidelity improvement across varying fast gate times.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from theoretical analysis of symmetry breaking due to STDs to proposing PSE, followed by detailed experimental diagnosis using leakage measurements and CPhase characterization, culminating in quantitative benchmarking via XEB on the superconducting hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-fidelity quantum gates are essential for scalable quantum computation. However, at short durations, short-timescale waveform distortions break the time-reflection symmetry of control pulses, preventing the precise closure of cyclic evolution. This mechanism renders conventional symmetric protocols intrinsically over-constrained. Conventional strategies typically rely on smoothing the pulse envelopes or embedding the interaction pulse within a longer qubit pulse to bypass short-timescale distortions, which inevitably leads to a persistent speed-fidelity trade-off. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a cyclic control strategy based on parameter-space expansion, which restores controllability by incorporating an additional degree of freedom. We experimentally demonstrate this approach in a superconducting controlled-Z gate, achieving robust suppression of coherent errors without increasing gate duration, reducing the average coherent error from 0.27% to 0.12% across multiple two-qubit gates, as validated by cross-entropy benchmarking. Our results establish a general route to fast, high-fidelity cyclic quantum gates beyond the conventional speed-fidelity trade-off.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced analytical model to accurately characterize superconducting resonators used for qubit readout. By integrating four-port network analysis and conformal mapping, it provides closed-form expressions for resonance frequency and coupling Q-factor. The model is validated against both high-fidelity FEM simulations and physical measurements, offering a crucial tool for designing scalable quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the hardware characterization bottleneck in superconducting quantum computing by providing a computationally fast alternative to intensive full-wave electromagnetic simulations for critical readout components.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a fast, accurate analytical model to determine the resonance frequencies ($f_r$) and coupling Q-factors ($Q_c$) of feedline-coupled resonators used in superconducting qubit readout circuits.

**Main result.** The proposed analytical model successfully predicts $f_r$ and $Q_c$ with good agreement ($\Delta f/f_{sim} < 1\%$, $\Delta Q_c/Q_{c,sim} < 20\%$) when compared against both full-wave FEM simulations and direct cryogenic measurements.

**Method.** The methodology involves using four-port microwave network analysis combined with conformal mapping techniques to derive closed-form expressions for the transmission coefficient ($S_{21}$).

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting $\lambda/4$ resonator coupled to a CPW feedline, often implemented in complex 3D heterogeneous architectures. The model treats the coupling section as a four-port network reduced to two ports.

**Key observables.** Resonance frequency ($f_r$), coupling quality factor ($Q_c$), and the transmission spectrum $|S_{21}(f)|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Geometric parameters such as conductor thickness ($t$), gap spacing ($h_s$), and resonator lengths ($l_s, l_o$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating the superconductor thickness as zero in some derivations, simplifying transitions to right-angles, and using an impedance matrix approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate physical layouts of 3D flip-chip resonators, E-field distributions for even/odd modes, and conformal mapping steps used for capacitance calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the need for a fast model to detailing the mathematical framework (four-port analysis, impedance matrix derivation via conformal mapping) before validating the final closed-form $S_{21}$ expression against both simulation results and experimental measurements on fabricated chips.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting quantum chips commonly utilize quarter-wavelength (λ/4) transmission line resonators as readout circuits. An analytical model for the accurate determination of resonance frequencies and coupling Q-factors of feedline-coupled superconducting resonators is introduced. The model leverages four-port microwave network analysis, integrating boundary conditions and conformal mapping techniques to compute even- and odd-mode impedances in edge-coupled coplanar waveguide (CPW) structures. Its versatility allows application to both planar and 3-D heterogeneous architectures, making it a powerful tool for resonator design. To validate the model, a test chip with λ/4 resonators of varying geometries is fabricated and measured in a cryogenic environment. Comparisons with finite element method (FEM) simulations and experimental measurements confirm the model's accuracy, with resonance frequencies and coupling Q-factors aligning closely across configurations. This proposed model facilitates the design of superconducting resonators in readout circuits for more effective, scalable, and adaptable quantum computing architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce Ensemble Feature Selection (EFS), a novel technique to estimate process infidelity for difficult non-Clifford quantum gates. By training on an ensemble of simulated noisy channels and using ridge regression, EFS constructs a robust linear estimator. Validation on superconducting hardware confirms that EFS provides accurate measurements comparable to established methods like IRB.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial tool for characterizing noise in complex quantum hardware beyond simple gate sets, which is vital for scaling up quantum computation by providing reliable benchmarking metrics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a reliable method to estimate process infidelity for multi-qubit gates, especially non-Clifford targets, where standard Clifford-based benchmarking techniques fail.

**Main result.** The proposed Ensemble Feature Selection (EFS) method shows strong agreement with independent Randomized Benchmarking measurements on superconducting hardware, achieving high estimation precision across a wide range of gate infidelities.

**Method.** EFS selects a compact set of executable circuit measurements from a candidate pool using offline training on a physically motivated ensemble of noisy channels and combines them into a linear estimator via ridge regression.

**Model / system.** The work is validated on superconducting hardware (ibm_kingston) using three-qubit non-Clifford gates like CCZ. The analysis relies on Pauli Transfer Matrices to define process fidelity and infidelity ($\epsilon$).

**Key observables.** Process infidelity ($\epsilon$), gate fidelity ($F(G, T)$), and comparison metrics against independent Interleaved Randomized Benchmarking (IRB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Estimation precision of $\approx 0.01$ over an infidelity range of $0.02-0.2$. The optimal feature set size was determined to be around $M=24$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a physically motivated training ensemble incorporating prior knowledge about dominant hardware noise mechanisms, and that the target gate satisfies an anchor condition.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare EFS estimates against true process infidelity from independent IRB measurements for Clifford benchmarks (ICCZ, CZ(0, 2)), demonstrating strong linear correlation. Further comparisons test transferability to non-Clifford gates like CCZ.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using Pauli Transfer Matrices and an anchor condition, details the EFS methodology involving ensemble training and ridge regression, validates the estimator against independent IRB measurements on Clifford targets, and finally applies it directly to estimate infidelity for non-Clifford gates like CCZ.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an Ensemble Feature Selection (EFS) method for fast estimation of process infidelity of involutory multi-qubit gates, including non-Clifford targets, for which standard Clifford-based benchmarking does not apply. The method selects a compact set of experimentally executable circuit measurements from a candidate pool through offline training on a physically motivated ensemble of noisy channels, and combines them into a linear estimator with weights learned by ridge regression. The training ensemble is an explicit and tunable component of the protocol, incorporating prior knowledge about dominant hardware noise mechanisms. The estimator is validated on ibm_kingston using two Clifford validation benchmarks structurally related to the transpiled CCZ circuit, against independent Interleaved Randomized Benchmarking (IRB). Both show close EFS-IRB agreement across a wide range of process infidelities, with an estimation precision of approximately 0.01 over a process infidelity range of 0.02-0.2. EFS is subsequently applied directly to CCZ on the same device.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive experimental characterization of superconducting spiral inductors designed for RF reflectometry in quantum computing. By using dual measurement techniques, the authors successfully separated inductive and capacitive effects to map out how device performance degrades with increasing temperature or magnetic field. The results provide crucial design rules for building robust readout circuitry for next-generation quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts the hardware layer of quantum computing by providing quantitative guidelines for designing superconducting readout circuits that must maintain performance stability under non-ideal operating conditions (T > 1K, B $
eq$ 0).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the need for reliable superconducting circuit components, specifically spiral inductors, to function correctly in future quantum computing architectures that operate at elevated temperatures and magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The research successfully characterized the temperature and magnetic field dependence of inductor performance, establishing design metrics that show frequency shifts are dominated by inductive effects, while loss is limited by quasiparticle dissipation or vortex entry.

**Method.** The authors employed two complementary microwave measurement setups—weakly coupled resonator measurements and independent two-port inductance extraction—to systematically deconvolve the device's electrical response.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting spiral inductor made of NbTiN film, which acts as a distributed resonator. The analysis models its behavior using lumped admittance fitting techniques to separate geometric ($L_g$) and temperature/field-dependent kinetic inductance ($L_k$).

**Key observables.** Resonant frequency ($f_0$), internal quality factor ($Q_i$), total inductance ($L$), and the field-dependent loss onset field ($B_Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperatures (several Kelvin), magnetic fields approaching 1 T, kinetic inductance fraction ($\alpha$), and superconducting gap temperature dependence $\Delta(T)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that combining two independent measurement techniques allows for the accurate separation of inductive and capacitive contributions to characterize the true device response.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between frequency shifts derived from two different measurement setups, show $Q_i$ degradation versus applied magnetic field (highlighting $B_Q$), and plot design trade-offs relating miniaturization ($\alpha$) to stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements using two distinct microwave characterization setups. It then analyzes the temperature and magnetic field dependence of resonance shifts, confirming inductive dominance, before concluding with a discussion on engineering trade-offs between device size and operational robustness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting spiral inductors are emerging as key components for radio-frequency (RF) reflectometry, a widely used readout technique for semiconductor spin qubits. Future scalable quantum-computing architectures are expected to operate at elevated temperatures and magnetic fields, placing new demands on the performance and stability of superconducting circuit elements. Here, we present a systematic study of NbTiN spiral inductors under temperatures of several kelvin and magnetic fields approaching 1 T. By combining weakly coupled resonator measurements with independent two-port inductance extraction, we separate inductive and capacitive contributions to device behaviour and directly identify the origin of resonance shifts and quality factor degradation. Furthermore, we establish practical design metrics linking geometry, temperature sensitivity, and magnetic-field robustness. These results provide a general framework for benchmarking superconducting inductors and guiding the design of future RF-reflectometry circuits for practical quantum technologies.

</details>

### [Demographic senescence as multi-level selection in miniature](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00262v1)

**Authors:** Ananda Shikhara Bhat, Hanna Kokko  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00262v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

(A) The model consists of M organisms, each of which have N intra-organismal sub-systems capable of failure. (B) An organism with f ∈{0, 1</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work establishes a deep formal equivalence between biological senescence and multi-level selection theory. By developing sophisticated mathematical tools, the authors show that age-related deterioration can be modeled as a stochastic process governed by competition at two interacting levels: sub-systems within an individual, and individuals competing against each other. This provides a powerful unifying framework for understanding aging dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the rigorous mathematical machinery developed—using SPDEs and functional central limit theorems to model complex interacting stochastic processes—is highly relevant for modeling open quantum systems or many-body dynamics where noise and collective behavior are key.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To formally demonstrate that demographic senescence (age-related mortality increase) can be mathematically understood as an emergent consequence of multi-level selection processes.

**Main result.** The progressive aging pattern is shown to be formally equivalent to the patterns generated by a within-generation, two-level stochastic process modeling competition between sub-systems and groups.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced stochastic calculus, deriving functional Master Equations and their subsequent diffusion approximations (Fokker-Planck equations) in multiple scaling limits.

**Model / system.** The model tracks the state of $M$ 'systems' (organisms), where each system contains $N$ interacting sub-systems. Dynamics involve within-system failure/repair ('warming/cooling') and between-system selection/extinction, modeled via a ball-and-urn scheme.

**Key observables.** The proportion of systems $\xi_{M,N}(f, t)$ possessing a certain fraction $f$ of failed sub-systems; the scaled mean mortality rate $b_\mu(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Replacement probability ($\varrho$), system size ($M$), sub-system count ($N$), and failure/repair rates ($r_+, r_-$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on diffusion approximations (Gaussian limits) for both the number of systems ($M$) and the continuous trait space ($N 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the model components: (A) $M$ organisms, each with $N$ sub-systems; (B) Dynamics involving failure accumulation, repair, and death; (C) Representation using a ball-and-urn scheme.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the stochastic process via Master Equations for finite $M$ and $N$, deriving functional Fokker-Planck equations through diffusion approximations, and finally analyzing specific scaling limits to recover known models of senescence and selection.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-level selection and senescence do not at first sight have much in common. Here, we demonstrate that the emergent mortality patterns generated by demographic senescence can be understood as the product of multi-level selection. We formulate a two-level Moran type process and use its scaling limits to illustrate that a simple mathematical framework that models multi-level selection in group-structured populations also models damage accumulation patterns and resultant mortality curves in ageing organisms. To verbally make the connection, observe that defectors spread within a group consisting of cooperators and defectors; when groups compete against each other, defector-rich groups suffer, and between-group selection causes such groups to be systematically under-represented. Exactly analogously, senescing individuals accumulate damage to physiological sub-systems, and `damage begets damage'; individuals who are more damaged are more likely to die, hence damage-rich individuals are systematically under-represented in later age classes. Thus, emergent senescence patterns in complex, integrated organisms are formally equivalent to the patterns generated by a within-generation multi-level selection process in which intra-organismal sub-systems play the role of particles, organisms play the role of collectives, and selective disappearance plays the role of group selection.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Susceptibility-Kinetic Uncertainty Relation (S-KUR) as a universal bound governing current precision in open quantum systems. By defining Partial Dynamical Activity using quantum Fisher information, it overcomes limitations of previous bounds that failed under strong coupling or coherence. This tool is demonstrated to be robustly applicable to complex transport phenomena like those in double quantum dots.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general theoretical tool for quantifying fundamental limits on measurement precision in non-equilibrium quantum transport, which is highly relevant to mesoscopic physics and quantum thermodynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is establishing universal uncertainty relations that constrain current precision for open quantum systems operating out of equilibrium.

**Main result.** The authors derive the Susceptibility-Kinetic Uncertainty Relation (S-KUR), a general bound on current precision derived from partial dynamical activity, which remains valid even when standard kinetic uncertainty relations fail due to quantum coherence or strong coupling.

**Method.** The relation is derived using the Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound applied to the response of an observable to changes in system-reservoir coupling strength ($	heta$), leading to the definition of Partial Dynamical Activity (PDA).

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on open quantum systems coupled to reservoirs, exemplified by transport through a Double Quantum Dot (DQD) structure. The dynamics are governed by time-dependent Hamiltonians and analyzed in the steady-state limit.

**Key observables.** Current ($\hat{J}(\hat{X})$), Susceptibility term ($\dot{M}(\hat{X})$), Zero-frequency noise ($S(\hat{X})$), Partial Dynamical Activity ($A_V(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($	heta$), Bias voltage ($\Delta\mu$), Temperature ($T$), and the time evolution parameter $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on defining PDA via quantum Fisher information related to coupling rescaling, and in some analyses, approximations like the secular approximation or Markovian limits are used.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the failure of classical local KURs compared to the saturation of the S-KUR for particle current in a DQD across varying bias; another figure shows the disappearance of classical bound violations upon increasing temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the problem using the QCRB, introducing PDA via quantum Fisher information, deriving the universal S-KUR, and then validating this relation by applying it to complex physical examples like steady-state transport in a DQD under various bias and temperature conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Kinetic uncertainty relations bound current precision of stochastic processes by dynamical activity. The extension of these bounds to quantum systems has been impeded by coherence, strong system-reservoir coupling, and the subtlety of defining dynamical activity in the quantum regime. Here, we introduce a partial dynamical activity through the quantum Fisher information associated with the rescaling of the system-reservoir coupling and show that it bounds current precision via a universal susceptibility-kinetic uncertainty relation. The general validity of this relation for any open quantum system is guaranteed by the natural contribution of a susceptibility term, which is experimentally accessible by tuning the system-reservoir coupling strength. We show how the partial dynamical activity encompasses previous definitions of activity in the weak-coupling Markovian limit and that it provides an information-geometric interpretation of correlator-based activities. We illustrate the tight constraint on precision that our bound provides with the example of steady-state transport through a double quantum dot, where quantum effects invalidate previously developed kinetic uncertainty relations. We expect our bound to provide a powerful tool for optimizing precision in arbitrary quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) arises from an inelastic resonance process mediated by a quantized excitation called a heliciton. By treating the static chiral potential as a dynamic interaction, the authors show that spin polarization is achieved when the electron undergoes resonant energy and momentum exchange with this environmental quantum. This establishes CISS as a robust, dynamical mechanism rather than just a geometric effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, dynamical mechanism for spin selectivity rooted in quantized environmental coupling, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems dynamics and spintronics theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a mechanism for Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) that incorporates energy exchange with the environment, moving beyond static chiral potentials.

**Main result.** The paper shows that CISS can be understood as a heliciton-assisted resonant inelastic scattering process in the sideband sector, where full spin polarization is achieved at specific resonance conditions.

**Method.** First Born scattering theory is used to analyze the coupling between an electron state and quantized environmental excitations (helicitons), leading to sidebands.

**Model / system.** The model involves a confined Dirac electron interacting with a quantized, screw-symmetric environmental motion called a 'heliciton.' The interaction couples spin channels while conserving helicity via momentum ($\pm q$) and energy ($\hbar \Omega_q$) exchange.

**Key observables.** Sideband polarization ($P_{sb}$), which reaches $\pm 1$ at resonance, and the resonant detuning conditions ($\Delta_{\pm}(k, q) = 0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Helicon frequency ($\Omega_q$), wave number ($q$), and the local geometric overlap kernel $J_\chi(k)$ characterizing the helical Dirac-current texture.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation relies on treating the coupling perturbatively using first Born scattering theory, assuming sideband amplitudes are small.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian incorporating the heliciton interaction, applies first Born scattering to analyze inelastic channels (absorption/emission), and derives the polarization conditions based on resonant energy/momentum matching.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a quantized chiral-field mechanism for chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS). The corresponding quantum is a heliciton: a helical mode with phase coordinate $φ-qz$, screw momentum $\hbar q$, and energy $\hbarΩ_q$. A helical electron can absorb or emit this quantum, converting the static chiral vertex developed in our preceding work into an inelastic resonant scattering process. Using first Born scattering theory, we show that an incident two-spin-channel state generates two heliciton-assisted sidebands. Absorption converts the $\uparrow k$ channel into the $\downarrow,k+q$ sideband, while emission converts the $\downarrow k$ channel into the $\uparrow,k-q$ sideband. Thus the heliciton supplies both the screw momentum and energy needed to turn the handedness-conversion into a resonant spin-selective channel.   The two sidebands inherit the same sampled-current overlap $J_χ(k)$ from the static theory, but acquire different kinematic weights and different resonance detunings. The sideband sector reaches full spin polarization at the respective isolated heliciton resonances, with $P_{\rm sb}(k,q)\simeq +1$ for $Δ_-(k,q)=0$ and $P_{\rm sb}(k,q)\simeq -1$ for $Δ_+(k,q)=0$. Reversing the screw handedness, $q\rightarrow -q$, interchanges the two sideband channels and reverses the polarization. No ad hoc spin-dependent potential is introduced. The spin selectivity comes from three ingredients: helical Dirac-current texture, quantized screw-symmetric environmental motion, and resonant exchange of screw momentum and energy. This identifies CISS as a heliciton-assisted resonance mechanism that produces spin polarization in the inelastic sideband sector.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** This paper systematically explores the role of quantum squeezing in enhancing dissipation-induced nonreciprocal effects within coupled cavity systems. By analyzing both energy storage (quantum batteries) and signal isolation (optical isolators), the authors demonstrate that squeezing can exponentially boost key performance metrics. The findings establish a powerful theoretical framework for designing advanced, nonreciprocal quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, quantitative methods for engineering nonreciprocity using fundamental quantum resources like squeezing, which is highly relevant for designing next-generation quantum optical components.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum squeezing can be utilized to enhance nonreciprocal coupling induced by dissipation in various quantum devices.

**Main result.** Squeezing leads to an exponential enhancement of the effective nonreciprocal coupling, significantly boosting performance metrics like stored energy and output signal strength in both quantum batteries and optical isolators.

**Method.** The analysis employs master equations and Quantum Langevin Equations (QLEs) to model the time evolution of system operators under nonlinear and dissipative couplings. Expectation values are calculated across multiple squeezing configurations.

**Model / system.** The core model involves two coupled cavity modes interacting with a common reservoir, potentially incorporating $\chi^{(2)}$ nonlinearity. Applications include quantum batteries (energy storage) and optical isolators (unidirectional signal transmission).

**Key observables.** Effective Coupling (EC), Enhancement Effect (EE), stored energy ($E_b$), ergotropy ($\mathcal{E}_b$), and the output transmission coefficient ($T_{ab}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), coupling strength ($J$), detuning ($\omega_s$), and cavity decay rates ($\kappa, \Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes applying a broad-band squeezed-vacuum field to manage noise, and often simplifies dynamics by setting certain frequency parameters (e.g., $\omega_s = 0$ in some battery contexts).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy evolution of quantum batteries under different squeezing regimes, show the enhancement factor of stored energy vs. squeezing parameter $r$, and plot the transmission coefficient spectrum for optical isolators.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates five cases of squeezing application in a coupled cavity system. It then applies this framework to analyze two key physical applications: quantum batteries (analyzing energy/ergotropy) and optical isolators (analyzing transmission coefficients).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We systematically investigate the role of squeezing in improving dissipation-induced nonreciprocal coupling mediated by a common reservoir, by incorporating a $χ^{(2)}$ nonlinearity into either the cavity mode, the reservoir, or both. Squeezing is capable of exponentially enhancing the effective coupling, but this enhancement is not universal in such a nonreciprocal interaction. Instead, it depends on the system configuration and specific parameter regimes. Specifically, the squeezing of the reservoir injects additional energy into the system via the dissipation channel, modifying the underlying dynamics in a nontrivial manner. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the proposed approach enhances the performance of the quantum battery, including stored energy, charging power, and ergotropy. We provide the corresponding analytical expressions, together with a systematic analysis of energy transport and parameter optimization. Extending the framework to optical isolation, we observe an exponentially amplified output signal. Our results open a new avenue for nonreciprocal quantum information processing and nonreciprocal quantum device design.

</details>

**Summary.** This research tackles the challenge of identifying robust topological phase transitions at non-zero temperatures without relying on symmetry breaking. By introducing the decoded Wilson-loop correlation $f_W$, the authors provide a new, quasi-local-channel invariant diagnostic that successfully maps out the full phase diagram of the 3D toric code. This work advances our understanding of how topological order persists and manifests in realistic, thermal quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides advanced techniques for characterizing topological order in thermal equilibrium states, which is crucial for understanding quantum materials where finite temperature effects are dominant. The focus on channel-invariant diagnostics is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is establishing robust topological invariants that can distinguish between different phases of matter at non-zero temperature ($T>0$) without relying on the spontaneous breaking of any exact symmetry.

**Main result.** The decoded Wilson-loop correlation $f_W$ was introduced as a quasi-local-channel invariant order parameter, which successfully distinguishes topological (value 1) from trivial (value 0) phases across finite temperatures and external fields.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical analysis combined with large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to probe the thermal Gibbs state ($ho_eta$) of the system.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the three-dimensional ($	ext{3D}$) $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code, analyzed in its finite-temperature Gibbs state under generic magnetic fields where higher-form symmetries are explicitly broken or emergent.

**Key observables.** Topological Entanglement Entropy ($\gamma$), Decoded Wilson-loop correlation ($f_W$), and various string/loop correlators (e.g., $\langle ar{W}_1 ar{W}_2 angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), external magnetic fields $(h_x, h_z)$, and system size ($L$). The critical temperature $T_c$ is often compared to the 3D Ising model transition point.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rigorous definition of phase equivalence for Gibbs states at $T>0$ remains an active area of research; some results rely on quasi-local channel invariance arguments.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare various diagnostics ($\gamma$, $ho_{FM}$, correlators) across T-scans, $h_x$-scans, and $h_z$-scans, showing how different observables are sensitive to different types of phase transitions (thermal vs. magnetic vs. electric).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first identifying the failure points of conventional diagnostics ($\gamma$ is not channel invariant), then introducing robust invariants like $f_W$, and finally applying these tools across the full $(h_x, h_z, T)$ phase diagram to demonstrate a universal diagnostic.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the finite-temperature topological order of the three-dimensional $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code in a generic magnetic field, where every higher-form symmetry is explicitly broken and can at most be emergent. We show perturbatively, and confirm by large-scale quantum Monte Carlo, that the topological entanglement entropy stays quantized at $γ= \ln 2$ throughout the topological phase -- at finite temperature and under the symmetry-breaking field alike -- and collapses to $0$ across the thermal transition, a quantization protected geometrically by the Bianchi identity rather than by any exact symmetry of the system. The plateau $γ= \ln 2$ is, however, not invariant under quasi-local channels: a constant-depth channel can generate this identical quantized value from a trivial product state. We therefore introduce the decoded Wilson-loop correlation $f_W$, which quantizes to $1$ in the topological phase and $0$ in the trivial phase as $L\to\infty$ and, unlike $γ$, is a quasi-local-channel invariant -- a robust topological invariant of the mixed state.

</details>

**Authors:** Fabricio Toscano, G. Cañas, A. Z. Khoury, P. H. Souto Ribeiro, S. P. Walborn

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the Twisted Gaussian Schell state, establishing it as a key system bridging classical optics and quantum information. It demonstrates that introducing a 'twist' parameter induces nonclassicality and entanglement even when the initial state is separable. The methodology uses advanced tools like Fock-basis probability calculations and logarithmic negativity to map out the resource landscape governed by this twist.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework connecting classical beam engineering concepts (like the Schell model) directly to measurable quantum resources (squeezing and entanglement) in continuous variable systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces and analyzes the Twisted Gaussian Schell (TGS) state, a quantum-optical analog of classical beams, to study its nonclassicality and entanglement properties.

**Main result.** The TGS state can be separable yet exhibit nonclassical features like sub-shot-noise squeezing and measurable entanglement when the twist parameter is non-zero. The twist parameter acts as a resource that activates or deepens quantum correlations.

**Method.** Analysis involves calculating covariance matrices, deriving closed-form expressions for joint photon-number distributions in the Fock basis, and quantifying entanglement using logarithmic negativity across various bipartite splittings.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-mode TGS state ($\hat{ho}_{	ext{TGS}}$), a mixed Gaussian state generated via metaplectic evolution from an initial thermal state. The analysis extends to four-mode systems by coupling modes to ancillary vacuum fields.

**Key observables.** Nonclassicality (quadrature squeezing below shot-noise limit), Entanglement (quantified by logarithmic negativity, $	ext{EN}$), Joint photon-number distribution $P(n_1, n_2)$, and Mutual Information ($I$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist parameter ($v$), local squeezing ($\Omega$), and noise/mixedness ($\kappa$); physical constraints include $\kappa + v \geq 1/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the formal analogy between classical paraxial optics and continuous-variable (CV) quantum systems, and uses Gaussian state criteria like the PPT criterion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the generation scheme from thermal modes, density plots of mutual information for photon number distributions, and phase diagrams showing entanglement boundaries in the $(\kappa, v)$ plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically defines the TGS state, analyzes its nonclassicality via quadrature variances, quantifies correlations using Fock-basis probabilities and mutual information, and finally investigates entanglement across various bipartite splittings of an extended four-mode system.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the Twisted Gaussian Schell (TGS) state, a two-mode mixed Gaussian state defined as the quantum-optical analog of the Twisted Gaussian Schell-model beam of classical paraxial optics, characterized by the so-called twist phase. In the TGS state, the twist parameter arises when an asymmetric two-mode thermal state is subject to local squeezing after the action of phase shifters and a beam splitter. Its defining quantum feature is nonclassicality: although the state is separable in its natural bipartition, when the twist parameter is nonzero there are global quadratures that can be squeezed below the shot-noise limit. The nonclassicality has also a direct signature in the joint photon-number distribution, which we obtain in closed form. Moreover, coupling each mode to an ancillary vacuum at a balanced beam splitter yields a four-mode state with entanglement in select $2\times2$ bipartitions, with local description given by two TGS states, and all $1\times3$ bipartitions.   For fixed input squeezing, increasing the twist parameter activates entanglement where the state is otherwise separable and deepens it where already present. The classical physicality bound on the twist parameter coincides with the quantum physicality condition. These results advance the two-way bridge between classical beam engineering and quantum information.

</details>

**Authors:** Zeping Shi, Wenbin Wu, Zhiwei Zhang, Yuhan Du, Chenyao Xu, Congming Hao, Xiangyu Jiang, Xin Chen, Guangyi Wang, Mingsen Zhou, Chunhui Pan, Wei Lu, Hao Shen, Haifeng Pan, Zhenrong Sun, Junhao Chu, Xiang Yuan

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced magneto-infrared spectroscopy system designed to overcome polarization degradation caused by light beam reflections in high-field environments. By implementing a collimated optical architecture and placing the polarization module outside the magnet bore, the setup achieves superior polarization fidelity and low noise floors. This platform enables sensitive, continuous measurements of magnetic field effects on material optical properties via linear and circular polarimetry.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum information theory, this work represents a significant advancement in experimental quantum optics instrumentation. The ability to perform polarization-resolved spectroscopy *in situ* under extreme conditions (high B-field, vacuum) is crucial for probing fundamental quantum phenomena like Landau quantization and magnetic circular dichroism.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving high-fidelity, continuous in-situ polarization control for magneto-infrared spectroscopy measurements performed within strong magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The authors report a novel collimated system that successfully integrates remote polarization modules and multiple interchangeable focusing optics, enabling robust polarization-resolved measurements across various geometries.

**Method.** The methodology involves redesigning the optical path using Kepler type telescopes to collimate the FTIR beam, thereby suppressing detrimental multi-wall reflections in long light tubes. Polarization control is achieved externally via automated polarizers and Fresnel rhombs.

**Model / system.** The system is a complex magneto-infrared spectroscopy platform featuring incident/exit collimation chambers, interchangeable focusing modules for Faraday/Voigt geometries (transmission/reflection), and an external polarization module operating under vacuum conditions. It operates within a superconducting magnet bore up to 50 mm.

**Key observables.** Linear polarization extinction ratio (up to 40:1), minimum root-mean-square noise ($\le 0.0033\%$), relative transmission/reflectivity spectra as a function of magnetic field and polarization angle.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength (up to 12 T), sample bore size ($\sim 50 	ext{ mm}$), spectral range (broadband, VIS to FIR).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The collimation design successfully suppresses higher-order wall reflections compared to conventional direct focusing schemes. The polarization module is placed outside the high-field region to maintain functionality.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the overall system schematic (Fig. 1), incident/exit collimation optics (Fig. 2, Fig. 3), the in-situ polarization module setup (Fig. 4), and the four interchangeable magneto-infrared focusing modules supporting various geometries (Faraday/Voigt transmission/reflection) (Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the scientific need for polarization control, details the optical engineering solution (collimation architecture), describes the modular hardware implementation (polarization module and focusing optics), and finally demonstrates its capability through measurements of linear and circular polarimetry on single crystals.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magneto-infrared spectroscopy under strong magnetic fields provides a powerful probe of Landau quantization and field-induced collective excitations, yet its full potential has long been constrained by the lack of in-situ polarization control, because the highly divergent infrared beam propagating through narrow light tubes undergoes multiple wall reflections, leading to severe polarization degradation. Here we report a collimated magneto-infrared spectroscopy system that integrates continuous in-situ polarimetry. The system employs incident and exit collimation chambers forming a Kepler type optical architecture, which converts the large-aperture FTIR output into a low-divergence beam and strongly suppresses multi-reflection trajectories inside long gold-plated light tubes, thereby enhancing both optical throughput and polarization fidelity. A remotely controlled polarization module, consisting of an automated linear polarizer and a switchable Fresnel rhomb positioned entirely outside the high-field region, enables continuous in-situ tuning between linear, circular, and arbitrary elliptical polarization states without thermal cycling, manual realignment, or breaking vacuum. Interchangeable compact focusing modules further support Faraday and Voigt geometries in both transmission and reflection experiments within a 50 mm magnet bore, providing efficient beam focusing and signal collection while maintaining polarization fidelity. The setup achieves a minimum root-mean-square noise of 0.0033%, an average noise of 0.0082%, and a linear polarization extinction ratio up to 40:1. We demonstrate the capability through continuous in-situ linear polarimetry and broadband circular polarimetry in the magneto-infrared spectroscopy of various single crystals. This platform establishes a robust experimental framework for in-situ polarization-resolved magneto-infrared spectroscopy.

</details>

(c) Exciton local density of states ρ(x, ω), showing bound-state spectral weight localized in energy and modulated periodically in space, with moir´e minibands as a consequence of the incommensurability of the on-site modulation V (x). (d) Total exciton density of states, separated into scattering-continuum and...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an advanced tensor network framework to solve tight-binding Hamiltonians on systems of unprecedented size. By mapping large lattices onto efficient pseudospin chains, it allows routine calculation of complex observables like spectral functions and topological invariants for quantum materials at the mesoscopic scale. This methodology overcomes severe memory bottlenecks associated with simulating ultra-large quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable numerical toolkit for simulating complex quantum matter phenomena (like moiré physics or correlated states) that are typically restricted to small system sizes by conventional diagonalization methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is simulating quantum materials on systems so large (billions of sites) that explicit matrix representations for tight-binding Hamiltonians become computationally prohibitive.

**Main result.** The authors present a unified tensor-network methodology capable of solving these ultra-large scale problems by mapping the system onto a manageable pseudospin chain, keeping memory scaling logarithmic with system size.

**Method.** They utilize Matrix Product Operators (MPO) and Tensor Network algorithms, combined with the Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM), to evaluate observables without explicit diagonalization or storage of massive matrices.

**Model / system.** The method applies to tight-binding Hamiltonians on various lattices, including moiré/super-moiré heterostructures and quasicrystals. It handles complex physics like non-Hermiticity, correlation effects (Hubbard model), and excitonic binding.

**Key observables.** Spectral functions $A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$, real-space topological invariants (Chern marker), local density of states (LDOS), time-dependent currents, and exciton spectral weight.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System sizes up to billions of sites; bond dimension ($\chi$) remaining modest (tens) for compressible structures; Chebyshev polynomial order ($N_{	ext{cheb}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The efficiency relies heavily on the tensor network compressibility inherent in the real-space structure, and spectral calculations are approximations via KPM.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mapping from large real-space lattices to 1D pseudospin chains (Fig. 2), the calculation of various observables like spectral functions and topological markers using MPO representations (Figs. 1, 6, 7, 10).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops algorithms for encoding Hamiltonians ($\hat{H}$) as MPOs, then details specialized pathways to compute key quantities: spectral functions via KPM, topological invariants via position operators, and time evolution/correlations using MPO-MPO products.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding quantum materials at meso and even macroscopic scales requires tight-binding calculations on system sizes where explicit matrix representations become prohibitively costly. This represents a major bottleneck to rationalize phenomena in moiré and super-moiré heterostructures and quasicrystals. Here, we present a unified tensor-network methodology to solve tight-binding problems at exceptionally large scales, by mapping a system of $N = 2^L$ sites onto a many-body problem of $L$ pseudospin sites, which is subsequently solved with tensor network algorithms. For Hamiltonians with compressible real-space structure, the tensor network bond dimension remains modest, typically of order a few tens, independent of $N$.Tensor network representations of arbitrary hopping functions including long-range, spatially modulated, and twisted-layer couplings are built with quantics tensor cross interpolation, and all physical observables are evaluated entirely with tensor network algebra without explicit matrix storage or diagonalization. We demonstrate applications to spectral functions, momentum-space spectra via the tensor-network quantum Fourier transform, real-space topological invariants, real-time dynamics, correlation induced symmetry breaking with self-consistent mean-field calculations, non-Hermitian phenomena, and excitonic many-body physics. Our methodology enables routinely solving systems with billions of sites, by leveraging the tensor network compressibility of real-space structures, and establishing a flexible framework to study quantum matter at ultra-large length scales. The methodology is implemented in the open-source Julia package TensorBinding.jl.

</details>

**Summary.** This research analyzes charge separation efficiency in organic solar cells by comparing analytical rate models with detailed kMC simulations. It demonstrates that while large offsets are traditionally favored, introducing Gaussian energetic disorder can unexpectedly boost efficiency at low offsets. This enhancement occurs because thermalization within the disordered energy landscape generates an effective driving force for charge separation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models complex, non-equilibrium charge dynamics in a disordered solid-state material using advanced simulation techniques (kMC), directly addressing how local energetic disorder modifies fundamental electronic processes like exciton dissociation—a key topic in open quantum systems and condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge separation efficiency in organic solar cells depends on the energetic offset between local exciton (LE) and charge-transfer (CT) states, particularly when significant Gaussian energetic disorder is present.

**Main result.** Energetic disorder can significantly boost charge separation efficiency at intermediate and low offsets by creating an effective driving force through thermalization within the disordered density of states. The rate model successfully captures shifts in efficiency explained by state degeneracies.

**Method.** The work compares results from a macroscopic two-state rate model against detailed, microscopic three-dimensional kinetic Monte-Carlo (kMC) simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is an organic solar cell modeled on a 3D simple cubic lattice simulating donor-acceptor interfaces. The dynamics involve exciton hopping, charge hopping, and dissociation governed by the $\Delta E_{LE-CT}$ offset.

**Key observables.** Internal Quantum Efficiency (IQE), relative steady-state populations of LE and CT states, and average energy levels ($\langle 	ext{ECT} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exciton-Charge Transfer Offset ($\Delta E_{LE-CT}$), Gaussian disorder strength ($\sigma_{	ext{dis}}$), and temperature ($k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rate model assumes constant rates and ignores triplet excitons; the kMC simulations assume a linear potential gradient (1 V built-in voltage) and focus on short-circuit conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate IQE dependence on $\Delta E_{LE-CT}$ for different disorder strengths, showing crossover behavior. Other figures map out how average CT energy shifts with disorder strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the rate model's agreement with kMC in the absence of disorder. It then introduces Gaussian disorder via kMC simulations to analyze the resulting crossover behavior and the mechanism by which thermalization creates an effective driving force for charge separation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

State-of-the-art organic solar cells increasingly rely on low-offset semiconductor blends, challenging the traditional requirement of a large energetic driving force for efficient charge separation. In these systems, the energetic offset $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$ between local exciton (LE) and charge-transfer (CT) states approaches the thermal energy, making exciton-CT hybridization and thermal repopulation of the exciton level critical to device performance. In this work, we directly compare a macroscopic two-state rate model with three-dimensional kinetic Monte-Carlo (kMC) simulations to investigate microscopic charge separation dynamics and the role of Gaussian energetic disorder. We demonstrate that in the absence of disorder, the analytical rate model accurately reproduces kMC predictions for the whole range of $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$. Specifically, the macroscopic model successfully explains horizontal shifts in the internal quantum efficiency curves that arise depending on how the energetic offset is physically realized in the constituent molecules. We show that these variations can be captured entirely through the ratio of degeneracies of the LE and CT states, respectively. Introducing Gaussian energetic disorder into the kMC simulation reveals a distinct crossover behavior depending on $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$. While disorder is mostly detrimental at large offsets, it can significantly boost efficiency at intermediate and low offsets. Thermalization of charge carriers within the disorder-broadened density of states creates an effective driving force allowing charge separation even at zero or negative energetic offsets.

</details>

**Summary.** This review comprehensively surveys the bidirectional relationship between Artificial Intelligence and Quantum Information. It details how AI can be applied to control and analyze quantum hardware, while also exploring how quantum structures—such as tensor networks—can enhance machine learning models. The paper emphasizes that future progress hinges on deeply integrating theoretical understanding with experimental realities.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level roadmap connecting modern ML techniques (like generative models and optimization theory) directly to the fundamental challenges of quantum computation, which is crucial for theorists working on resource estimation and algorithm design.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The review surveys the rapidly co-evolving interface between Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Quantum Information (QI). It addresses how AI can be used to control quantum systems, and conversely, how quantum computation enhances machine learning.

**Main result.** Progress requires a tighter integration of theory, experiment, and hybrid quantum-classical systems. The review details specific methodologies for both directions of interaction.

**Method.** The paper is a comprehensive survey organizing recent progress into two main streams: AI for QI (e.g., training algorithms on noisy data) and QI for AI (e.g., using tensor networks for ML representations).

**Model / system.** The underlying physical system involves $n$-qubit quantum systems described by density operators ($ho$) evolving under unitary or open-system dynamics. The analysis uses concepts like POVM measurements, expectation values, and parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs).

**Key observables.** Expectation values $\langle O angle_ho$, measurement probabilities $p_ho(s)$, Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), and expected return in RL ($J(\pi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales are governed by the number of qubits ($n$), the required precision ($\epsilon$), and the structure/sparsity assumptions used for approximation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A key assumption is that quantum information must be accessed indirectly via classical measurements. The review notes limitations when drawing analogies between classical and quantum statistical learning theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured by reviewing the two primary interaction directions: AI for QI (covering tasks like algorithm discovery and hardware stabilization) and QI for AI (examining algorithmic speedups, expressivity, and tensor network representations). It concludes with cross-cutting challenges.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial intelligence (AI) and quantum information (QI) are rapidly co-evolving. AI is becoming a practical tool for learning, designing, controlling, and verifying quantum systems, while QI offers new computational models, representational structures, and learning-theoretic questions for AI. This survey reviews the interface from both directions. In the AI for QI direction, we organize recent progress around the central tasks of extracting information from limited measurements, training and discovering quantum algorithms, stabilizing noisy hardware, automating experimental and programming workflows, and extending learning-based methods to sensing and networking. In the QI for AI direction, we examine how quantum computation and quantum-inspired structures affect learning through algorithmic speedups, expressivity, trainability, generalization, neural-network design, and tensor-network representations. We close by identifying cross-cutting challenges in reproducibility, scalability, hardware realism, and co-design, arguing that progress will depend on tighter integration of theory, experiment, and hybrid quantum--classical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes electron confinement and scattering in WSe2 quantum dots induced by localized magnetic fields. By solving the Dirac equation with complex boundary conditions, the authors show that the magnetic field suppresses tunneling and creates tunable resonant states. This establishes WSe2 as a promising platform for next-generation spintronic and valleytronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for controlling quantum transport in 2D materials using magnetic fields, which is highly relevant to understanding engineered quantum confinement effects beyond simple particle-in-a-box models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study theoretically investigates how confining massive Dirac fermions within a quantum dot formed by a localized magnetic field affects electron transport in WSe2.

**Main result.** The localized magnetic field effectively suppresses Klein tunneling and promotes the formation of stable, tunable quasibound states, leading to sharp resonance peaks.

**Method.** The authors derived exact wave functions and scattering coefficients using advanced mathematical techniques involving Kummer's confluent hypergeometric functions and Bessel/Hankel functions.

**Model / system.** The system is monolayer WSe2, modeled by an effective Dirac Hamiltonian incorporating spin-orbit coupling. The geometry involves a circular quantum dot subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field confined within the dot region.

**Key observables.** Scattering efficiency (Q), scattering cross-section ($\sigma$), and carrier density distributions ($ho(x, y)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Incident energy ($E$), magnetic field strength ($B$), and quantum dot radius ($R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the effective Dirac Hamiltonian approach and assumes specific boundary conditions for the magnetic vector potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate scattering efficiency (Q) versus B or R, showing resonance behavior, and density probability distributions ($ho$) confirming carrier localization patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using the Dirac Hamiltonian, derives analytical solutions by solving coupled differential equations in polar coordinates, calculates scattering coefficients via boundary matching, and finally analyzes the dependence of transport properties on $E$, $B$, and $R$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Monolayer tungsten diselenide (WSe$_2$) has become a suitable platform for quantum transport and spintronics and valleytronics applications because it possesses an intrinsic band gap and strong spin-orbit coupling and spin-valley coupling features. The electrostatic confinement of Dirac fermions proves challenging in graphene because of Klein tunneling, yet WSe$_2$ provides an environment that supports both carrier localization and the development of confined quantum states. In this work, we theoretically investigate the confinement of massive Dirac fermions in a WSe$_2$ quantum dot generated by a localized magnetic field. Using the effective Dirac Hamiltonian in the presence of a magnetic flux, we derive the exact wave functions and scattering coefficients by employing Kummer's confluent hypergeometric functions together with Bessel and Hankel functions. Our results show that the localized magnetic field provides an efficient mechanism to suppress Klein tunneling and promote the formation of stable quasibound states. We systematically examine the scattering efficiency and carrier density distributions as functions of the incident energy, magnetic field strength, and quantum dot radius. We find that low-energy carriers are strongly confined by the magnetic barrier, while the interplay between magnetic localization and geometric confinement gives rise to sharp and tunable resonance peaks. These results provide valuable insight into the control of spin-valley transport in transition metal dichalcogenide nanostructures and establish a theoretical basis for the development of quantum confinement devices and quantum information technologies.

</details>

### [Hierarchy of hidden nonlocality: A genuine activation of Incompletability](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00797v1)

**Authors:** Soumajit Das, Shampa Mondal, Preeti Parashar, Atanu Bhunia  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00797v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

A LOCC distinguishable, completable set of product state is trans- formed into a strictly incompletable, LOCC-indistinguishable set, si- multaneously activating incompletability and nonlocality.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.00797_figures/2607.00797_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2: Hierarchy between activation of nonlocality and activation of incompletability. Every incompletability activable set is nonlocality activable, while the converse does not hold in general.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous framework to compare different forms of quantum correlation by introducing the concept of 'activation of incompletability.' The authors prove that this phenomenon implies nonlocality, establishing a strict hierarchy between the two. This sheds new light on how local distinguishability breaks down when considering fundamental structural properties like basis completion.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep resource-theoretic understanding of quantum correlations, linking geometric properties (completeness) to operational limitations (LOCC distinguishability) in a way that is highly relevant for characterizing genuine nonclassicality.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the operational relationship between local distinguishability, quantum incompletability, and nonlocality for sets of orthogonal quantum states.

**Main result.** It establishes a strict hierarchy: activating incompletability implies activating nonlocality, but the reverse implication fails. Furthermore, it shows that such activation can be achieved while extending the set to a basis that is no longer perfectly distinguishable by LOCC.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical constructions involving Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC) protocols applied to multipartite quantum states, defining concepts like 'activation of incompletability' via measurements.

**Model / system.** The system under study consists of sets of mutually orthogonal quantum states in tensor product Hilbert spaces ($\mathcal{H}_1 \otimes \cdots \otimes \mathcal{H}_n$), analyzed using the operational constraints of LOCC and LICC.

**Key observables.** Local distinguishability (by LOCC), Incompletability, Nonlocality, Quantum Coherence, and local redundancy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the Hilbert space ($d_1, d_2, \dots$) and the specific structure of the orthogonal state sets are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is fundamentally restricted to operations achievable via LOCC or LICC, which defines the operational boundaries for distinguishability claims.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the hierarchy between activation phenomena (Incompleteness $\implies$ Nonlocality) and show examples of states that activate nonlocality without activating incompletability.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining necessary concepts (OPLM, locally irreducible/indistinguishable sets), then constructing explicit counterexamples ($S_3$) to establish the hierarchy, and finally analyzing the implications within the LICC framework concerning coherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum nonlocality admits several operational manifestations, one of which emerges from sets of orthogonal quantum states that cannot be perfectly distinguished by local operations and classical communication (LOCC). Such sets are regarded as nonlocal because their perfect discrimination requires global measurements. In contrast, sets that are perfectly distinguishable by LOCC are generally considered locally accessible and operationally classical. In this work, we investigate the role of incompletability in local state discrimination and introduce the notion of \emph{activation of incompletability}. Specifically, we demonstrate the existence of orthogonal sets that are initially perfectly distinguishable by LOCC and free from local redundancy, but which can be transformed via LOCC into strictly incompletable sets. We prove that activation of incompletability necessarily implies activation of nonlocality, whereas the converse fails in general, thereby establishing a hierarchy between the two activation phenomena. Furthermore, within the framework of local incoherent operations and classical communication (LICC), we show that any set whose incompletability can be activated can nevertheless be extended to a complete orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space, although the resulting completed basis is no longer perfectly distinguishable by LOCC. Our results uncover a fundamental interplay among local distinguishability, incompletability, coherence, and nonlocality, and provide new insight into the structure of locally accessible quantum information.

</details>
