**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that relying on simplified error models (like Pauli twirling or reset channels) when simulating complex quantum networks leads to inaccurate predictions of protocol performance. By analyzing entanglement purification and swapping, the authors show that neglecting coherent noise contributions causes significant discrepancies between predicted and true fidelity values. This mandates rigorous validation using complete noise architectures for reliable quantum technology forecasting.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides critical theoretical guidance on validating quantum network simulations, warning that computational shortcuts can lead to fundamentally incorrect predictions about achievable performance thresholds.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simplified error models used for simulating large-scale quantum networks can lead to severe quantitative and qualitative discrepancies in predicting protocol performance.

**Main result.** Neglected error contributions, particularly coherent off-diagonal terms, cause significant under-/over-estimations and outcome-dependent fidelity oscillations when compared to rigorous noise architectures.

**Method.** The authors analyze the failure of common approximations like Pauli twirling or using reset channels by comparing results from full quantum evolution models against simplified stochastic simulations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum networks implementing protocols such as entanglement purification (DEJMPS) and entanglement swapping in repeater chains, involving noisy Bell pairs.

**Key observables.** Fidelity, Negativity, and the state evolution across multiple sequential protocol steps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters like depolarization probability ($p_{dep}$) and over-rotation angles ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption being challenged is that standard stochastic approximations (like PTA or RC) accurately capture noise accumulation in complex, coherent quantum processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare fidelity and negativity across multiple steps for EPP and swapping, contrasting results from individual measurement outcomes versus ensemble averages, and comparing full models against simplified diagonal/reset channel approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the failure of noise approximations by first examining single-step protocols (EPP) to distinguish between outcome averaging and coherent effects, then extending this analysis to multi-step processes like entanglement swapping and repeater chains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient classical simulation of large-scale quantum networks frequently relies on noise approximations, which consider a restricted set of operators to describe noisy channels and operations. In this work, we demonstrate how such simplified error models, such as Pauli twirling or reset channels, can lead to severe quantitative and qualitative discrepancies in protocol performance predictions. We analyze, in particular, how small differences can accumulate in iterative and sequential protocols such as entanglement purification, entanglement swapping, and repeater chains. Our results reveal that neglected error contributions can lead to important performance under- and over-estimations, measurement-outcome dependency, and oscillations in the fidelity, which are entirely overlooked by the simplified error model approximations. These results show that rigorous validation of complete noise architectures is indispensable for accurately predicting operational thresholds in future quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel framework to train deep neural networks for inference on Ising machine hardware by treating the network as a sequence of thermodynamic blocks. It introduces 'Gibbs regularization' within a backpropagation scheme, allowing high accuracy while keeping computational cost manageable through careful control of sampling parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges statistical mechanics (Ising models, Gibbs sampling) directly into practical AI engineering, providing a concrete roadmap for realizing energy-efficient neuromorphic computing using principles from non-equilibrium statistical physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing scalable methods for training large Artificial Intelligence (AI) models to run inference on low-power thermodynamic computing hardware based on the Ising model.

**Main result.** The authors developed a purely backpropagation-based algorithm using Gibbs regularization that successfully trains deep convolutional networks, achieving high accuracy while demonstrating that inference cost is bounded by a controllable tradeoff with performance.

**Method.** Training involves a multi-phase process combining standard loss functions (CE) with specialized fixed-point and magnitude regularizers to stabilize the network weights for operation within stochastic Ising dynamics. Inference relies on time-averaging output spins via Gibbs sampling.

**Model / system.** The system is based on deep convolutional networks implemented as sequential 'thermodynamic blocks' operating on an Ising model Hamiltonian. The core computation uses high-temperature Gibbs sampling rather than ground-state annealing.

**Key observables.** Accuracy (e.g., 94.9% on CIFAR-10), Inference Cost, Integrated Autocorrelation Time ($	au_{	ext{int}}$), and the percentage of FLOPs offloaded to Ising hardware.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature gradient parameter ($\delta$), total number of sweeps ($G$), and various loss weighting parameters ($\alpha, \lambda_{	ext{FP}}, \lambda_{	ext{mag}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the time-averaged behavior of high-temperature Gibbs-sampled Ising systems can accurately emulate feed-forward neural inference. The theoretical analysis relies on linearizing dynamics around mean-field approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall classifier structure (Encoder $	o$ Blocks $	o$ Decoder) and detail the internal architecture of a single thermodynamic block as an Ising system with input, hidden, and output spins.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical correspondence between neural networks and Ising systems to developing the scalable backpropagation training algorithm via Gibbs regularization. It then analyzes error sources (related to $G$ and $\delta$) and concludes by discussing hardware implications for convolutional topologies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermodynamic computing devices based on the Ising model show great promise for low-power AI inference and edge computing, but scalable methods for training large models for such hardware remain limited. Prior theory shows that the time-averaged behavior of high-temperature Gibbs-sampled Ising systems can implement feed-forward neural inference. We turn this theoretical correspondence into a scalable and purely backpropagation-based algorithm for training deep convolutional networks for thermodynamic inference on Ising machine hardware. Our image classification models achieve accuracies of 94.9% on CIFAR-10 and 76.0% on CIFAR-100 under binary Gibbs sampling. We then develop and experimentally validate a mathematical theory relating inference cost to accuracy and controlling autocorrelation times. Subsequently, we calculate asymptotic results showing that inference cost is bounded by a well-controlled tradeoff with performance and exhibit algorithms for computing optimal inference schedules. Finally, we discuss implications for hardware development and the future of high-temperature thermodynamic AI models.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses single-cell measurements of bacterial growth and division (like size and time) to mathematically distinguish between competing theoretical models governing cell biology. By analyzing the full statistical relationships—including variances and correlations—the authors show that these quantitative tools can pinpoint whether a species follows an Adder, Sizer, or Timer mechanism, and whether its growth is linear or exponential.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is biology, the methodology relies heavily on advanced statistical mechanics tools—deriving moment equations, analyzing non-equilibrium steady states, and using probability distributions to constrain underlying physical rules. This mathematical rigor connecting microscopic processes to macroscopic statistics is relevant.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to differentiate between various underlying cell-division (e.g., Timer, Sizer, Adder) and single-cell growth paradigms (linear vs. exponential) in microbial cells.

**Main result.** Analysis of statistical relationships, particularly those involving standard deviations ($\sigma$) and correlations, can successfully distinguish between the different division models and growth types.

**Method.** The authors derive analytical probability distributions and statistical moments (means, variances, covariances) for single-cell quantities based on assumed model parameters. These theoretical predictions are then compared against experimental data.

**Model / system.** The system is bacterial cell growth and division dynamics in culture. Models include Timer, Sizer, and Adder division rules, coupled with linear or exponential single-cell growth kinetics.

**Key observables.** Single-cell quantities: birth-size ($s_b$), division-size ($s_d$), division-time ($	au_d$), and division-added-size ($\Delta d$). Statistical measures include means, standard deviations, and Pearson Correlation Coefficients (P.C.C.).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model parameters defining the distributions of size/time additions ($\Xi(s_d)$, $\Omega(\Delta d)$), growth rate distribution ($\Lambda(\alpha)$), and partitioning fraction ($\kappa(eta)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include assuming independence between certain model parameters (e.g., growth rate $\alpha$ independent of size-related quantities) and using approximations like Taylor series expansions or 'sharply spiked' distributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare experimental single-cell distributions and correlation coefficients against analytical predictions for various combinations of division/growth models, with the Adder model showing better agreement in several instances.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing that correlations are useful, then demonstrating that probability distributions alone suffice. It systematically derives theoretical relationships for means, variances, and cross-correlations ($\langle \cdot angle$ vs $\sigma^2$, etc.) for different model combinations before comparing these to experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Complex interactions among regulatory molecules determine the rules underlying cell growth and division in microbial cells. While the governing molecular network may not always be obvious, it is well known that correlations among certain physiological quantities measured in experiments, such as birth-size, division-size, division-time, and division-added-size, can differentiate among various cell-division models, such as Timer, Sizer, and Adder. Here we show that, apart from these correlations, which we extend for the case of stochastic single-cell growth and stochastic asymmetric partitioning, probability distributions of these quantities and statistical relationships between them can also be used to differentiate between these division models. Interestingly, we show that these quantities can not only differentiate the division models, but also distinguish among the single-cell growth paradigms, such as linear and exponential growth. We then demonstrate this differentiability among various division and growth models by comparing our analytical results with published experimental data. We further show that these results remain valid even when the growth rate of a cell is correlated with the growth rate of cells from previous generations in the lineage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a scaling theory to characterize how quantum critical dynamics near physical boundaries differ from bulk behavior. Using the 2D quantum Ising model, the authors derive distinct power-law decay exponents ($	heta_1, 	heta'_1$) for boundary correlations depending on the specific boundary universality class. This work offers new insights into boundary criticality and its implications beyond standard quantum-classical mappings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide a detailed, quantitative framework for understanding how spatial boundaries fundamentally alter the dynamics of quantum phase transitions, which is crucial for modeling confined or patterned quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates universal short-imaginary-time quantum critical dynamics occurring near boundaries, recognizing that boundary effects modify standard bulk critical scaling behaviors.

**Main result.** The paper establishes distinct scaling laws for boundary order parameter decay and correlation functions depending on the boundary universality class (ordinary vs. special), showing that key exponents do not follow conventional quantum-classical mappings.

**Method.** A universal scaling theory is developed, extending the Short-Distance Expansion (SDE) to analyze power-law decays of various correlation functions in imaginary time ($	au$).

**Model / system.** The primary model studied is the two-dimensional (2D) quantum Ising model on a square lattice with mixed boundary conditions (periodic in one direction, open/boundary along another). The Hamiltonian includes transverse field and nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Key observables.** Boundary order parameter ($M_s$), autocorrelation functions ($A_s$), and two-time correlation functions ($P_s, G_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis distinguishes between 'ordinary' and 'special' boundary universality classes, characterized by different critical exponents ($eta_1$, $	heta_1$, $	heta'_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work relies on developing and verifying scaling forms derived from extending the SDE to quantum short-imaginary-time dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate data collapse for various correlation functions ($M, A, P, G$) in both bulk and boundary regimes, confirming predicted power-law scaling laws for different transition types.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general scaling theory, applies it to the 2D quantum Ising model using QMC simulations, derives specific exponents ($	heta_1, 	heta'_1$), and verifies these results by analyzing multiple correlation functions ($A, P, G$) across different boundary universality classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While imaginary-time evolution has long served as a standard paradigm for ground-state preparation in numerical simulations and quantum devices, its intrinsic dynamical properties has been largely overlooked. Here, we investigate the short-imaginary-time critical dynamics in quantum systems with boundaries. A universal scaling theory is developed and verified in the two-dimensional quantum Ising model, uncovering rich dynamic critical behaviors dictated by boundary universality classes. For ordered initial states, the boundary order parameter $M_s$ decays with imaginary time $τ$ as $M_s \propto τ^{-β_1/νz}$, where $β_1$ denotes the boundary order parameter exponent, and $ν$ and $z$ correspond to the correlation length exponent and the dynamic exponent, respectively. For disordered initial states, the autocorrelation of the boundary order parameter is governed by a novel critical exponent $θ_1$, which is closely related to the critical initial slip behavior of $M_s$ characterized by the corresponding exponent $θ_1'$. In contrast to its positive bulk counterpart, the boundary initial-slip exponent $θ_1'$ is negative for the ordinary transition while remaining positive for the special transition. Although the static universality classes of $d$-dimensional quantum phase transitions generally coincide with those of $(d+1)$-dimensional classical phase transitions, we show that $θ_1$ does not follow this conventional quantum-classical mapping. We further discuss the implications of our results for more exotic forms of boundary criticality. Our findings provide new physical insights into boundary critical dynamics and offer a novel route for probing exotic boundary critical behaviors in quantum many-body systems.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.00767"></a>
### [Power-Law Relaxation of Non-Gaussian Parameter and Self-Dynamic Structure Factor in Multidimensional Rugged Energy Landscapes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00767v1)

**Authors:** Bimman Bagchi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00767v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a rigorous framework to study diffusion on rugged energy landscapes by analyzing the time evolution of higher moments, particularly the non-Gaussian parameter. It establishes that this relaxation exhibits distinct power-law scaling depending on dimensionality ($d$), providing a sensitive diagnostic tool beyond simple effective diffusion coefficients.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit derivation of dimension-dependent power laws for non-Gaussian relaxation, going beyond simple mean-square displacement analysis, provides a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing disorder effects in complex systems relevant to quantum dynamics and transport.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to characterize finite-time diffusion dynamics, specifically focusing on the power-law relaxation of non-Gaussian parameters ($\alpha_2(t)$) and the self-dynamic structure factor in multidimensional rugged energy landscapes.

**Main result.** The paper predicts dimension-dependent power-law decays for $\alpha_2(t)$: $t^{-1/2}$ in 1D, $(\ln t)/t$ in 2D, and $t^{-1}$ for $d>2$, which serve as distinct signatures of mobility heterogeneity.

**Method.** The theoretical framework uses a propagator-based approach derived from the master equation, relating higher moments (like $\alpha_2(t)$) to the time correlation of the local diffusivity field via a mobility-memory approximation.

**Model / system.** The system is tagged-particle diffusion on a multidimensional lattice with quenched disorder, where hopping rates are governed by site energies following the Miller–Abrahams rule. The landscape is modeled as correlated quenched Gaussian random fields.

**Key observables.** Non-Gaussian parameter ($\alpha_2(t)$), Self-dynamic structure factor ($F_s(\mathbf{k}, t)$), Apparent diffusion coefficient ($D_{	ext{app}}(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality ($d$), Ruggedness amplitude ($\epsilon$), Spatial correlation length ($\xi$), Initial preparation parameter ($eta_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of a homogenized diffusive regime for long-time limits and uses approximations relating $\alpha_2(t)$ to the variance of the time-integrated local mobility.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate analytical predictions showing the characteristic power-law decay of $\alpha_2(	au)$ in 1D, confirming the $t^{-1/2}$ dependence based on raw data and log-log plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from a general propagator formulation to derive specific results for low dimensions (e.g., 1D benchmark) before establishing the universal dimensional hierarchy ($d=1 	o t^{-1/2}$, $d=2 	o (\ln t)/t$, $d>2 	o t^{-1}$) by analyzing the self-dynamic structure factor's small-$k$ expansion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ruggedness of the underlying energy landscape gives rise to heterogeneous mobility and non-Gaussian diffusion. We develop a theoretical framework for tagged-particle diffusion in multidimensional rugged energy landscapes modeled as correlated quenched Gaussian random fields. Using the self-propagator and self-dynamic structure factor, we characterize finite-time diffusion beyond the effective diffusion coefficient. We determine the effects of dimensionality, spatial correlations, and initial preparation. By introducing a coarse-grained mobility field and a mobility-memory approximation, we relate the non-Gaussian parameter to the time correlation of the mobility sampled by the particle. In the homogenized diffusive regime, the mobility correlation decays algebraically, leading to long-time relaxation of the non-Gaussian parameter as $t^{-1/2}$ in one dimension, $(\ln t)/t$ in two dimensions, and $t^{-1}$ for $d>2$, with amplitudes that depend on dimensionality and the initial ensemble. Our results show that rugged energy landscapes leave distinct signatures in the effective diffusion coefficient, self-dynamic structure factor, and relaxation of non-Gaussian fluctuations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the limitations imposed by optical imperfections—specifically laser chirp, interferometer delay, and imbalance—on time-bin BB84 QKD systems. By simulating and measuring visibility degradation, the authors quantify how these factors reduce the achievable Secret Key Rate. The work is important for developing robust quantum communication hardware by providing concrete metrics for required component stabilization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges fundamental quantum optics characterization (chirp measurement) directly into the practical performance limits of a quantum communication protocol (QKD), which is crucial for building real-world quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how imperfections like interferometer delay, imbalance, and laser chirp degrade the performance of a time-bin BB84 quantum key distribution (QKD) system.

**Main result.** System visibility is highly sensitive to these parameters; specifically, laser chirp introduces significant degradation in the Secret Key Rate that must be accounted for beyond simple visibility loss calculations.

**Method.** The authors combine simulations of QKD performance with experimental measurements using advanced techniques like PROUD (ultrafast optical differentiation) to characterize the chirp.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a time-bin BB84 QKD protocol implemented using an imbalanced interferometer setup. The analysis models pulse propagation and interference visibility under varying delay, imbalance, and frequency chirp.

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR), Interference Visibility (V), Intensity Imbalance (U), and the Chirp parameter ($\Delta\omega$ or $eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System visibility ($V$), relative time delay ($	au$), intensity imbalance, and chirp coefficient ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that linear chirp is the dominant source of phase variation; discrepancies between simulated unchirped results and experimental data are used to quantify the chirp effect.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SKR vs. visibility (Fig. 1), setup schematics for visibility measurement (Fig. 2) and chirp retrieval (Fig. 4), and contour plots showing visibility dependence on imbalance/delay (Fig. 3, Fig. 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the QKD performance metric (SKR vs. V), then measuring how delay and imbalance affect V, and finally introducing chirp analysis using PROUD to quantify an additional, significant degradation factor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the effect of interferometer delay and imbalance on the performance of a BB84 time-bin quantum key distribution system. We simulate the impact of interference visibility on system performance and measure the visibility of a pair of interferometers as a function of their relative time delay and intensity imbalance. In addition, our analysis highlights the effect of laser chirp on system performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the conditions under which exotic dimer states are guaranteed to be the ground state in generalized spin chains and ladders with long-range and anisotropic interactions. By combining theoretical semi-positivity arguments with numerical validation, it maps out precise phase boundaries that extend the known understanding of quantum magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical criteria for identifying specific quantum phases (dimerized) in complex, interacting many-body systems, which is crucial for understanding magnetic materials where long-range correlations are important.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining precise, explicit conditions on coupling constants for long-range interacting spin chains and ladders to guarantee that the exact dimer state is the ground state.

**Main result.** The authors derive sufficient conditions (e.g., involving non-negativity of auxiliary coefficients $\alpha_n$ and $eta_n$) under which the dimer state is guaranteed to be the ground state, often extending beyond previously known necessary conditions.

**Method.** The methodology combines theoretical derivation using semi-positivity arguments on the Hamiltonian ($ar{H} \ge 0$) with validation via exact diagonalization (ED) for finite systems and analysis of anisotropic quantum top models.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on spin-1/2 interacting quantum spin chains and ladders, described by generalized Hamiltonians including long-range interactions ($J_n$ and $K_n$) and anisotropy terms ($\gamma_\mu^n$).

**Key observables.** Ground state energy (EGS), Dimer entanglement entropy ($\Delta S_{	ext{dimer}}$), and the non-negativity of derived coefficients ($\alpha_n, eta_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants $\{J_n\}, \{K_n\}$, anisotropy parameters $\{\gamma_\mu^n\}$, and system size $L$ (often assumed even).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a ground state characterized by semi-positivity, and some derivations rely on finite-size approximations or specific structural assumptions about coupling ranges.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing boundaries between Dimer, Ferromagnetic (FM), and Partially Magnetized states for both isotropic and anisotropic chains/ladders, comparing theoretical predictions with ED results.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing general Hamiltonian forms to deriving necessary conditions via semi-positivity arguments ($\alpha_n \ge 0, eta_n \ge 0$). It then tackles the complexity of anisotropy by relating it to quantum top spectra and concludes by comparing theoretical sufficient conditions against numerical ED results on phase diagrams.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interacting spin chains and ladders are known to support a plethora of quantum phases with complex ground-state phase diagrams. In this work, we study a large family of such models and determine precise, explicit conditions under which an exact dimer state is guaranteed to be the ground state. These general conditions are validated for various generalizations of the Majumdar-Ghosh model using exact diagonalization. Our results provide exact reference points in the phase diagrams of a wide class of spin chains and ladders, including those with anisotropic and arbitrary-range interactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes that exploiting symmetry in Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) requires enforcing consistency across all computational interfaces: encoding, dynamics, measurement, and readout. By developing 'observable-orbit completion,' the authors demonstrate that aligning these four components yields significant performance boosts over models where symmetry is broken at any single point.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous architectural guide for designing quantum machine learning models, showing that the physical implementation details—especially measurement design—are as critical as the underlying Hamiltonian dynamics for extracting useful information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is ensuring that symmetry present in time-series data (like cyclic patterns) is preserved and visible throughout the entire Quantum Reservoir Computing pipeline, as mere symmetry in one component is insufficient.

**Main result.** Achieving strong predictive gains requires aligning symmetry constraints across all four interfaces: input encoding, reservoir dynamics, measurement observables, and classical readout. This alignment mechanism proves crucial for successful data-driven quantum modeling.

**Method.** The authors employ a systematic 'four-interface audit' comparing fully aligned models against baseline configurations to quantify the necessity of symmetry preservation at every stage, particularly focusing on observable-orbit completion in measurements.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) applied to cyclic forecasting tasks on periodic rings. The dynamics involve mapping inputs through fixed quantum evolution and measuring expectation values before training a classical readout layer.

**Key observables.** Normalized Root Mean Square Error (NRMSE), percentage gain in forecasting accuracy, and the measured feature map $\Phi_M(x)$ restricted to the span of measured observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of sites on the ring ($K$), fixed feature budget (e.g., 61 features/site), and the specific symmetry group action ($\mathbb{Z}_K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A critical limitation is the 'measured-span obstruction': if a symmetry-carrying channel is not explicitly measured, it cannot be recovered by the classical optimization process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate performance gains comparing baseline vs. fully aligned models across various tasks (e.g., Advection, L96), and tables detail the systematic audit results for the E/H/O/R switches.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the necessity of symmetry alignment by first defining the QRC framework, then introducing observable-orbit completion to enforce measurement symmetry, followed by a comprehensive factorial audit across all four interfaces (E, H, O, R), and finally validating these findings on diverse tasks including real weather data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Symmetry is a powerful inductive bias, but in quantum reservoir computing (QRC) it cannot be imposed only by making the reservoir symmetric. QRC maps inputs through fixed quantum dynamics into nonlinear expectation-value features and trains only a classical readout, so the relevant symmetry must be visible in the measured feature map. We study cyclic forecasting tasks, such as sensors around a turbine or weather stations along a latitude circle, where the same local pattern should be forecast by the same rule wherever it appears on the ring. Thus, rotating the input by one site should rotate, not change, the predicted field. We show that a symmetric Hamiltonian is not enough: even large Pauli measurement sets can fail if their channels do not match the data symmetry, since optimization cannot recover channels that were never measured. We address this through observable-orbit completion, which measures symmetry-related observable channels and aligns encoding, dynamics, measurement, and readout. The strongest gains arise from aligning all four interfaces together, with matched spin-ring, real-weather, and IBM hardware checks supporting the same measured-span mechanism.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a powerful Monte Carlo technique to calculate free energy differences between distinct global sectors of lattice models. Applied to the 3D Ising model, it yields precise values for symmetry-twisted Casimir effects and spectral gaps by analyzing finite-size corrections in various geometric limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the methodology of using complex sampling techniques (like flat histograms) to extract subtle spectral information (gaps, boundary effects) is highly relevant to modern quantum many-body simulations where direct measurement of ground state properties is difficult.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to estimate partition function ratios between distinct global sectors of a lattice theory, specifically for the critical 3D Ising model.

**Main result.** The method successfully yields the symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference $\Delta Z_2 = 0.327(2)$ and estimates the $Z_2$-odd energy gap $\kappa_{\sigma} = 1.230(3)$.

**Method.** It employs a Monte Carlo strategy using an enlarged configuration space to sample an interpolating family of lattice systems, allowing for direct reconstruction of free energy differences via flat histogram methods.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the three-dimensional Ising model simulated on a slab geometry ($R^2 	imes S^1_{L_z}$) at its bulk critical point. The analysis compares the untwisted (periodic) sector with the $\mathbb{Z}_2$-twisted (antiperiodic) sector.

**Key observables.** Sector free energy difference ($\Delta F_{\mathbb{Z}_2}$), symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference ($\Delta Z_2$), and spectral gap estimates ($\kappa_\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves extrapolations in the large-$q$ limit ($q=L/L_z 	o \infty$) and the small-$q$ limit ($q \ll 1$), as well as finite-size scaling parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a suitable interpolating family for sampling and relies on extrapolations to infinite volume and specific limits (e.g., $q	o\infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate large-$q$ evaluations of slab data versus $L_z$, showing convergence towards $\Delta Z_2$, and stability checks for both the Casimir difference and the gap exponent $\kappa_\sigma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the general Monte Carlo framework using an enlarged configuration space, applies it to the 3D Ising model on a slab geometry, performs finite-size scaling analysis by extrapolating results in both large-$q$ and small-$q$ regimes, and concludes with universal values for sector differences.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a Monte Carlo strategy for directly estimating partition function ratios between distinct global sectors of a lattice theory. It enlarges the configuration space to sample an interpolating family whose endpoints are the desired sectors, and uses flat histogram methods to reconstruct the corresponding free energy difference. Although the construction is more general, we focus here on the three-dimensional Ising model on the slab $\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}_{L_{z}}$ at the bulk critical point, comparing the untwisted periodic sector with the $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-twisted antiperiodic sector. A large-volume and aspect ratio extrapolation gives the symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference $Δ_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=0.327(2)$ directly, without lattice derivatives or bulk subtractions. This provides an independent twisted sector probe of tensions observed in periodic sector thermodynamic Casimir observables. More generally, the method gives direct but selective numerical access to CFT compactification data, including estimates of the effective thermal screening scale and the $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-odd sector energy gap on $T^{2}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work resolves a conflict regarding the measured speed of a quantum particle tunneling through a barrier, where experimental estimates disagreed with Bohmian predictions. The authors show that naive time estimates fail because they ignore entanglement between degrees of freedom. By correctly applying and conditioning the dwell-time formula, they demonstrate perfect agreement with Bohmian mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides deep insight into the interpretation of quantum measurements in non-trivial regimes (like tunneling) and rigorously tests the equivalence between different formalisms (QM vs. Bohmian mechanics) under specific physical constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves discrepancies between particle speed estimates in classically forbidden regions (tunneling) derived from experiments/heuristics and predictions made by Bohmian mechanics.

**Main result.** A correct application of the dwell time formula, conditioned on penetration into the barrier, yields results that exactly agree with Bohmian mechanical predictions for the average velocity.

**Method.** The analysis compares three methods—tunneling time estimate, Büttiker dwell time formula, and Bohmian trajectory calculation—by analyzing time-dependent wave packets in 1D and 2D coupled waveguide models.

**Model / system.** The system is a non-relativistic quantum particle modeled by the Schrödinger equation in a potential landscape featuring a potential step realized via coupled waveguides (a double-well structure). The analysis covers both propagating and evanescent regimes.

**Key observables.** Particle populations ($|\Psi|^2$), Bohmian velocities, average speeds ($ar{v}_B$), and conditional dwell times ($	au_{pen}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evanescent decay constants ($\delta_\kappa, ar{\kappa}$), energy splitting ($J_0$), spectral width ($\sigma_k$), and characteristic equilibration length scales ($X_{eq}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial discrepancy arose from assuming the tunneling time is set only by transverse coupling (ignoring longitudinal entanglement) or applying the standard Büttiker formula without conditioning on barrier penetration.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate potential landscapes, relative population dynamics in both oscillatory (propagative) and monotonic (evanescent) regimes, and numerical simulations comparing $|\Psi|^2$ density with Bohmian trajectories.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the experimental challenge, then analyzing the theoretical estimates (tunneling time/dwell time), demonstrating where these estimates fail due to entanglement effects in the evanescent regime, and finally resolving the conflict by applying a corrected, conditional dwell-time argument that matches Bohmian predictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, Sharoglazova et al. [Nature 643, 67 (2025)] proposed a procedure for determining the speed of a quantum particle in the classically forbidden region of a potential step, and implemented it in a beautiful experiment. The inferred speeds disagree significantly with the Bohmian velocities, which the authors presented as an experimental challenge to Bohmian mechanics. This is puzzling because the speeds are inferred from particle populations in coupled waveguides for which Bohmian mechanics and standard quantum mechanics make identical predictions. We resolve the puzzle by a detailed theoretical analysis of the experimental setup. We show that the speed inference rests on an assumption that fails in the relevant (evanescent) regime according to both Bohmian mechanics and standard quantum mechanics -- namely that the inter-waveguide tunneling time is set by the transverse coupling and is not affected by entanglement with the longitudinal degree of freedom. We also consider a second speed estimate suggested by Sharoglazova et al., which is based on the Büttiker dwell time formula for particles in the forbidden region. We show that the authors applied the formula incorrectly, and that a correct application yields exact agreement with the predictions of Bohmian mechanics. Our analysis includes explicit calculations of Bohmian trajectories, dwell times, and longitudinal speeds in the two-dimensional waveguide model of the experiment.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses Quantum Monte Carlo simulations on a fractionalized model to study the transition between an antiferromagnetic insulator and a d-wave superconductor. The key finding is evidence for a second-order deconfined quantum phase transition (DQCP) at which both magnetic and superconducting orders vanish continuously. This provides theoretical support for understanding competing orders in strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the use of lattice gauge theory coupled to fermionic excitations and the explicit study of quantum critical points provide rich theoretical tools applicable to strongly interacting many-body systems relevant in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum phase transition between an antiferromagnetic (Néel) insulator and a nodal d-wave superconductor in a lattice model.

**Main result.** Evidence is found for a second-order deconfined quantum phase transition (DQCP) where both Néel order and d-wave superconductivity orders vanish continuously.

**Method.** Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations are employed on a fractionalized representation of the electron using spinon and chargon fields to avoid the sign problem.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled on a square lattice at half-filling, described by an SU(2) lattice gauge theory coupled to fermionic spinons and bosonic chargons. This setup mimics aspects of high-temperature superconductivity in cuprates.

**Key observables.** Electron spectral function $A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$, dynamical spin structure factor $\chi_S(\mathbf{q}, \omega)$, and correlation ratios for dSC, AFM, and VBS orders.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transition is tuned by parameters like $\kappa$ and $\kappa_	au$, which control the gauge field dynamics and coupling strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the half-filled, particle-hole symmetric case to ensure the QMC simulation remains sign-problem free.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams mapping out dSC, AFM, and deconfined regimes, alongside spectral functions showing Dirac dispersion in the superconducting phase that gaps into a gapped spectrum in the antiferromagnetic phase near the transition point.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the fractionalized Hamiltonian using spinon/chargon fields, details the QMC simulation techniques (including gauge field updates), and then analyzes key observables like spectral functions and structure factors across different parameter regimes to pinpoint the DQCP.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a quantum Monte Carlo study of the transition between the insulating Néel state and the nodal $d$-wave superconductor on the square lattice at half-filling. We access a regime of frustrated magnetic order without a sign problem using a parton representation of the electron in terms of fermionic spinons and bosonic chargons. Both partons move in a background $π$-flux (so the electron experiences no net flux) and are coupled to a quantum fluctuating SU(2) lattice gauge field. In contrast to earlier studies directly on the electronic degrees of freedom, we find evidence for a second-order deconfined quantum phase transition at which both the Néel and $d$-wave superconductivity orders vanish continuously. We compute correlators of the spinon-chargon composite with the same quantum numbers as the electron: we find a gapless Dirac dispersion inside the $d$-wave superconductor, turning into a gapped dispersion in the antiferromagnet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates qubit leakage as a major error source in transmons by quantifying 'leakage mobility.' Using advanced perturbation theory and non-Hermitian Hamiltonians, the authors derive conditions to suppress unwanted excitation hopping. They conclude by proposing and analyzing two passive hardware schemes that utilize this engineered mobility for effective leakage removal.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed, quantitative theoretical tools for mitigating decoherence in superconducting qubits by engineering the system's Hamiltonian to actively suppress leakage pathways using tunable couplers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Qubit leakage is a significant error source in quantum computation, necessitating methods to characterize its dynamics and implement passive removal units.

**Main result.** The authors propose two passive leakage removal units—one based on a tunable coupler/pumped transmon and another on junction readout—whose performance can be analyzed using leakage mobility concepts.

**Method.** The study employs perturbative methods, including Schrieffer-Wolff transformations and non-Hermitian Hamiltonians, to analyze the effective coupling strengths and decay rates in open quantum systems.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is superconducting transmons coupled via tunable couplers. The analysis extends beyond the computational subspace to include leakage excitations (e.g., $|2angle$ states).

**Key observables.** Leakage hopping rates, population decay times ($T^*$), effective coupling strengths ($g_{	ext{eff}}$), and residual ZZ interactions ($\zeta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frequency detunings ($\Delta$), anharmonicity differences ($\Delta U_{12}$), and coupler/qubit coupling strengths ($g_c, g_q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dispersive regime ($\lambda \ll 1$) and uses approximations like the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA) for passive dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate connectivity maps, detuning conditions required for interaction cancellation, time evolution of leakage populations in different removal schemes (Coupler20, Pumped21), and effective hopping strengths vs. detuning.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes leakage dynamics across increasing complexity: first characterizing general mobility; then analyzing two-transmon systems to suppress coherent exchange ($g_{	ext{eff}}=0$); subsequently extending to three-transmon chains to study higher-order tunneling; and finally proposing specific passive removal units.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qubit leakage is a noticeable source of errors for quantum computing. In quantum processors, leakage excitations traveling between qubits generate correlated errors and perturb gate implementations. Leakage mobility can also be utilized for creating dedicated leakage removal pathways and removal units. To quantitatively characterize leakage mobility and to guide better design of processor architectures, we study here leakage dynamics in transmons with tunable couplers through numerical and analytical methods. Even if the couplers are tuned to cancel the single-excitation exchange or the ZZ interaction, the leakage hopping rates still persists in the range of 0.8-10 MHz due to transmon nonlinearity. In typical operation regimes, however, transmon frequency detuning localizes leakage excitations. The next-nearest-neighbor transmons can be still be near-resonant opening leakage tunneling channels. To suppress longer-range hopping, we find that the frequency spread of the next-nearest-neighbor transmons needs to be in the range of 1-4 MHz. Utilizing leakage mobility, we propose two passive leakage removal units. One is based on a tunable coupler and a pumped transmon, and another on a junction readout scheme. Based on realistic experimental parameters, our results on selectively mobilizing or localizing leakage excitations are readily applicable in superconducting quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes topological Anderson insulators under long-range correlated disorder, demonstrating that such correlations cause the system's properties to become non-self-averaging. The authors introduce the concept of a 'non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator,' characterized by a finite relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent in the thermodynamic limit. This shows that for these systems, individual sample measurements are fundamentally different from ensemble averages.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the understanding of topological phases into complex disordered regimes, providing a quantitative diagnostic ($R_\gamma$) for when simple averaging techniques fail, which is crucial for modeling real-world amorphous or glassy quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates topological Anderson insulators subjected to long-range correlated disorder, an area lacking tractable analytical methods compared to standard uncorrelated disorder models.

**Main result.** Long-range correlated disorder can induce non-self-averaging behavior in the topological states, meaning single sample properties deviate significantly from ensemble averages.

**Method.** The study uses numerical calculations involving ensemble averaging over multiple disorder realizations and analyzing the relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent to diagnose self-averaging breakdown.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a disordered Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain, where the disorder ($\epsilon_j$) is characterized by long-range correlations defined by a power-law spectral density $S(k) \sim 1/k^\alpha$.

**Key observables.** Topological Quantum Number ($Q$), Lyapunov Exponent ($\gamma$), and the Relative Variance of the Lyapunov Exponent ($R_\gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correlation parameter $\alpha$ (where $\alpha=0$ is uncorrelated disorder) and the system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that non-self-averaging is confirmed by a finite relative variance ($R_\gamma$) in the thermodynamic limit, signaling a breakdown of the Central Limit Theorem.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $\langle Q angle$, $\langle \gamma angle$, and $R_\gamma$ across different correlation parameters ($\alpha$), showing smooth crossovers for long-range disorder versus sharp transitions for uncorrelated disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the limitations of current theory, introducing the model with long-range correlated disorder, demonstrating that this correlation leads to non-self-averaging behavior via finite $R_\gamma$, and concluding that ensemble averages are insufficient for characterizing the phase diagram.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Current research on the disordered topological quantum phases primarily focuses on the uncorrelated and short-range correlated disorder regime. These topological Anderson insulators are typically self-averaging. However, topological quantum systems with long-range correlated disorder have received limited attention due to the absence of tractable analytical methods. In fact, the long-range correlated disorder introduces more complex effects on topological quantum states. Here, we demonstrate that the long-range correlated disorder could induce anomalously statistical feature where the topological Anderson states become non-self-averaging, and the phase diagram is strongly dependent on individual disorder configurations. We term this statistical phase the non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator. The non-self-averaging property is identified by the non-vanishing finite values of the relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent in the thermodynamic limit, alongside the non-Gaussian distributions of the Lyapunov exponent. Consequently, the topological properties of a single disordered sample deviate from the ensemble average, causing a breakdown of the central limit theorem. The non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator provides insights into the interplay between correlated disorder and topology.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously classifies three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes by developing advanced graph-theoretic and combinatorial techniques. It demonstrates that these paradoxical structures are far more abundant than previously believed, overturning restrictive assumptions about the necessary form of such paradoxes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the limits of local realism in multipartite quantum systems, which is foundational knowledge for understanding genuine quantum advantage.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To completely classify all three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes established via a biconditional parity proof, extending beyond previously known examples like the GHZ paradox.

**Main result.** The authors provide a complete structural classification for these paradoxes, showing that the landscape of nonlocality is much richer than previously thought by identifying new exotic paradoxes.

**Method.** The work employs graph-theoretic formalisms (2-CNF formulae and implication graphs) combined with combinatorial techniques to map the set of specifications ($	ext{BPP}$) to a canonical data structure ($	ext{CanonicalTriples}$).

**Model / system.** The focus is on three-qubit quantum systems exhibiting 'nonlocality paradoxes,' which are logical obstructions to classical probabilistic models arising from restricted joint measurability contexts.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal fraction, the existence of a witness path in the implication graph $I_{\Psi}(z)$, and the structure of the associated biconditional parity proof.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits (fixed at three), measurement settings $\{M\}$, and parameters defining the entangled state $|	ext{B}(\lambda, \Phi)angle$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initially relied on five restrictive assumptions; later work drops these to show a richer structure. Specific analyses focus on interpolant states or restricted Charlie measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of balanced states and schematic forms of equations relating measurement angles $(	ext{A}, 	ext{B}, 	ext{C})$ to target directions, alongside implication graphs for specific examples.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining contextuality and nonlocality in three qubits (Chunk 2), establishing the link between paradoxes and biconditional parity proofs using graph theory (Chunk 3), developing a structural classification via 'Charlie clocks' (Chunk 4), presenting counterexamples outside interpolant states (Chunk 5), and finally confirming paradoxicality through detailed analysis of witness paths in implication graphs for various scenarios (Chunks 6, 7, 8).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum nonlocality paradoxes, such as that of GHZ, provide maximally sharp logical obstructions to classical probabilistic models of quantum correlations. They are key resources in a broad variety of information-theoretic tasks that exhibit unconditional quantum advantage. For example, in nonlocal games, which are communication tasks that serve as core technical tools in recent landmark results in quantum computational complexity theory.   Their role in establishing quantum advantage motivated their study by Abramsky et al. who introduced an infinite family of three-qubit paradoxes exhibiting novel conditional structure. This was later extended by de Silva et al. into a full classification program.   In this work, we completely classify all three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes established via a biconditional parity proof; this is a very large class of paradoxes that encompasses all earlier-known examples. We do this by introducing a suite of new structural and combinatorial techniques. We find that the landscape of nonlocality paradoxes is far richer than previously understood, violating regularity conditions underlying all prior constructions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates optimizing observables in four-level quantum systems using advanced quantum control techniques. It proves that the null control acts as a high-order trap and numerically demonstrates that the efficiency of finding optimal controls varies dramatically depending on whether the system Hamiltonian resembles a V-V structure or a chain interaction. This highlights how subtle differences in system physics dictate the feasibility of quantum state preparation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of quantum control theory by quantifying how system structure dictates optimization success near degenerate or trapped points, which is crucial for designing robust quantum protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the quantum control landscape for optimizing an observable by finding optimal external time-dependent controls $f(t)$ in specific four-level quantum systems.

**Main result.** The null control is proven to be a five-order trap, and the optimization efficiency shows a diverse behavior: sharp increases for V-V systems versus slower changes for chain interaction systems.

**Method.** The work combines analytical proofs regarding the order of traps with numerical simulations using GRAPE (unconstrained) and GPM (constrained) algorithms.

**Model / system.** The system involves four-level quantum dynamics governed by a Hamiltonian $H(t) = H_0 + f(t)V$. Specific models compared are a V-V type system and an anharmonic system with chain interaction.

**Key observables.** Optimization efficiency (fraction of successful runs), the objective functional $	ext{Tr}[U_f^T ho_0 U_f^\dagger T O]$, and the order of the control traps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial control distance $l$ from the null control, and system-specific Hamiltonian parameters defining the V-V vs. chain interaction structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes controllability of the quantum system and relies on Taylor expansion methods to define trap orders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level structures for the two systems (V-V and chain), and show the fraction of failed runs versus initial control distance $l$ for both optimization methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by proving the null control is a high-order trap. It then proceeds to numerical experiments comparing GRAPE and GPM performance on two distinct four-level models, highlighting quantitative differences in efficiency behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we perform an analytical and numerical analysis of quantum landscapes for controlling special four-level quantum systems for which we prove that the null control is a five-order trap: a $V-V$ system and an anharmonic system. As a control goal, an observable optimization is considered. The rigorous theoretical analysis is followed by the numerical experiments based on the GRadient Ascent Pulse Engineering (GRAPE) algorithm and Gradient Projection Method (GPM), performed to investigate the behavior of the efficiency of optimization for unconstrained (using GRAPE) and constrained (using GPM) controls. As the main result, we observe an interesting phenomenon with a diverse behavior of the optimization efficiency depending on the system Hamiltonian -- sharp increase of the optimization efficiency up to 100% at certain distance from the null control for a V-V system, while much slower and less significant increase (and even small decrease) for a system with the chain interaction. This sharp difference might be related with the fine structure of the subspace of controls where second derivative of the objective functional is zero.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analytically connects the exact solutions for a periodically-driven two-level system under linear driving and the rotating-wave approximation by mapping the dynamics onto Heun differential equations. The authors use sophisticated mathematical techniques, including hypergeometric function expansions and perturbative analysis, to rigorously demonstrate this connection while recovering crucial physical corrections like the Bloch-Siegert shifts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical insight into the quantum dynamics of fundamental models like qubits, connecting complex physical approximations (RWA) to exact analytical forms through advanced special functions theory. This is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing a rigorous analytical connection between exact solutions for periodically-driven two-level systems (TLS) derived under different physical approximations.

**Main result.** The authors successfully establish a direct analytic link between the exact solutions obtained for linear driving and those for the rotating-wave approximation, recovering higher-order corrections like Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts.

**Method.** The analysis maps the time-dependent Schrödinger equation onto Heun differential equations (or confluent forms) and utilizes techniques involving hypergeometric functions, path-multiplicative Floquet solutions, and perturbative expansions in the weak-driving limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a periodically-driven two-level system (TLS), modeled by a time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t) = f(t) \cdot \sigma$, where $\sigma$ are Pauli matrices. The dynamics are governed by the Schrödinger equation, which reduces to Heun equations.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the time evolution of the system's state vector components ($\psi_e(	au)$), and derived quantities include the Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the driving frequency ($\omega$), the transition frequency ($\omega_0$), and the ratio between them, which define different regimes (weak-driving, high-frequency, resonant).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping to Heun equations and is most rigorously developed in the weak-driving regime ($	ilde{f} = o(1)$), though it claims general validity.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the structure implies mathematical derivations involving differential equations, expansions, and parameter constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the governing Heun equations for different driving types (linear vs. RWA), derives exact solutions using hypergeometric function representations, establishes the connection via perturbative analysis, and verifies results in various limiting regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate and establish an analytic connection between the exact solutions describing the dynamics of a two-level system driven by periodic external fields, focusing on the cases of linear driving and the so-called rotating-wave approximation, or circular driving. In both cases, the exact solutions can be obtained by mapping the Schrodinger equation onto Heun equations: the confluent Heun equation for linear driving and the Heun equation for the rotating-wave case. In particular, we demonstrate a direct analytic connection between the exact solutions for linear driving and those for the rotating-wave case. This result is obtained by analyzing local solutions expressed in terms of hypergeometric functions, which, in the case of the confluent Heun equation, can be derived by considering path-multiplicative Floquet solutions involving a bilateral series. This series leads to two continued-fraction expansions that can be perturbatively solved by imposing a suitable consistency condition. The connection between the linear-driving and rotating-wave solutions is established through a perturbative procedure that allows us to recover not only the rotating-wave approximation itself, but also the correct Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts, as well as the so-called high-frequency approximation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive free-energy framework combining DFT and an extended Hubbard model to explain the structural phase transition in $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$. The analysis reveals that this transition is governed by a delicate balance between phonon entropy (stabilizing the high-T phase) and spin entropy/lattice dimerization (favoring the low-T phase). This provides a detailed, multi-faceted understanding of how competing entropic forces dictate material structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit coupling of vibrational entropy (phonon softening/hardening) with magnetic spin entropy to drive a structural transition is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in correlated materials, bridging solid-state physics and statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to clarify the microscopic origin of a first-order structural phase transition in $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$ around 90 K, which suppresses its potential quantum spin liquid (QSL) state.

**Main result.** The transition is jointly driven by phonon and spin entropy; high temperatures favor the $\alpha$ phase due to soft phonons and large spin entropy, while low temperatures favor the $eta$ phase via interlayer dimerization and spin-singlet formation.

**Method.** A unified free-energy framework combining first-principles DFT calculations with an extended Hubbard model is employed to analyze lattice, phonon, and spin contributions separately.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$, modeled using a low-energy effective Hamiltonian derived from the $2a_1$ orbital manifold. The total free energy incorporates DFT energies, harmonic phonon free energy, and Hubbard model contributions.

**Key observables.** Helmholtz free energies ($\alpha$ vs $eta$), phonon frequencies, generalized enthalpy under pressure, and transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical structural transition temperature ($T_c \approx 113	ext{ K}$), the critical uniaxial pressure for suppression ($\approx 2.6	ext{ GPa}$), and Hubbard parameters ($U, V, t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include using the harmonic approximation for phonons (neglecting anharmonicity) and treating the low-energy subspace explicitly within the correlated model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phonon dispersions showing softening in $\alpha$ vs. hardening in $eta$, total free energy crossing curves determining $T_c$, and generalized enthalpy plots demonstrating pressure stabilization of the $\alpha$ phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds a comprehensive free-energy functional by combining DFT results with contributions from lattice vibrations (phonons) and electronic correlations (Hubbard model). It analyzes how entropy terms dictate the stability crossover between the two structural phases ($\alpha \leftrightarrow eta$) under varying conditions like temperature and pressure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As a prototypical flat-band cluster Mott insulator on an effective triangular lattice, Nb$_3$Cl$_8$ is a potential candidate for hosting a quantum spin liquid (QSL) state. Nevertheless, a first-order structural phase transition around 90K transforms the high-temperature paramagnetic $α$ phase into the low-temperature nonmagnetic $β$ phase, suppressing the candidate QSL regime of the $α$ phase. To clarify the microscopic origin of this transition, we combine first-principles calculations with an extended Hubbard model to construct a unified free-energy framework. This framework reveals that the transition is jointly driven by phonon and spin entropy: the $α$ phase is stabilized by softer phonons and larger paramagnetic spin entropy, whereas the $β$ phase is favored by interlayer dimerization, which hardens the phonons and quenches the spin entropy through singlet formation. Furthermore, by evaluating the pressure-dependent generalized enthalpy, we provide a thermodynamic explanation for the suppression of the transition under c-axis uniaxial pressure, where stabilizing the $α$ phase may allow the candidate QSL regime of the $α$ phase to be explored at low temperatures.

</details>

### [Fisher Glasses: Tail-Certified Quantum Metrology in Quenched Environments](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01085v1)

**Authors:** El Mustapha Mansouri, Keigo Arai

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'tail-certified' quantum metrology, recognizing that standard averaging of Fisher information fails when environmental noise freezes during measurements. It develops a rigorous framework using Conditional Value-at-Risk to certify the true achievable precision based on the worst-case (upper tail) performance across many noisy sessions. This is critical for designing robust quantum sensors operating in complex, fluctuating environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, advanced statistical tool for quantum sensing that moves beyond simple mean estimates to quantify performance guarantees against rare but detrimental noise realizations, which is vital for real-world quantum hardware deployment.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard quantum metrology certification based on averaged Fisher information fails when sensors operate in quenched environments where environmental noise varies between measurements. The goal is to quantify the true, robust precision achievable under these non-self-averaging conditions.

**Main result.** The certified resource must be quantified using tail statistics (Conditional Value-at-Risk) applied to the inverse loss, which yields a 'tail-certified information' that can be orders of magnitude higher than the average QFI in certain regimes.

**Method.** The methodology involves projecting out nuisance directions associated with slow environmental fluctuations and then applying extreme value theory concepts via CVaR to the resulting inverse-loss function. This establishes a rigorous, non-averaging certification bound.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to quantum sensors like shallow Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers, superconducting qubits, and semiconductor spin qubits operating in environments with latent disorder ($\xi$). The theoretical framework models the measurement process as conditional on this frozen environmental state.

**Key observables.** Tail-Certified Information (ITC), Inverse-Loss Tail Exponent ($eta$), Transverse Fisher Information ($F_{	heta|\mu}$), and the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tail index $eta$ dictates finite certification; safe windows, portfolio size ($K$), and the CVaR confidence level ($\alpha$) are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption violated by standard theory is that the experiment samples an averaged channel; instead, it samples a specific, frozen environmental configuration ($\xi$).

**Figures summary.** Figures contrast average-QFI optimization (which can be 'tail-catastrophic') against tail-certified designs (like portfolios or reserve channels), showing significant recovery of certified information. They also illustrate the dependence on the inverse-loss tail exponent $eta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the failure of averaged metrics, then developing the quenched certification pipeline ($	ext{Condition} 	o 	ext{Project} 	o 	ext{Invert Loss} 	o 	ext{Tail Certify}$). It establishes theoretical no-go theorems and concludes with practical design laws (e.g., Fisher reserves) demonstrated on NV center simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum metrological advantage is certified by averaged Fisher responses: contrast, susceptibility, or quantum Fisher information (QFI). This fails in quenched sensors, where slow environmental variables freeze within a session but vary between repetitions: shallow nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers, superconducting qubits with slow two-level fluctuators, and semiconductor spin qubits in drifting charge noise. They sample session-resolved Fisher geometries, not an averaged channel. Certification conditions on the latent session, projects nuisance directions, inverts to attainable loss, then tail-certifies; this inverse upper-tail loss defines quenched tail-certified information. A no-go theorem: no averaged Fisher data determine this certificate; ensembles sharing averaged Fisher matrix, QFI, and projected information have finite or zero certified precision. A Fisher-zero integrability transition governs collapse: the inverse-loss tail exponent $β$ sets the boundary, with nonintegrable certified loss for $β\le 1$, even when annealed information is large or scaling. The certified quantum resource is response transverse to latent disorder, not raw amplification sharing its generator; universal design laws: safe windows, nondegenerate portfolios, Fisher reserves, action separation, Fisher-cut criteria. A shallow-NV Ramsey tournament shows average-QFI optimization is tail-catastrophic, whereas tail-certified designs recover nearly three orders of magnitude in certified information at equal shot budget and latent ensemble. These non-self-averaging phases are Fisher glasses, governed by Fisher-zero rare-event statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a double-occupancy cell fluid model with Curie-Weiss interactions, demonstrating its rich phase diagram structure. By relating it to the Blume-Capel model, the authors map out multiple coexistence regimes, including gas-liquid and liquid-liquid transitions. The complexity arises from the interplay between local repulsion and global attraction.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the mapping to the Blume-Capel model connects it to quantum spin systems, making the study of complex phase transitions relevant for understanding critical phenomena in many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex phase diagram and rich thermodynamic behavior of a fluid modeled by a double-occupancy cell model interacting via Curie-Weiss forces.

**Main result.** The competition between local repulsion and long-range attraction generates complex phase behavior, including regimes with single/double critical points, tricritical points, and triple-point formation, leading to gas-liquid and liquid-liquid coexistence.

**Method.** The analysis uses the grand-canonical ensemble formalism, employing analytical techniques like Laplace's method on integrals derived from the partition function.

**Model / system.** The system is a double-occupancy cell fluid model ($n_l \in \{0, 1, 2\}$) whose Hamiltonian is shown to be isomorphic to the Blume-Capel model on a complete graph. Interactions include global Curie-Weiss attraction and local repulsion.

**Key observables.** Critical temperatures ($T_c^*$), critical densities ($ho_c^*$), triple-point coordinates, and phase coexistence lines (Gas $\leftrightarrow$ LDL $\leftrightarrow$ HDL).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $a = \Delta/J$, which governs the transition between different types of critical points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed in the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$), and the analysis distinguishes results for distinguishable versus indistinguishable particles.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagrams (T*-rho* vs a) showing coexistence lines, critical point loci (blue/red), and triple-point lines (black).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model's isomorphism, deriving the grand partition function, applying advanced mathematical techniques (Laplace's method), and then detailing the phase diagram analysis for both distinguishable and indistinguishable particle cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A double-occupancy cell model of a fluid with Curie-Weiss interaction is studied. First, we show that the model is isomorphic to the Blume-Capel model on a complete graph through a simple transformation from spin to occupancy variables. We then investigate its phase behavior within the grand-canonical ensemble using a combination of analytical and numerical methods. Despite its simplicity, the model exhibits a remarkably rich thermodynamic behavior depending on the ratio between the local repulsive and global attractive interactions. We identify regimes characterized by a single critical point, two distinct critical points, tricritical behavior, and triple-point formation. For sufficiently strong repulsion, the system possesses three fluid phases of different densities, leading to both gas-liquid and liquid-liquid coexistence. The locations of the critical, tricritical, and triple points are determined, and the corresponding phase diagrams are constructed. These results demonstrate that the competition between double-occupancy repulsion and long-range attraction is sufficient to generate complex phase behavior in a minimal multiple-occupancy lattice-gas model.

</details>
