**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the quantum-advantage resource of two-mode Gaussian light by solving a constrained convex optimization problem. It establishes that while simple cases yield closed forms, the generic case for coupled modes leads to an optimal value determined by the least real root of a degree-12 polynomial. This demonstrates a fundamental mathematical barrier to finding general analytical solutions in this quantum regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical insight into resource quantification in continuous variable quantum optics, establishing rigorous boundaries on what can be solved analytically versus what requires numerical methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the 'quantum-advantage resource' of a mixed Gaussian state, which requires solving a complex convex optimization problem.

**Main result.** For generic two-mode states with genuinely coupled modes, the optimal quantum-advantage resource cannot be expressed in terms of elementary functions or radicals due to an algebraic obstruction related to the Galois group $S_{12}$.

**Method.** The authors use convex optimization techniques, formulating the problem as a semidefinite program (SDP) and solving it by reducing it to maximizing a dual concave cost function.

**Model / system.** The system models multimode light described by its quadrature covariance matrix $V$. The focus is on the two-mode case ($M=2$), which represents the smallest regime exhibiting genuinely coupled modes.

**Key observables.** Quantum-advantage resource, $N_q(V)$, defined as $rac{1}{2}(	ext{Tr} V_{\star q} - 2)$; the optimal covariance matrix $V_{\star q}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of nonclassical directions ($\kappa$); compatibility between eigenvectors; and the structure of the polynomial whose root determines $N_q(V)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on reducing the SDP to a dual problem, and closed-form solutions are only possible when the system falls into specific 'decoupled strata' where eigenvector compatibility holds.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the structure is built around deriving polynomial equations whose roots determine the resource value.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the optimization problem (primal/dual), establishing an oracle formula for the minimizer, analyzing specific low-$\kappa$ cases ($\kappa=0, 1$), and culminating in the general result that requires solving a degree-12 polynomial equation for coupled systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the problem of extracting a quantum complexity resource from a mixed Gaussian state of the multimode light. We present the first complete, certificate-checked solution to this problem in a genuinely coupled sector. We carry this out for the two-mode case, the smallest case in which modes are genuinely coupled. Even in this case the solution is highly nontrivial, and we rigorously prove that it cannot be given in a closed form.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper classifies complex two-qudit gates within finite fields by analyzing their structure using symplectic groups. It establishes precise quantitative bounds on 'local masking'—the ability to hide input information from subsets of output qubits while retaining full recoverability. This has significant implications for designing secure quantum communication protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (symplectic geometry, algebraic combinatorics) to quantify quantum information security primitives like data hiding and local observability in structured quantum computation models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper classifies two-qudit finite-field Clifford dual-unitary gates and applies this classification to determine exact local masking properties in homogeneous brickwork quantum circuits.

**Main result.** It proves that sufficiently short quantum messages can be completely hidden from any one- or two-qudit output subsystem, even when the input is entangled, while remaining perfectly recoverable from the full output.

**Method.** The analysis uses symplectic matrix representations over finite fields ($\mathbb{F}_q$) to classify gate loci and employs concepts like local masking distances derived from Weyl edge channels.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum circuits built using two-qudit gates defined over finite fields, specifically analyzing the structure of dual-unitary Clifford elements within $	ext{Sp}(4, q)$.

**Key observables.** Local masking distances ($d_1(t), d_2(t)$); symplectic core types ($S_\delta$, $SR$, $SS$); and recovery fidelities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The finite field size $q$; circuit depth $t$; and the determinant parameter $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between local gate equivalence and homogeneous circuit transport, and assumes specific boundary conditions for edge channel analysis.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize exact phase counts for different edge-transport phases within the dual-unitary symplectic classes; diagrams illustrate core structures related to cross-ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the finite-field Clifford representation, classifying dual-unitary gates and perfect tensors using symplectic invariants, developing the concept of masking distances via Weyl channels, and finally applying these results to derive specific local privacy guarantees.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We classify two-qudit finite-field Clifford dual-unitary gates and apply the classification to exact local masking and operator transport in homogeneous brickwork circuits. Under ordered one-qudit Clifford equivalence, the dual-unitary locus contains $q-2$ perfect-tensor cores, one rank-one core, and one SWAP core. The perfect cores are indexed by \[ δ=\det B=\det C, \] and are related to the ordered cross-ratio $λ$ of the associated $[4,2,3]_q$ MDS configuration through \[ λ=\fracδ{δ-1}. \] Homogeneous repetition separates these cores into five transport phases whose algebraic codimensions in $\operatorname{Sp}(4,q)$ are $0,1,2,3,4$.   The one-site Weyl edge channels determine exact local-masking distances. Perfect-tensor circuits attain \[ d_1(t)=4t, \qquad d_2(t)=4t-2, \] whereas delayed erasers satisfy \[ d_1(t)=4t-2, \qquad d_2(t)=4t-4 \] for $t\geq 2$. Consequently, sufficiently short quantum messages are completely hidden from every one- or two-qudit output subsystem, even when the input is entangled with a reference, while remaining exactly recoverable from the full output.   For $q=3$, we construct an explicit perfect-tensor Clifford gate from two inverse SUM gates. Exhaustive Weyl-support searches for $t=1,2,3$ reproduce the predicted masking distances, and a one-period Choi-channel calculation for a four-qutrit periodic circuit gives numerical residual leakage below $2\times 10^{-16}$. Under the coherent perturbation considered here, local leakage is linear in the perturbation strength, whereas the infidelity of recovery using the ideal inverse is quadratic near the perfect point.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors show that Bose-Einstein condensation leaves a universal 'cusp' signature in the time-of-arrival distribution of released trapped atoms. This cusp is quantified by a ratio ($R_\infty$) predicted to match the specific heat ratio of the gas, providing a robust, measurable diagnostic tool for BEC dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable dynamical signature of BEC that links time-domain measurements (TOA statistics) directly to fundamental thermodynamic quantities (specific heat ratios), offering an experimental test for many-body quantum phenomena in ultracold gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) produces a universal, measurable signature—a cusp—in the time-of-arrival (TOA) statistics of a harmonically trapped gas released into free fall.

**Main result.** The BEC transition imprints a universal slope ratio ($R_\infty \approx 2.556$) in the TOA statistics, which is predicted to equal the specific-heat ratio of the ideal trapped Bose gas.

**Method.** Semiclassical analysis of the time-of-arrival distribution ($\Pi(t)$) in the long time-of-flight limit, analyzing how the mean arrival time and standard deviation exhibit non-analytic behavior at the critical temperature ($T_c$).

**Model / system.** An ideal Bose gas confined in an anisotropic harmonic trap, analyzed after being released into free fall. The analysis is performed in the semiclassical far-field limit ($\epsilon \ll 1$).

**Key observables.** Time-of-arrival distribution $\Pi(t)$, mean arrival time $\langle T angle$, standard deviation $\Delta T$, and the universal one-sided slope ratio $R_\infty$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), trap frequencies ($\omega$), initial velocity variance ($\sigma_V^2$), and the small parameter $\epsilon = \sigma_V / \sqrt{2gH}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ideal gas limit, the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$), and the semiclassical far-field approximation. Weak interactions are tested but found to perturb the ratio only weakly.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of $\langle T angle - t_c$ and $\Delta T$, showing a clear cusp at $T=T_c$. Later figures confirm that finite atom number rounds this cusp, while weak interactions cause much smaller shifts in the ratio itself.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the TOA distribution semiclassically, derives model-independent relations connecting moments ($\langle T angle$ and $\Delta T$), calculates the universal slope ratio $R_\infty$, and finally tests this prediction's robustness against finite atom numbers and weak repulsive interactions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that Bose--Einstein condensation produces a cusp in the time-of-arrival (TOA) statistics of a harmonically trapped gas released into free fall. In the semiclassical long time-of-flight regime, with $ε=σ_V/\sqrt{2gH}\ll1$, both the mean and standard deviation of the arrival time distribution, which are governed by the longitudinal velocity variance, remain continuous, but acquire a cusp whose one-sided slope ratio is universal within the ideal-gas far-field limit, $\mathcal{R}_\infty=2.5556\ldots$, and equals the trapped-gas specific-heat ratio $C(Tc^-)/C(Tc^+)$. Finite atom number rounds the cusp and weak interactions perturb it only weakly, leaving a measurable time-domain signature of condensation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Dual-Rail Cat Code (DRCC), a concatenated bosonic error-correcting code designed for fault-tolerant quantum computing. It demonstrates that DRCC can support universal, bias-preserving logical gates using only beam-splitter interactions. Furthermore, it develops robust protocols by combining DRCC with outer repetition codes to deterministically correct photon loss errors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses key challenges in photonic quantum computation—namely, achieving high fidelity gates while managing realistic hardware errors like photon loss and decoherence—by proposing a novel code structure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols that can simultaneously support universal logical operations, suppress hardware noise, and efficiently handle photon-loss errors.

**Main result.** The proposed Dual-Rail Cat Code (DRCC) offers a robust bosonic encoding that preserves erasure bias during gate operations and allows for deterministic single-photon loss correction via concatenation with outer repetition codes.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving the construction of logical gates using beam-splitter interactions, analyzing error propagation using the Knill–Laflamme condition, and deriving stabilization mechanisms via engineered dissipation (Lindblad master equations).

**Model / system.** The system utilizes bosonic encodings, specifically the DRCC, which is a concatenated structure combining an inner cat code with an outer dual-rail structure. Operations are modeled using cavity modes ($\hat{a}_i$) and governed by Hamiltonians or dissipators.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates (X, Y, Z), relative geometric phases, success probability of QEC protocols, and expectation values of number operators ($\langle \hat{n} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon loss rate ($\kappa$), dephasing rate ($\gamma$), cat state parameter ($\alpha$), and the concatenation level (e.g., two-qubit or three-qubit repetition code).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations such as the small-$\alpha$ limit for dephasing noise or the large-$\alpha$ limit for photon loss, and assumes specific dissipation mechanisms are active for stabilization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of syndrome measurement using joint parity checks ($\hat{U}_{JPC}$) and show the structure of QEC protocols involving outer repetition codes concatenated with DRCC-1.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the limitations of individual bosonic codes to proposing the DRCC. It then details gate construction via beam-splitters, analyzes error correction under various noise models (photon loss/dephasing), and concludes by outlining advanced QEC protocols using outer repetition codes for robust fault tolerance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable fault-tolerant quantum computation requires quantum error-correcting codes that simultaneously support universal logical operations, suppress hardware-specific noise, and enable efficient handling of photon-loss errors. Bosonic encodings such as the dual-rail and cat codes each offer attractive features but also exhibit important limitations when used in isolation. The dual-rail code enables efficient single-photon-loss detection by converting leakage out of the computational subspace induced by photon-loss errors into an erasure error. In contrast, the cat code provides a resource-efficient, bias-tailored error-correction scheme with bias-preserving logical gate operations. Here, we introduce the dual-rail cat code (DRCC), a concatenated bosonic encoding that combines an inner cat code with an outer dual-rail structure, thereby inheriting and enhancing the advantages of both constituent codes. We analyse the error-correction properties of the DRCC and propose a deterministic single-photon-loss correction protocol by concatenating it with an outer repetition code. Exploiting the code's intrinsic noise bias, we construct a universal set of logical gates using only beam-splitter interactions and demonstrate that all logical operations preserve the erasure-biased noise structure. The DRCC offers several distinctive advantages, including the absence of relative geometric phases during gate operations, deterministic erasure detection and correction, and simultaneous syndrome extraction without interrupting stabilisation. These features make the DRCC a promising bosonic code for hardware-efficient, bias-preserving, and erasure-resilient fault-tolerant quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a novel, purely differential derivation of the Obara-Saika recurrence relation used to calculate electron repulsion integrals. By deriving these relations from fundamental properties of Gaussian basis functions, the authors create a hierarchical formulation that exposes independent primitive terms. This structure is particularly valuable for developing highly parallelized code implementations on modern GPU architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum chemistry integrals rather than AMO systems directly, the rigorous development of derivative-based computational schemes with a focus on GPU parallelization is highly relevant to developing efficient numerical solvers for complex many-body Hamiltonians encountered in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a computationally efficient method for evaluating complex two-center four-orbital Electron Repulsion Integrals (ERIs) required in quantum chemistry calculations.

**Main result.** The authors present a novel, rigorous derivation of the Obara-Saika recurrence relation based purely on differential relations between Gaussian basis functions, confirming its algebraic equivalence to established methods.

**Method.** The method involves applying repeated partial differentiation to unnormalized integrals and exploiting the structure of the resulting expressions to derive hierarchical recurrence relations for the ERIs.

**Model / system.** The calculations are performed within the framework of electronic structure theory using Gaussian basis sets (Cartesian, Hermite, Spherical Harmonics Gaussians) to represent molecular orbitals.

**Key observables.** Electron Repulsion Integrals (ERIs), specifically Coulomb and Exchange integrals ($J_{mn}, K_{mn}$), and the resulting recurrence relations (e.g., the vertical recurrence relation).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The angular momentum components of the basis functions, and the quadratic nature of the integration arguments $T$ and $u$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that exploiting differential structures allows for a systematic identification of independent primitive derivative quantities, which is crucial for parallelization.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes; the focus is on deriving and comparing algebraic relations (Eqs. 14, 20, 45, 46).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the ERI in terms of Gaussian basis functions, developing recurrence relations for auxiliary Boys functions via differentiation, and culminating in the differential derivation that yields the final OS vertical recurrence relation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Obara-Saika (OS) method is one of the most widely used techniques in quantum chemistry for evaluating electron repulsion integrals (ERIs) via a set of recurrence relations that build higher angular momentum integrals from lower-order ones. The original derivation by Obara and Saika proceeded by directly relating integrals of differing angular momentum. In this work, we present a compact novel derivation of the OS vertical recurrence relation based solely on differential relations between Gaussian basis functions, expanding on a method suggested in earlier work. By explicitly deriving the required derivative expressions we identify all non-zero primitive terms contributing to the full ERI to develop a hierarchical formulation of the OS recursion relations. This approach has pedagogical value as a rigorous and self-contained derivation. Additionally, the resulting organization exposes independent primitive derivative quantities and may be useful for code generation and parallel implementations on modern GPU architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates how a breathing mode structural distortion in Kagome materials can stabilize exotic superconducting phases. The authors classify possible pair symmetries and demonstrate that this specific distortion induces odd-frequency, dynamical Cooper pairs. This finding provides a mechanism for designing unconventional superconductivity using lattice engineering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the concept of dynamical pairing driven by structural modulation and its connection to unconventional superconductivity has parallels with engineered quantum systems where lattice vibrations or external fields induce novel many-body states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To classify superconducting symmetries in Kagome superconductors and demonstrate the emergence of odd-frequency dynamical Cooper pairs driven by structural distortions.

**Main result.** The breathing mode induces odd-frequency spin-singlet Cooper pairs, which are entirely dependent on the structural distortion breaking inversion symmetry.

**Method.** Full classification of superconducting symmetries using anomalous Green's function symmetry analysis and self-consistent solution of the Bogoliubov de Gennes Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is Kagome materials studied under a 'breathing mode' structural distortion ($t_a 
eq t_b$). The superconductivity is modeled using an onsite spin-singlet pairing approach within the Nambu Green's function formalism.

**Key observables.** Superconducting pair amplitudes, symmetry classes (e.g., OSEO), and gap opening in band structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structural distortion parameter ($t_a 
eq t_b$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and frequency/momentum dependence of pairing functions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes onsite spin-singlet superconductivity, though the resulting odd-frequency pairs imply sublattice symmetry breaking is key.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows pair amplitude dependence on imaginary time; Figure 2 compares band structures with and without distortion (showing gap opening); Figure 3 illustrates spectral function and OSEO pair amplitudes vs. frequency/momentum.

**Paper structure.** The paper first classifies all possible superconducting symmetries in the Kagome lattice, then analyzes how the breathing mode breaks inversion symmetry to stabilize a specific odd-frequency pairing state ($E^{(2)}_2$), which is shown to be non-local in time.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The breathing mode in Kagome materials is a structural modulation that breaks inversion symmetry and has been shown to be a crucial source for intriguing phases in the normal state. In this work, we carry out a full classification of superconducting symmetries in kagome superconductors and demonstrate the emergence of odd-frequency dynamical Cooper pairs entirely driven by the breathing mode. We then show that odd-frequency spin-singlet Cooper pairs can be realized by controlling the breathing mode in kagome lattices with conventional spin-singlet $s$-wave superconductivity. Since odd-frequency pairing is intrinsically nonlocal in time, our results put forward the breathing mode for designing dynamical Cooper pairs in kagome materials.

</details>

### [Leggett-Garg inequality in the massive scalar vacuum: No violation under spacelike-separated measurements](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00314v1)

**Authors:** Yang Xiang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00314v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2607.00314_figures/2607.00314_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. K3(τ, L) for the massive scalar ﬁeld with spacelike-separated measurements (L = τ +2+ǫ, ǫ = 10−3) for various masses m. The thin horizontal line marks the classical bound K3 = 1; for all masses considered, K3 remains strictly below 1. For each mass, the maximum occurs at the smallest accessible τ (τ = 0.001), and Kmax 3 decreases monotonically as m increases, from Kmax 3 ≈0.716 at m = 0.001 down to 0.403 at m = 0.4. For τ ≥2, the two time windows no longer overlap; the curves decay exponentially with τ, as expected from the spacelike decay of the massive Hadamard function.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper investigates whether the vacuum state of a massive scalar field violates the Leggett-Garg inequality (LGI), which tests for macrorealism. By employing a spacelike-separated measurement protocol, they rigorously satisfy noninvasive measurability conditions in QFT. The key finding is that $K_3 < 1$, demonstrating that temporal correlations in this free quantum field are constrained by causality and do not violate the LGI.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous methodological benchmark for future tests of macrorealism in relativistic quantum fields, distinguishing between entanglement-based nonlocality (Bell) and temporal constraints imposed by causality (LGI).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses testing for macrorealism using Leggett-Garg inequality (LGI) tests in relativistic Quantum Field Theory (QFT), specifically overcoming the challenge of ensuring noninvasive measurability (NIM).

**Main result.** Under strict spacelike separation conditions, the vacuum state of a free massive scalar field does not violate the LGI ($K_3 < 1$), establishing that macrorealism holds for temporal correlations in this system.

**Method.** The authors use a protocol involving three independent ensembles measured at causally disconnected (spacelike-separated) events to calculate the LGI parameter $K_3$ from two-time correlation functions derived via the Heisenberg picture.

**Model / system.** The model is the vacuum state of a free massive scalar field ($\phi$) in 1+1 dimensions. The analysis relies on calculating correlations using dichotomic observables, $	ext{sign}(\phi(f))$, and exploiting the Gaussian nature of the vacuum state.

**Key observables.** The LGI parameter $K_3 = 2C(	au, L) - C(2	au, 2L)$ and the two-time correlation function $C(f, g) = \langle 0| 	ext{sign}(\phi(f)) 	ext{sign}(\phi(g)) |0angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mass ($m$), time separation ($	au$), and spatial separation ($L$). The condition for NIM is $L > 	au + 2	au_0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a free field theory, which implies the vacuum state is Gaussian. The protocol rigorously enforces spacelike separation to satisfy the Noninvasive Measurability (NIM) criterion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots $K_3(	au, L)$ vs. $	au$ for different masses, showing that the calculated value remains below the classical bound of 1 and decays exponentially for large $	au$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the QFT framework for LGI testing under NIM, derives the necessary correlation functions using the Heisenberg picture, performs numerical computations for overlapping windows, and concludes by comparing the result to known Bell violations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We overcome the long-standing noninvasive measurability (NIM) challenge in Leggett-Garg tests by exploiting the causal structure of quantum field theory (QFT). Our protocol uses three independent ensembles of the vacuum state, each measured by a different pair of observers at spacelike-separated events, yielding the three two-time correlators. By placing these events at positions $(0,0)$, $(τ,L)$, and $(2τ,2L)$ with $L>τ+2τ_0$, we rigorously ensure that no measurement can influence another. We investigate the vacuum state of a free massive scalar field in 1+1 dimensions, employing the dichotomic observable $Q(f)=\operatorname{sign}(φ(f))$ where $φ(f)$ is the smeared field. In the Heisenberg picture, the time evolution is absorbed into a translation of the time-window function, allowing us to derive the two-time correlation function $C(τ,L)$ and the Leggett-Garg parameter $K_3=2C(τ,L)-C(2τ,2L)$. For non-overlapping time windows, we find that the correlation function decays exponentially with $τ$ for a massive field. For overlapping windows, our numerical computation for a rectangular time window yields $K_3<1$ across the entire mass range, firmly establishing that the vacuum does not violate the LGI. Thus, under strict noninvasive conditions, the vacuum shows no violation of macrorealism, in stark contrast to its well-known violation of spatial Bell inequalities. Our spacelike-separated protocol provides the first LGI test in QFT with rigorously satisfied NIM, setting a methodological benchmark for future studies and highlighting the fundamental distinction between spacelike entanglement and temporal macrorealism in relativistic quantum fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the experimental detection of flat energy bands near the Fermi level in the bulk Weyl semimetal $	ext{TaRhTe}_4$. Using ARPES, the authors demonstrated that these flat bands coexist with the material's topological properties, a finding that was not predicted by standard DFT calculations. This suggests $	ext{TaRhTe}_4$ is a promising platform for studying the interplay between topology and strong electronic correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state topology, the observation of emergent electronic states (flat bands) that deviate from standard mean-field theory predictions is highly relevant to understanding strongly correlated physics in novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was observing flat bands near the Fermi level in a nonmagnetic bulk Weyl semimetal, as such features are rare and unexpected based on initial theoretical calculations.

**Main result.** The study reports clear evidence of flat bands via ARPES measurements in $	ext{TaRhTe}_4$ that coexist with its nontrivial topological character (Weyl points), despite these bands not being predicted by DFT.

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) on cleaved single crystals with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe the electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{TaRhTe}_4$, a noncentrosymmetric van-der Waals type-II Weyl semimetal. Measurements were performed on different crystal terminations (A and B), comparing experimental data against DFT predictions.

**Key observables.** Flat bands (constant energy over momentum range), Fermi surfaces, Weyl points, and Fermi arcs connecting these points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The flat band energy was observed near $4 	ext{ meV}$ below the chemical potential ($	ext{E}_F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A key finding is that the observed flat bands were *not* predicted by DFT, suggesting a novel physical mechanism at play.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare ARPES and DFT Fermi surfaces for two terminations (A and B). Specific cuts highlight the distinct presence of momentum-independent flat bands in Termination A data relative to theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context by noting the rarity of flat bands in Weyl semimetals. It then presents comparative analysis using ARPES vs. DFT, focusing on identifying and characterizing the unexpected flat band features observed experimentally.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flat bands have been theoretically predicted for decades but have only recently been realized in quantum materials such as magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene, kagome and Lieb lattices, and rare-earth metal compounds. To date, only twisted layered materials have enabled tuning of flat-band energies near the electronic chemical potential, thereby influencing transport and thermodynamic properties. Here, we report the presence of flat bands near the chemical potential in bulk TaRhTe$_{4}$, a noncentrosymmetric van-der Waals type-II Weyl semimetal. Flat bands are rarely observed in Weyl semimetals, particularly in nonmagnetic bulk systems, and the observed flat bands were not predicted by density functional theory calculations. TaRhTe$_{4}$ therefore provides a platform in which nontrivial topology coexists with flat bands near the Fermi level, as evidenced by our angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses a sophisticated variational technique applied to the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator to derive the Wallis product. By analyzing the energy ratio in specific limits corresponding to odd and even effective angular parameters, the authors successfully connect quantum mechanical dynamics to fundamental infinite product identities. This demonstrates how deep mathematical constants can emerge from physical models involving inverse-square potentials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on AMO systems like cold atoms or cavity QED, the use of variational methods on singular potentials and the connection between quantum mechanics and fundamental mathematical constants (like $\pi$) via spectral analysis is highly relevant theoretical physics material.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives the Wallis product and its reciprocal using a quantum mechanical variational approach applied to a singular radial system.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that both infinite products arise naturally from the large-$
u$ limit of the variational energy ratio calculated for odd ($
u=2n-1$) and even ($
u=2n$) effective angular parameters.

**Method.** A variational method is employed using a quartic trial wave function family, $R_a(ho) = Nho^
u e^{-aho^4}$, to estimate the ground state energy of the singular harmonic oscillator.

**Model / system.** The model is the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator with an inverse-square interaction term. The system's dynamics are related to the three-dimensional Coulomb problem via a Kustaanheimo–Stiefel map, allowing for duality analysis.

**Key observables.** The variational energy ratio $R_{4d}(
u)$ and its limit as $
u 	o \infty$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Effective angular parameter $
u$, which is restricted to odd ($
u=2n-1$) or even ($
u=2n$) sequences.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the validity of the variational approximation and the use of a specific quartic trial wave function family motivated by Coulomb duality.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the effective Hamiltonian for the singular oscillator, applies the variational principle with the chosen trial function, calculates the energy ratio $R_{4d}(
u)$, and then analyzes the limits corresponding to odd and even $
u$ sequences to recover the Wallis product identities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a variational derivation of the Wallis product and its reciprocal from the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator. The inverse-square interaction is absorbed into an effective angular parameter $ν$, so that the lowest exact energy in a fixed sector is $E_{4d,\mathrm{exact}}=\hbarω(ν+2)$. Motivated by the radial Kustaanheimo--Stiefel relation $r=ρ^2$ between the four-dimensional oscillator and the three-dimensional Coulomb problem, we use the quartic trial family $R_a(ρ)=Nρ^νe^{-aρ^4}$. The minimized variational energy yields an accuracy ratio governed by adjacent Gamma functions. In the large-$ν$ semiclassical limit, this ratio approaches unity. Restricting $ν$ to the odd sequence $ν=2n-1$ gives the standard Wallis product, whereas the even sequence $ν=2n$ gives its reciprocal form. The Coulomb-dual interpretation further relates the two branches to integer and half-integer effective angular sectors in the dual Coulomb/MICZ description. The result shows that Wallis-type infinite products persist under an inverse-square deformation of the oscillator and arise from a common Gamma-function structure in radial variational dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses nanocrystals of NiS2 to probe the origin of weak ferromagnetism. By measuring magnetization and heat capacity, the authors confirm the retention of bulk AFM transitions while observing strong hysteresis only at low temperatures. They conclude that surface uncompensated moments are responsible for this magnetism, which constrains theoretical models regarding its true nature in the bulk material.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of surface effects in confined magnetic systems provides valuable insights into how dimensionality reduction modifies bulk magnetic ordering mechanisms, which is relevant for designing spintronic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the microscopic origin of weak ferromagnetism (WF) observed in NiS2, specifically determining if it arises from homogeneous magnetic canting or localized effects at antiferromagnetic domain walls.

**Main result.** The hysteretic ferromagnetic response below T_N2 is best explained by uncompensated surface moments generated where the low-temperature AFM order terminates at the nanocrystal surface. This indirectly favors a domain-wall scenario for bulk NiS2 WF.

**Method.** The research employed structural characterization (XRD, TEM) and detailed magnetic measurements (ZFC/FC magnetization curves, M-H loops) on synthesized NiS2 nanocrystals.

**Model / system.** The physical system is structurally well-ordered NiS2 nanocrystals. The investigation contrasts the observed magnetic behavior with theoretical models like homogeneous canting versus domain-wall localization mechanisms.

**Key observables.** Retention of two-step AFM transitions, large coercivity (Hc), exchange bias (HEB), and vertical loop shifts in M-H curves below T_N2.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanocrystal diameter ($27.0 \pm 6.5$ nm); magnetic transition temperatures ($T_{N1} \approx 39	ext{ K}$, $T_{N2} \approx 30	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed hysteresis is dominated by surface uncompensated moments, which helps constrain theoretical models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show TEM/XRD confirmation of NC structure, ZFC/FC curves showing magnetic anomalies near 30K and 40K, and M-H loops demonstrating clear hysteresis and HEB below T_N2.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the structural stability of the nanocrystals, measuring the bulk-like two-step AFM transitions magnetically and thermodynamically, analyzing the temperature dependence of magnetic hysteresis features (HEB/Hc), and finally comparing these findings against theoretical models to deduce the source of weak ferromagnetism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structurally well-ordered NiS$_2$ nanocrystals with an average diameter of $27.0 \pm 6.5$ nm retain the bulk-like two-step antiferromagnetic transitions, as shown by magnetization and heat-capacity measurements. Below the lower transition, the nanocrystals exhibit a hysteretic ferromagnetic response with large coercivity, exchange bias, and a vertical loop shift after field cooling, whereas the $M$-$H$ response just above the transition is nearly linear. These features are best explained by uncompensated surface moments generated where the low-temperature antiferromagnetic order terminates at the nanocrystal surface. The absence of a clear additional bulk-like weak-ferromagnetic component constrains homogeneous-canting models and indirectly favors a domain-wall scenario for the weak ferromagnetism of bulk NiS$_2$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper unifies the description of spontaneous Raman scattering by bridging quantum mechanics (KHD theory) and nonlinear optics ($\chi^{(3)}$). It rigorously derives the cross-section using vacuum field concepts, predicting both standard gain (sRG) and a novel loss mechanism (sRL). The ultimate achievement is demonstrating perfect analytical agreement between these two powerful theoretical descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical unification of two major frameworks—quantum electrodynamics and nonlinear optics—for a fundamental process like Raman scattering, offering rigorous predictions for vacuum-seeded optical phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a theoretical framework that fundamentally bridges quantum-mechanical and nonlinear-optical approaches for spontaneous Raman scattering.

**Main result.** The authors establish perfect analytical agreement between the two descriptions, predicting a novel phenomenon called "spontaneous Raman loss" (sRL).

**Method.** The study uses perturbation theory, comparing results derived from traditional Kramers-Heisenberg-Dirac (KHD) theory with those from nonlinear optical susceptibility ($\chi^{(3)}$) calculations involving vacuum field expectation values.

**Model / system.** The system is spontaneous Raman scattering, modeled using quantum electrodynamics formalism and considering molecular vibrational quanta interacting with incident and scattered photons. The analysis involves comparing Hilbert space (quantum mechanical) and Liouville space (nonlinear optical) descriptions.

**Key observables.** Spontaneous Raman cross-section, Spontaneous Raman Gain (sRG), Spontaneous Raman Loss (sRL), and the third-order nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi^{(3)}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Third-order nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi^{(3)}$), molecular density ($N$), vibrational frequency ($\omega_{vib}$), damping factor ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that unobservable terms (e.g., L5–L8, G5–G8) are physically prohibited in the spontaneous scattering regime; it also utilizes approximations like $\gamma 	o +0$ for comparison with KHD theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Feynman diagrams detailing sRL and sRG processes, and a table maps quantum mechanical terms to specific nonlinear susceptibility components, showing correspondence between different theoretical formalisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem using both KHD and nonlinear optics approaches. It then calculates sRG/sRL via vacuum field expectation values, establishes correspondences between KHD terms (T1-T4) and $\chi^{(3)}$ components ($G1-L4$), and finally generalizes this connection using a negative-frequency-assisted quantum mechanical framework to achieve perfect agreement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical framework for spontaneous Raman scattering that fundamentally bridges quantum-mechanical and nonlinear-optical approaches. By conceptualizing spontaneous Raman scattering as a stimulated Raman gain or loss event seeded by the quantum vacuum field, we rigorously derive the spontaneous Raman cross-section directly from the third-order nonlinear susceptibility. Crucially, this framework predicts the existence of a hitherto unrecognized phenomenon: "spontaneous Raman loss" (sRL), which acts as the vacuum-seeded counterpart to stimulated Raman loss, complementing traditional spontaneous Raman scattering (spontaneous Raman gain, sRG). Furthermore, we establish a rigorous connection to the traditional Kramers-Heisenberg-Dirac (KHD) theory, revealing that the spontaneous process is governed by interference before a detector between the signal field emitted from molecules and the vacuum field itself that stimulates the molecules. This insight uncovers a direct correspondence between the sRG susceptibility and the rotating/counter-rotating interference terms in the KHD formula. Ultimately, we extend the foundational KHD theory by incorporating previously unrecognized essential terms, achieving perfect analytical agreement between the quantum mechanical and nonlinear optical descriptions of Raman scattering.

</details>

### [Non-signaling assistance in prepare-and-measure scenarios with classical communication](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01193v1)

**Authors:** José Nogueira, Carlos Vieira, Lucas E. A. Porto, Lucas Pollyceno, Rafael Rabelo, Otfried Gühne  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.01193v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

Alice en- codes her input x into a preparation device, which emits either a classical or quantum system sent to Bob. Bob receives the system, chooses an input y for his measurement device, and produces an output b. The devices may share correlations, which can be clas- sical (shared randomness λ) or quantum (arising from a shared entangled state ρAB).</figcaption>

Schematic representation of a prepare-and-measure sce- nario with non-adaptive non-signaling assistance, according to the definition in Eq. (6). Alice and Bob share an arbitrary non- signaling box in the Bell scenario (nX, nA; nY , nβ). Alice inputs x into her share of the box, obtains outcome a, and communicates it to Bob. Bob independently inputs y into his share of the box and obtains β. Finally, he post-processes a,β to produce the output b.</figcaption>
</figure>

<img src="2607.01193_figures/2607.01193_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">

Alice and Bob share an arbitrary non-signaling box in the Bell sce- nario (nX, nA; nY nA, nB). Alice inputs x into her share of the box, obtains outcome a, and communicates it to Bob. Bob then inputs the composite setting (y, a) into his share of the box and obtains the output b. The protocol is adaptive because Bob’s input to the shared box depends on the message received from Alice.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper provides a detailed comparison between adaptive and non-adaptive Non-Signaling (NS) assistance in Prepare-and-Measure communication models. It establishes that while NS assistance can simulate quantum qudit transmission classically, any genuine advantage gained by adaptivity over non-adaptivity is restricted to highly specific scenarios. The findings help delineate the true power of different types of non-local correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work rigorously quantifies the limits of communication protocols using non-local correlations, providing fundamental boundaries for what can be achieved with limited resources like classical messages and NS assistance, which is crucial for understanding device-independent quantum information tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the operational advantage of using adaptive versus non-adaptive Non-Signaling (NS) assistance in Prepare-and-Measure (PM) quantum communication scenarios.

**Main result.** It proves that any genuine adaptive advantage over non-adaptive NS assistance can be traced back to scenarios where the receiver has no measurement choice, thus ruling out genuinely multi-setting advantages found in entanglement-assisted protocols.

**Method.** The authors characterize the sets of achievable correlations by deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on input-discrimination inequalities derived from structural properties of non-signaling boxes.

**Model / system.** The model is a Prepare-and-Measure (PM) scenario $(n_X; n_Y, n_B)$, where Alice prepares a message $a$ based on input $x$, and Bob measures using input $y$ to produce output $b$. The analysis compares correlations achievable with classical communication, non-adaptive NS assistance ($	ext{NSNA}$), and adaptive NS assistance ($	ext{NSA}$).

**Key observables.** The set of achievable correlation functions $q(b|x, y)$, and the maximum discrimination score $\max_{x \in [n_X]} q(b|x, y)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Message dimension ($n_A$), input sizes ($n_X, n_Y$), and output size ($n_B$). The comparison hinges on whether $n_A$ is large relative to these dimensions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on reducing the general PM scenario characterization to fixed-$y$ sub-behaviors (Theorem 3) and comparing structural constraints derived from NS boxes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the basic PM setup. Other figures detail the mathematical structures for non-adaptive and adaptive NS assistance, showing how inputs $x, y$ lead to an intermediate shared resource $\alphaeta$ or a message $a$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework of achievable correlations using various resources (classical, EA, NS). It then focuses on characterizing non-adaptive ($	ext{NSNA}$) and adaptive ($	ext{NSA}$) NS assistance separately. The core is comparing these two sets to pinpoint exactly when $	ext{NSA}$ provides a strict advantage over $	ext{NSNA}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extracting the full power of non-local correlations in prepare-and-measure (PM) scenarios requires precise control over the timing and structure of the receiver's measurements. Indeed, recent developments in entanglement-assisted classical communication scenarios have shown that adaptive strategies-where the receiver uses the transmitted message to guide their measurement choice-can outperform standard non-adaptive protocols. Moving beyond quantum theory, however, the ultimate limits of such advantages remain largely unexplored. In this work, we thoroughly study adaptive and non-adaptive non-signaling (NS) assistance in PM scenarios with classical communication. We provide simple characterizations of the sets of behaviors that can be realized using both non-adaptive and adaptive NS assistance in arbitrary PM scenarios. As a consequence, we show that non-adaptive NS assistance is already strong enough to reproduce quantum communication with the same message dimension: the transmission of a qudit can be simulated by a classical dit assisted non-adaptively by NS correlations. We then compare adaptive and non-adaptive NS assistance. We prove that any adaptive NS advantage can be traced back to scenarios in which the receiver has no measurement choice, ruling out the genuinely multi-setting advantages found in entanglement-assisted quantum protocols. Finally, we identify all PM scenarios where adaptive NS strategies provide a strict advantage over non-adaptive ones.

</details>

**Authors:** Phuoc Minh Vo, Thai Cong Ngoc Vu, Thien Ngoc Tran, Hoang Thanh Nguyen, Lan Nguyen Tran  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00575v1>

**Summary.** The paper improves the ADAPT-VQE algorithm, which is used for quantum chemistry simulations, by implementing an operator elimination strategy that prunes redundant terms from the ansatz pool. Furthermore, they enhance accuracy by coupling this method with a One-Body Downfolding Framework (OBDF) to incorporate dynamic correlation effects into the active space Hamiltonian. This combination significantly reduces required quantum resources while achieving energies closer to exact reference values.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource scaling in quantum simulation by providing a practical heuristic (operator elimination) to keep variational ansätze shallow while simultaneously improving accuracy via advanced embedding techniques (OBDF), making it highly relevant for near-term quantum hardware applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently computing ground-state electronic structure energies for quantum chemistry and condensed matter systems, which suffer from the exponential scaling of exact methods like FCI.

**Main result.** The combination of operator elimination in ADAPT-VQE with One-Body Downfolding (OBDF) yields energy estimates significantly closer to high-accuracy reference methods than standard ADAPT-VQE within the same active space.

**Method.** They propose an operator elimination scheme for ADAPT-VQE that removes non-contributing operators whose parameters vanish during optimization. This is combined with OBDF, which replaces the bare Hamiltonian in the active space with a correlated effective Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The work models molecular systems and materials using fermionic Hamiltonians. Benchmarking was performed on $\ce{H_6}$ chains, lattices, rings, and the $\ce{N_2}$ molecule, utilizing both Hartree-Fock (HF) and OBMP2 derived orbitals.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy ($E_0$), circuit depth (CNOT gate counts), iteration count, and gradient norm convergence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The elimination threshold ($\delta$) for vanishing parameters; the fraction of eliminated operators ($\phi$); and the active space definition derived from OBMP2.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The permanent elimination scheme is preferred over pool restoration because restoration introduces optimization instabilities. The use of OBDF assumes that dynamic correlation effects outside the active space can be effectively captured by a one-body approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow incorporating plateau-operator elimination into ADAPT-VQE, showing significant reductions in CNOT gate counts across $\ce{H_6}$ systems. Other figures compare energy convergence and Potential Energy Surfaces (PES) for various molecules ($\ce{N_2}$, $\ce{H_6}$) using different methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the limitations of standard VQE/ADAPT-VQE, then details the novel operator elimination technique to stabilize optimization and reduce circuit depth. Subsequently, it integrates this with the OBDF framework to incorporate external correlation effects, benchmarking performance across various molecular models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Adaptive Derivative-Assembled Problem-Tailored variational quantum eigensolvers (ADAPT-VQE) represent one of the most promising approaches for quantum chemistry on near-term quantum devices. However, their optimization is slow and may stall due to vanishing parameters and redundant operators in the ansatz. In this work, we propose a simple strategy of operator elimination that removes non-contributing operators from the pool once they are detected, enabling the optimization to continue progressing toward convergence. We examine two variants, with and without pool restoration after elimination, and find that the former converges more smoothly and faster than the latter and the standard ADAPT-VQE. To capture dynamical correlations between the active space and its environment, we combine ADAPT-VQE with our recently developed downfolding approach, the one-body downfolding framework (OBDF). In OBDF, the bare molecular Hamiltonian in the active space is replaced by a correlated effective Hamiltonian that incorporates dynamical correlation effects outside the active space. We benchmark our implementation on a linear \ce{H_6} chain, an \ce{H_6} lattice, an \ce{H_6} ring, and the \ce{N_2} molecule using the OpenFermion simulator. Our results show that operator elimination significantly reduces circuit depth and iteration count, and that OBDF-ADAPT-VQE yields energies closer to the full configuration interaction (FCI) reference than the standard approach within the same active space.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper compares Continuous-Variable (CV) and Discrete-Variable (DV) Quantum Neural Networks for classifying defects on semiconductor wafers. By keeping the feature extraction backbone constant, the authors demonstrate that CV-QNNs significantly outperform DV-QNNs, especially when capturing fine spatial details like Edge-Loc defects. The superior performance of CV is attributed to its continuous phase-space encoding and structured layer design.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, application-driven benchmark comparing two major QML approaches (CV vs DV) in a domain relevant to advanced hardware manufacturing, offering insights into the necessary structural properties for quantum feature extraction.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine which quantum computing paradigm, Continuous-Variable (CV) or Discrete-Variable (DV), is superior for classifying wafer-map defects in semiconductor yield assessment.

**Main result.** The CV head consistently outperforms the DV head, showing a significant advantage (e.g., 79.7% vs 61.6% accuracy at 4 qumodes/qubits) due to its structured design and continuous phase-space encoding.

**Method.** A controlled comparison was performed by attaching interchangeable quantum heads (CV-QNN or DV-QNN) to a shared, fixed convolutional backbone for feature extraction on the WM-811K dataset.

**Model / system.** The system is wafer-map defect classification using eight classes. The models compare CV-QNNs (using Gaussian/non-Gaussian gates) against DV-QNNs (using parameterized rotations and CNOT entanglers).

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy, Edge-Loc class recall (CV: 0.66 vs DV: <=0.05), and performance degradation under varying input noise ($\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit size (3, 4, 8 qumodes/qubits), Fock cutoff ($d=2$), and the classical baseline accuracy (85.0%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison isolates the quantum head as the sole variable; CV advantage is attributed to structure and continuous encoding rather than just Hilbert-space dimensionality at $d=2$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare model architectures, training dynamics (loss/accuracy vs. epoch), and performance robustness against increasing input noise ($\sigma$) for both CV and DV heads.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem context, details the controlled architectural comparison (shared CNN backbone feeding interchangeable quantum heads), presents quantitative results showing CV superiority on specific defect classes, and concludes by attributing this advantage to continuous encoding structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Realizing quantum neural networks (QNNs) in industry requires knowing which quantum computing paradigm suits which task. Motivated by AI accelerators and high-bandwidth memory, where die stacking makes wafer-level defect screening central to yield, we study WM-811K wafer-map defect classification (eight classes), comparing the dominant paradigms, continuous-variable (CV) and discrete-variable (DV), under controlled conditions. To isolate the quantum circuit as the sole variable, a shared convolutional backbone (~4.3M parameters) feeds interchangeable heads (classical dense, CV-QNN, or DV-QNN) as the only structural difference; each quantum head is scaled over three sizes (3, 4, 8 qumodes/qubits). The CV head consistently outperforms the DV head: at four qumodes/qubits it reaches 79.7 +/- 1.8% accuracy versus 61.6 +/- 1.4%, a non-overlapping 18-point gap. The advantage is sharpest on the spatially localized Edge-Loc class, easily confused with Scratch, which CV recovers with recall 0.66 +/- 0.06 while DV fails at every size (<=0.05), showing the structured CV layer better captures fine spatial distinctions between defect types. Training curves show the DV limitation is a representational-capacity ceiling, not an optimization failure; at the Fock cutoff used here (d = 2) the CV advantage reflects two intrinsic properties, a structured, neural-network-analogue layer and continuous phase-space encoding, not Hilbert-space dimensionality. On IBM hardware, DV accuracy holds at shallow depth, degrading only at the deepest circuit. Both quantum heads remain below the classical baseline (85.0%), but the controlled setting isolates where a structured head already helps and, as noise and scale improve, which paradigm can deliver practical advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces GsOQDC, an open-source GUI framework designed to simulate end-to-end operations in Optical Quantum Data Centers (OQDCs). It integrates quantum circuit design, network topology definition, compilation, and scheduling into a single platform. This allows researchers to quantitatively evaluate how physical network impairments affect the performance of distributed quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a crucial simulation tool for understanding the practical architectural limitations of scaling quantum computation by explicitly coupling quantum circuit requirements with realistic optical network constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** There is a lack of an integrated environment to evaluate the end-to-end behavior of distributed quantum applications, especially concerning the interaction between quantum computation and optical network resources in Quantum Data Centers (QDCs).

**Main result.** The GsOQDC framework enables quantitative analysis of how optical network characteristics influence application-level performance metrics like fidelity, overhead, and job execution time in future Optical Quantum Data Centers.

**Method.** It utilizes a graphical, open-source framework integrating quantum circuit modeling, optically switched interconnection design, distributed compilation, scheduling, and a Discrete-Event Simulation (DES) engine.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an Optical Quantum Data Center (OQDC), consisting of multiple interconnected Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) linked by optical networks. The framework simulates the entire workflow from quantum circuit definition to final execution.

**Key observables.** End-to-end performance metrics including entanglement-resource dynamics, fidelity, communication overhead, entanglement-resource utilization, and Job Execution Time (JET).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hardware noise parameters such as qubit properties, gate errors, coherence times, readout errors, and network latency/impairments.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the ability to model cross-layer interactions between quantum computation layers and optical networking layers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall GsOQDC workflow architecture (Circuit $ightarrow$ Network $ightarrow$ Compile $ightarrow$ Schedule $ightarrow$ Run). Figure 2 details the network designer interface for configuring QDC topologies. Figure 3 shows interactive Gantt charts visualizing distributed workload scheduling.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces GsOQDC, detailing its architecture which unifies circuit modeling, optical networking design (via a GUI), compilation, scheduling, and execution simulation using DES/Monte Carlo methods to evaluate cross-layer performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present GsOQDC, an open-source graphical framework integrating optical-network design, distributed quantum-circuit compilation, scheduling, and DES-based remote-gate simulation, enabling end-to-end cross-layer evaluation of entanglement-resource dynamics and system-level performance in Optical Quantum Data Centers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical issue of atmospheric turbulence degrading Quantum Illumination's advantage in free-space links. The authors introduce a novel physics-driven framework that uses Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks to predict turbulence strength directly from routine meteorological observations. This allows for the dynamic, all-weather quantification of quantum performance, moving QI systems closer to operational reality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the practical deployment challenge of quantum optics by providing a robust, predictive model for environmental noise sources (turbulence), which is crucial for developing real-world quantum sensing networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is quantifying the degradation of quantum advantage provided by Quantum Illumination (QI) when operating through atmospheric turbulence.

**Main result.** The authors propose an end-to-end framework that uses deep learning to map macroscopic weather data directly to the temporal evolution of quantum advantage, enabling all-weather operation for quantum radar.

**Method.** They integrate a physics-driven approach using Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KAN) trained on meteorological data to predict turbulence parameters ($C_n^2$), which are then fed into the QI channel model.

**Model / system.** The system models Quantum Illumination using a two-mode squeezed vacuum state in free-space channels affected by atmospheric turbulence. The framework bridges macroscopic meteorology (T, P, GHI, etc.) to microscopic quantum channel dynamics via KANs.

**Key observables.** Quantum advantage ($\log_{10} R$), average error probability ($ar{P}_{	ext{err}}$), and the ratio $R$ comparing classical vs. quantum error probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Meteorological variables (GHI, $L_\downarrow$, Dew Depression), turbulence structure constant ($C_n^2$), and specific thermodynamic regimes (e.g., twilight, nocturnal boundary layer).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes a physically consistent mapping from standard meteorological variables to quantum channel dynamics, bypassing the need for direct, costly measurements of $C_n^2$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how QI advantage is modulated by GHI (peaking at twilight), collapses under extreme solar forcing or strong mechanical shear in nocturnal conditions, and show the dependence on specific thermodynamic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem of turbulence degradation for QI. It then details a multi-step methodology: 1) Using MOST/thermodynamics to derive atmospheric parameters; 2) Employing KANs to map macro-weather data to $C_n^2$; and 3) Integrating this prediction into the quantum error probability calculation to quantify advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum illumination offers a significant theoretical advantage for target detection in high background noise environments. However, its practical deployment in free-space channels is hindered by atmospheric turbulence. Stochastic fluctuations in atmospheric turbulence inevitably degrade the quantum signature, rendering the real-time evaluation of quantum advantage under such dynamic conditions a critical yet unresolved challenge. To circumvent the reliance on costly direct turbulence measurements, we propose a physics-driven framework that integrates Kolmogorov-Arnold networks directly bridge macroscopic meteorological observations with microscopic quantum channel dynamics. Trained on 105,120 samples from 12 climatically diverse sites and validated on 26,280 unseen samples from three extreme boundary conditions (arid continental, tropical maritime, high-altitude plateau), our approach establishes a physically consistent mapping from standard meteorological variables to the temporal evolution of the quantum advantage. This end-to-end system dynamically quantifies the degradation of quantum advantage across diverse turbulence conditions. Our results provide a rigorous theoretical and data-driven pathway for environmental adaptation, facilitating the transition of quantum radar networks from proof-of-principle demonstrations to all-weather operational systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous mathematical framework for Geometric Quantum Machine Learning (GQML) applied to graph data encoded in qubits. It characterizes the necessary equivariant quantum circuits by leveraging group theory symmetries ($S_n$) to build a 'toolbox' of model components. The resulting models are shown to naturally integrate with classical ML and benefit from pre-training strategies, advancing the field toward practical quantum computation for structured data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical grounding for designing physically realizable quantum circuits for graph data. For AMO physicists, the focus on symmetries ($S_n$) suggests potential connections to physical Hamiltonians or measurement bases that naturally respect permutation symmetry, which is crucial when modeling interacting many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a rigorous framework for Geometric Quantum Machine Learning (GQML) models applied to graph problems, addressing the lack of a unifying design perspective compared to classical GML.

**Main result.** The authors provide a comprehensive 'toolbox' based on characterizing equivariant maps under the permutation group $S_n$, enabling the construction and analysis of quantum graph models with proven benefits like classical pre-training compatibility.

**Method.** The work relies heavily on abstract algebraic techniques to characterize symmetry constraints (equivariance/invariance) for linear and observable maps acting on quantum state spaces defined by $n$-qubit systems, utilizing symmetrization procedures.

**Model / system.** The system involves encoding $n$-node graphs into $n$-qubit states. The models are parameterized quantum circuits whose structure must respect the symmetries of the graph (i.e., be equivariant with respect to permutations $\sigma \in S_n$).

**Key observables.** Symmetric and equivariant observables, expectation values derived from specific Pauli string combinations ($\vec{k}$), and classification metrics like Bell numbers or tetrahedral numbers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of nodes ($n$), the depth of the quantum circuit ($L$), and the parameters $	heta$ governing the unitary evolution $U_	heta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that restricting to specific subspaces (like those spanned by Pauli strings involving only I and Z) is sufficient for practical implementation, and it treats expectation values as exact rather than accounting for shot noise.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall toolbox integrating classical pre-training and feature augmentation. Figure 4 compares the performance variance of pre-trained vs. randomly initialized models on graph classification tasks across varying system sizes $n$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically: first, establishing the need for GQML by comparing it to GML; second, developing the mathematical machinery (symmetrization) to characterize equivariant maps in quantum operator spaces ($	ext{Hom}_{S_n}(V, W)$); third, applying this framework to define concrete model architectures (invariant/equivariant expectation values); and finally, demonstrating practical benefits through pre-training studies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Geometric Machine Learning (GML) successes have been achieved through the thorough study and design of new equivariant neural networks. In comparison, geometric quantum machine learning (GQML) models lack such a detailed understanding and, despite already several proposals, a unifying perspective on their design remains elusive. In this work, we focus on GQML models for graph problems that showcase a lot of structure and still remain frontier in machine learning. For the case when n-node graphs are encoded in n-qubit states, we provide a comprehensive characterization of their constituents. Taken together, these furnish us with a toolbox for the design of quantum graph models, and we further probe its benefits including the natural integration with classical models, generalization of known GQML models (sometimes extending their expressivity at virtually no cost), and straightforward classical pre-training strategies. The latter two features are demonstrated in dedicated numerical experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes an exponential advantage for Quantum Finite Automata over classical automata by solving a problem rooted in contextuality. This advantage is quantified by the gap between the graph's chromatic number ($\chi$) and its orthogonal rank ($\xi$). Crucially, this resource separation remains robust even when subjected to realistic operational noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete computational realization of quantum resource theory, showing that structural incompatibilities between classical deterministic models (NCHVMs) and quantum mechanics can lead to provable, noise-resilient advantages in memory complexity for computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to demonstrate an exponential, noise-resilient memory advantage for quantum finite automata (QFAs) over classical deterministic finite automata (DFAs) by exploiting graph-theoretic properties of contextuality.

**Main result.** An exponential separation is achieved in memory cost ($d=O(n)$ vs. $N=2^{\Omega(n)}$) when solving a promise problem derived from the Kochen-Specker Problem on Boolean-orthogonality graphs, and this advantage is robust to operational noise.

**Method.** The analysis uses graph theory invariants ($\chi(G)$, $\xi(G)$) applied to an exclusivity graph $G$ to define a computational task. The quantum solution utilizes QFAs operating via Householder reflections, while the classical lower bound relies on the chromatic number.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Quantum Finite Automaton (QFA) solving a promise problem defined by an exclusivity graph $G$. The underlying structure relates to the Kochen-Specker Problem and involves comparing the quantum memory dimension ($\xi(G)+1$) against the classical state requirement ($\chi(G)$).

**Key observables.** Chromatic number $\chi(G)$, orthogonal rank $\xi(G)$, Quantum Memory Dimension $d$, Classical State Requirement $N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponential separation scaling ($d=O(n)$ vs. $N=2^{\Omega(n)}$) for Boolean-orthogonality graphs, and the noise resilience threshold ($\mathcal{O}(1)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The advantage relies on 'representational contextuality,' defined by the structural gap $\chi(G) > \xi(G)$, which is distinguished from statistical contextuality (SIC).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate graph structures like $\Omega_4$ and general graphs showing the relationship between $\chi$, $\xi$, and $\chi_f$. One figure compares linear quantum scaling to exponential classical lower bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the computational problem (KSP on $G$), derives the classical lower bound using $\chi(G)$, proposes the QFA solution bounded by $\xi(G)+1$, proves the exponential separation, and finally distinguishes this 'representational' advantage from other forms of contextuality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum contextuality is widely recognized as an essential non-classical resource underlying quantum technology, yet illuminating the precise mechanisms through which it translates into unconditional computational advantages remains an ongoing challenge. We demonstrate an exponential, noise-resilient memory advantage for quantum finite automata arising from graph-theoretic approaches to contextuality. We define a promise problem on an exclusivity graph $G$ for which any classical deterministic automaton acts as a non-contextual hidden variable model requiring at least $N=χ(G)$ states, where $χ(G)$ is the graph's chromatic number. In contrast, by exploiting a structural phenomenon we term \textit{representational contextuality}, a QFA solves this task using a memory of dimension at most $d=ξ(G)+1$, where $ξ(G)$ is the graph's orthogonal rank. This separation scales exponentially ($d=\mathcal O(n)$ versus $N=2^{Ω(n)}$) for Boolean-orthogonality graphs. Crucially, this memory advantage maintains an $\mathcal{O}(1)$ threshold against both depolarizing and coherent noise.

</details>
