**Summary.** This paper unifies the study of scattering and bound states for the 1D Dirac equation by mapping it onto an effective Schrödinger-like problem. Using Supersymmetric Quantum Mechanics and shape invariance, the authors derive closed-form solutions for transmission probabilities. The analysis reveals that resonances manifest as predictable pole structures in the complex momentum plane.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful analytical tools (SUSYQM) to analyze relativistic quantum systems, connecting continuum physics (scattering) directly to discrete spectra (bound states/resonances) within a single framework, which is valuable for modeling particle interactions in condensed matter or high-energy contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified theoretical framework to analyze both scattering phenomena (transmission/reflection) and discrete bound states for the one-dimensional Dirac equation.

**Main result.** It successfully maps the relativistic problem onto an effective Schrödinger-like equation, allowing the use of SUSYQM techniques to derive closed-form expressions for transmission probabilities and characterize resonance poles in the complex momentum plane.

**Method.** The core methods involve exploiting spin/pseudospin symmetries to reduce the Dirac system, applying Supersymmetric Quantum Mechanics (SUSYQM) with shape invariance, and analyzing pole structures via analytic continuation of scattering amplitudes.

**Model / system.** The model is a 1D relativistic fermion governed by the Dirac equation subjected to combined scalar and vector potentials. The analysis focuses on specific solvable forms, notably the hyperbolic Pöschl-Teller potential.

**Key observables.** Transmission probability $T(E)$, reflection amplitude $R(E)$, bound-state energies (poles of $T(k)$), and resonance/quasi-normal mode frequencies (poles in the complex momentum plane).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The energy $E$, coupling constants defining the potentials ($V_0$), and parameters related to symmetry breaking ($\Delta, \Sigma$). The regime is controlled by a discriminant $D(E)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assuming spin-symmetry or pseudospin-symmetry to reduce the coupled system to a single second-order equation. For scattering, short-range interactions are assumed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy dependence of transmission coefficients for attractive potentials (bound states) and map out the pole structure in the complex momentum plane, distinguishing between high-barrier resonance regimes and low-barrier virtual state regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the general Hamiltonian, exploiting symmetry to reduce it to a Sturm-Liouville form. It then applies SUSYQM/shape invariance to solve for scattering amplitudes, finally using pole analysis in the complex plane to characterize bound states and resonances.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a unified treatment of scattering and discrete spectra for the one-dimensional Dirac equation with scalar and vector interactions. Under the spin-symmetry condition, the coupled first-order Dirac system maps exactly onto an effective Sturm--Liouville (Schrö\-din\-ger-like) problem for a single spinor component. This mapping provides a convenient framework for analyzing transmission, reflection, and analytic continuation. As an explicit application, we consider effective interactions of hyperbolic Pöschl--Teller type and exploit supersymmetric quantum mechanics and shape invariance to obtain a closed-form expression for the transmission probability. The bound-state spectrum is then recovered from the poles of the analytically continued transmission amplitude, reproducing known results and offering a unified description of scattering and bound states. For the barrier configuration, we briefly comment on the resulting pole pattern in the complex momentum plane and its connection with resonance and quasi-normal-mode behavior. Moreover, we use the chiral transformation to relate the spin- and pseudospin-symmetry sectors and translate results between them without repeating the full derivation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical challenge of efficiently scheduling quantum circuits across multiple interconnected quantum cores. By proposing a dependency-aware greedy algorithm over a simple layer-by-layer approach, the authors demonstrate significant speedups by exploiting fine-grained parallelism and better managing communication latency. This work provides crucial insights for optimizing future multi-core quantum hardware implementations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical scheduling algorithms, the explicit modeling of resource constraints (qubit availability, entanglement links) and communication overhead is highly relevant for understanding practical limitations in building large-scale quantum simulators or hardware control stacks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to minimize the overall execution time (makespan) when running quantum circuits on multi-core quantum computing architectures.

**Main result.** A dependency-aware greedy scheduling strategy was shown to achieve an average 40% reduction in makespan and improve core utilization compared to baseline layered scheduling on real benchmarks.

**Method.** The authors compare a simple, sequential 'layered scheduling' approach against a proposed fine-grained, dependency-aware greedy heuristic that schedules gates as soon as all prerequisites and resources are available.

**Model / system.** The model is a multi-core quantum system structured on an n x n 2D mesh topology. Inter-core operations require quantum teleportation due to the no-cloning theorem, introducing significant communication latency.

**Key observables.** Makespan (T_total), Core Utilization, and Communication Overlap Ratio (R_comm).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system size is defined by the mesh topology ($n 	imes n$) and the number of qubits per core ($Q_i$). Inter-core communication delay ($	ext{com time}$) is a critical parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes deterministic teleportation with successful entanglement generation, and that inter-core communication latency is orders of magnitude higher than local gate execution latency.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the multi-core architecture (NoC), compare makespans between layered vs. greedy scheduling on case studies, and show performance metrics across varying mesh sizes using synthetic and real benchmarks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model of a multi-core quantum processor. It then introduces two scheduling methods: baseline layered scheduling and the proposed dependency-aware greedy heuristic. The core of the work involves rigorous evaluation against various benchmarks to quantify performance gains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-core quantum computing architectures have emerged as a promising solution to the qubit scalability limitations of monolithic NISQ devices. Quantum algorithms are expressed as quantum circuits composed of single- and two-qubit gates. However, circuit scheduling in multi-core quantum systems remains largely unexplored. Reducing overall execution time (makespan), increasing core utilization, and hiding communication latency behind computation depends on effective scheduling. In this paper, we first introduce a layered scheduling approach as a baseline where quantum gates within the same layer are executed in parallel, while layers themselves are executed sequentially. We then propose a greedy scheduling strategy which schedules each gate as soon as all its dependencies and required resources are available. This allows fine-grained parallelism across cores. Our evaluation shows that on real benchmarks, greedy scheduling achieves an average 40% reduction in makespan and improvement in core utilization. The results suggest that the use of intelligent circuit scheduling to exploit parallelism can greatly enhance the speed of circuit execution in multi-core quantum architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QPipe, an LLM-based multi-agent system designed to automatically generate executable quantum applications from natural language requirements for test optimization. By coordinating specialized agents through a structured workflow, it successfully translates abstract goals into concrete quantum circuits that solve real-world benchmarks. This demonstrates the potential of agentic AI to bridge the significant expertise gap in applying quantum computing to practical software engineering problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on SE tooling, the underlying challenge of translating high-level physical or computational goals into precise quantum circuit implementations is relevant to designing control protocols for complex quantum simulations or hybrid quantum-classical algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is autonomously translating high-level, natural-language (NL) requirements for Software Engineering (SE) optimization into executable quantum applications.

**Main result.** The proposed agentic system, QPipe, successfully generates these complex quantum applications with high success rates and generated solutions that often outperform classical baselines on test optimization benchmarks.

**Method.** It employs a multi-agent LLM architecture (QPipe) that decomposes the task into specialized stages: parsing, encoding (mapping to QUBO/Ising), code generation, review, and verification.

**Model / system.** The system operates on quantum computing principles, formulating optimization problems as QUBO or Ising models ($\min x^	op Qx$) suitable for execution via algorithms like QAOA on simulated quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** Success rates (e.g., 100% compilation pass rate), solution quality measured by the Quantum Resource Ratio (QRR) compared to classical solvers, and generation costs (time/tokens).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit budget constraints are critical, forcing the Encoding Agent to decompose large problems into smaller, manageable subproblems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system assumes that stable formulation patterns (QUBO/QAOA) exist for TCO tasks and that agentic coordination can reliably bridge the gap between abstract language and executable quantum code.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall QPipe workflow, show ablation results proving the necessity of specific agents (like review), and compare solution quality metrics (QRR) against baselines.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the multi-agent architecture (QPipe), describes the structured workflow involving specialized agents for each step (Parse $ightarrow$ Encode $ightarrow$ CodeGen $\leftrightarrow$ Review $ightarrow$ Verify), and concludes with empirical evaluations across multiple benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing is increasingly explored for software engineering (SE) optimization, but translating natural-language (NL) task-level requirements into executable quantum applications still demands substantial quantum and programming expertise. We present QPipe, a large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent architecture that autonomously turns NL requirements into traceable quantum-application workflows through specialized agents for requirement parsing, formulation, code generation, review, execution, and verification. We evaluate QPipe on 20 NL requirements, each associated with a real-world benchmark and a test-optimization problem. QPipe successfully completes the key stages of quantum-application generation across requirements, achieving average rates of 100% for code compilation and 96.7% for application execution and final-result combination, with average generation costs of 260.1 seconds and 1.89M tokens per requirement. Among the generated quantum applications that execute successfully, the returned solutions outperform the offline genetic algorithm baseline in most cases. Ablation results further show that QPipe's advantage depends on retaining code-generation skills, task knowledge, review feedback, and multi-agent decomposition. These results indicate that agentic coordination can support generation of executable quantum applications for tackling test optimization problems from real-world benchmarks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses dHvA measurements on $\mathrm{Sr}_2\mathrm{RuO}_4$ to map how uniaxial strain modifies its electronic structure. By observing changes in Fermi surface areas and effective masses, the authors demonstrate that stress induces charge transfer between orbital sheets, driving the system toward a Lifshitz transition. This establishes quantum oscillation measurements under mechanical stress as a powerful new experimental route for probing correlated electron physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology—using quantum oscillations under controlled strain combined with first-principles theory—is highly valuable for understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated systems where external tuning parameters (like stress) can drive topological phase transitions or alter superconducting pairing mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how uniaxial stress affects the electronic structure and Fermi surface of the unconventional superconductor $\mathrm{Sr}_2\mathrm{RuO}_4$, specifically addressing discrepancies between predicted density of states enhancement and observed $T_c$ suppression.

**Main result.** Under c-axis compression, charge transfer occurs from hole-like to electron-like sheets ($\alpha 	o \gamma$), driving the system toward a Lifshitz transition. The measurements confirm that quantum oscillation data, combined with band structure calculations, provide a powerful new tool for studying quantum materials.

**Method.** The research combines De Haas-van Alphen (dHvA) quantum oscillation measurements under uniaxial stress ($\sigma_{zz}$) with theoretical predictions from Density Functional Theory (DFT) and magnetostriction analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is the correlated electron material $\mathrm{Sr}_2\mathrm{RuO}_4$, an unconventional superconductor. The study probes its electronic structure evolution by applying controlled uniaxial strain/stress along the c-axis, which modifies the Fermi surface topology and carrier distribution among different orbital sheets ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$).

**Key observables.** dHvA frequencies (cross-sectional areas of $\alpha, eta, \gamma$ sheets), effective masses ($m^*$), critical stress for Lifshitz transition ($\sigma_{	ext{crit},c}$), and the superconducting critical temperature $T_c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial stress up to $-1.8$ GPa (or higher in some analyses); Fermi surface sheets $\alpha, eta, \gamma$; Temperature dependence of quantum oscillation amplitudes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed changes are due to intrinsic electronic structure evolution rather than experimental artifacts like strain inhomogeneity or temperature gradients; DFT calculations provide a reliable baseline for band structure predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show raw dHvA oscillations and Fourier spectra demonstrating frequency shifts under stress. Comparisons are made between these experimental results, magnetostriction measurements, and DFT-calculated frequency shifts to validate the physical mechanism of charge transfer and topology change.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for studying electronic structure evolution under strain in correlated materials. It presents dHvA measurements showing clear quantum oscillations up to high stresses. The core analysis details how Fermi surface areas change (charge transfer) and how effective masses are modified, comparing these findings rigorously against DFT calculations to pinpoint the approach toward a Lifshitz transition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Uniaxial stress has now been widely used to study correlated electron materials. However, Fermi surface-resolved experimental data on the evolution of the electronic structure under piezoelectrically applied stress are sparse, with no reports of de Haas-van Alphen (dHvA) effects under uniaxial stress. Here we present dHvA measurements under $c$-axis uniaxial stress on the unconventional superconductor $\mathrm{Sr}_2\mathrm{RuO}_4$. This allows us to study the evolution of the electronic structure directly and to gain insight into the contradicting behavior of the predicted enhancement of the electronic density of states and the observed suppression of $T_\text{c}$. We are able to follow all Fermi surfaces for stress up to $-1.8$~GPa and find that the cross-sectional areas of the hole-like $α$ sheet increase and electron-like $β$ sheet decrease. At the same time, the area of the electron-like $γ$ sheet increases. Therefore, in contrast to in-plane uniaxial stress, charge transfer is the mechanism for approaching the electron-to-hole Lifshitz transition and the associated Van Hove singularity. Additionally, we find that the effective masses on all three Fermi sheets are slightly enhanced as the Lifshitz transition is approached. We compare the dHvA results with quantum oscillations in the magnetostriction and band structure calculations, and find good agreement. At a more general level, our findings show that quantum oscillation measurements under uniaxial stress, combined with band-structure calculations, offer a promising new route for studying quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally investigates the superconducting state in the topological semimetal YPtBi, which features j=3/2 quasiparticle states. By testing samples with varying levels of disorder and doping, the authors demonstrate that $T_c$ is highly resilient. This resilience strongly suggests that superconductivity in this material is governed by phase stiffness rather than conventional pair-breaking mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observed robustness of superconductivity in a high-spin system suggests exotic pairing mechanisms that are insensitive to local perturbations, providing an experimental playground for theories beyond standard BCS theory and potentially linking to quantum criticality or topological protection.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the robustness of exotic j=3/2 superconductivity in the topological semimetal YPtBi against significant perturbations like magnetic and nonmagnetic disorder, and changes in carrier density.

**Main result.** The superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) remains remarkably robust across large variations in disorder and doping levels, suggesting that the transition is governed by phase stiffness rather than conventional pair formation mechanisms.

**Method.** The study combines electrical transport measurements (resistivity, Hall effect) with quantum oscillation analysis (Shubnikov de Haas oscillations) on various doped and disordered samples of YPtBi.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the topological semimetal YPtBi, characterized by strong spin-orbit coupling leading to a manifold of j=3/2 quasiparticle states near the Fermi level where superconductivity occurs. The analysis compares results against theoretical expectations from BCS theory and phase stiffness models.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), residual resistivity ratio (RRR), quantum oscillation frequency ($F$) and effective mass ($m^*$), and carrier density derived from the Hall coefficient.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder levels, carrier densities spanning orders of magnitude, and the high angular momentum state j=3/2.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The robustness suggests a mechanism where phase coherence (phase stiffness) dictates $T_c$, rather than simple pair amplitude formation, challenging standard BCS predictions for disorder effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show normalized resistivity and Hall resistivity across different sample types; quantum oscillation analysis confirms changes in Fermi surface parameters ($F, m^*$) upon doping/purity changes; a key figure summarizes the constant $T_c$ despite large variations in disorder and carrier density.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the material's exotic superconducting nature (j=3/2) and then systematically tests its stability by varying disorder and doping, using quantum oscillations to probe normal state changes while monitoring $T_c$. The conclusion pivots on interpreting this resilience as evidence for phase stiffness control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cooper pairing in most of the known fermionic superfluids occurs via spin-1/2 quasiparticle interactions that lead to spin-singlet or spin-triplet pairing. In the topological semimetal YPtBi, strong spin-orbit coupling results in a band inversion between highly symmetric $s$- and $p$-like electronic bands and a degeneracy at the $Γ$ point that ensures the manifold of $j$=3/2 quasiparticle states thrive near the Fermi level, where superconducting pairing occurs. Here we study the effects of magnetic and nonmagnetic disorder and carrier density on this exotic superconducting pairing state. By varying levels of disorder and carrier densities by nearly two and three orders of magnitude, respectively, we show that the superconducting critical temperature of YPtBi has a remarkable robustness, with little variation across this span. Our results suggest that superconductivity in YPtBi may reside in a regime where phase stiffness, rather than pair formation, governs the transition temperature. The insensitivity of Cooper pairing to dramatic changes in quasiparticle environment in a $j$=3/2 superconductor highlights a new form of protection realized in topological high-spin superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the structure of the dynamical Lie algebra governing QAOA-MaxCut on complete graphs. By employing advanced techniques like Schur-Weyl duality, the authors prove that the variance of the loss function scales linearly with system size. This result definitively shows that barren plateaus are absent for this specific quantum optimization problem.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical proof regarding the complexity landscape of quantum optimization algorithms, directly addressing the critical issue of barren plateaus which limits current NISQ device applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the dynamical Lie algebra associated with the QAOA-MaxCut problem on complete graphs and determines if the system suffers from barren plateaus.

**Main result.** It proves that the variance of the loss function scales linearly ($\Theta(n)$) with the number of qubits, thereby confirming that barren plateaus do not occur for this specific quantum optimization problem.

**Method.** The analysis uses Dynamical Lie Algebras (DLAs), Schur-Weyl duality to decompose operators in $u(2n)_{S_n}$, and commutator relations to establish structural inclusions within the algebra.

**Model / system.** The system is QAOA applied to finding the MaxCut on a complete graph ($K_n$) using $n$ qubits. The mathematical framework revolves around analyzing the generators of the associated Lie algebra, $\{iX, iZ^2\}$.

**Key observables.** Loss function variance ($	ext{Var}_	heta[\ell(ho, O; 	heta)]$), and the structure/basis elements of the dynamical Lie algebra $[\mathfrak{g}, \mathfrak{g}]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$); indices $j$ related to irreducible representations (spins).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on establishing specific reverse inclusions in the structure of the commutator subspace, and for variance calculation, it assumes the circuit is deep enough to form a unitary 2-design.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the focus is on mathematical theorems (Theorem 1.2, Theorem 1.3) and structural proofs (Lemma 3.8, Lemma 3.9).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the DLA for QAOA-MaxCut, then using Schur-Weyl duality to decompose the algebra structure ($\mathfrak{g} \sim c \oplus [g, g]$). It rigorously proves that $[g_{K_n}, g_{K_n}]$ contains specific subspaces related to $\mathfrak{su}(W^{(j)}_{	ext{even}}) \oplus \mathfrak{su}(W^{(j)}_{	ext{odd}})$, culminating in the variance scaling result.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an analytical expression for the dynamical Lie algebra corresponding to the QAOA-MaxCut problem on complete graphs, and show that the variance of the associated loss function scales linearly in the number of qubits. This solves an open problem from [ASYZ26] and confirms that such systems do not exhibit barren plateaus. The proof is based on projecting the dynamical Lie algebra generators onto subspaces given by the Schur-Weyl duality between irreducible representations of the unitary and symmetric groups.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers measured the real-time charge and spin dynamics in a double quantum dot driven by nonequilibrium phonons. They found that at triplet resonance, the phonon environment induces strong rectification, leading to a measurable current via unidirectional electron transport cycles. This demonstrates how non-equilibrium driving can generate directed quantum currents in solid-state devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct experimental realization of non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, showing how external energy baths (phonons) can induce directed, non-reciprocal transport cycles in mesoscopic solid-state systems, which is highly relevant to open quantum system physics and energy harvesting concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates real-time charge and spin dynamics in excited states of a double quantum dot (DQD) driven by nonequilibrium phonons to understand emergent functionalities beyond equilibrium.

**Main result.** The system exhibits strong, measurable rectification ($\eta \approx 1$) leading to unidirectional electron transport cycles when driven by the phonon gradient at triplet resonance conditions.

**Method.** Time-resolved charge sensing and rate analysis are used to track inter-dot tunneling events under controlled nonequilibrium phonon irradiation.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a semiconductor DQD fabricated on an AlGaAs/GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure, coupled with a nearby phonon source QD. Dynamics are analyzed using spin states (Singlet/Triplet) and rate equations derived from time-resolved measurements.

**Key observables.** Inter-dot resonant tunneling rates ($\Gamma$), waiting-time histograms, current ($I \sim 10^{-19}$ A), and rectification parameter ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature ($\approx 20$ mK); detuning voltage ($V_R$); phonon bias voltage ($V_{PS}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are governed by the coupling between electronic states and the nonequilibrium phonon bath, allowing for rate-equation descriptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show charge stability diagrams revealing new triplet resonances (Fig. 1c); time traces illustrate resonant tunneling at both singlet and triplet conditions (Fig. 2a/b); waiting-time histograms compare rates across resonance types (Fig. 3); and later figures detail the unidirectional current generation and rectification ($\eta$) in weak coupling regimes (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the experimental setup and observing basic resonant tunneling at singlet/triplet resonances, to quantifying dwell times using waiting-time statistics, and finally demonstrating nonreciprocal transport by analyzing current generation and rectification ($\eta$) in the weak coupling limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven nonequilibrium systems can host emergent functionalities beyond equilibrium, but real-time access to excited-state dynamics remains limited. Here we report real-time measurements of phonon-driven charge and spin dynamics in excited states of a double quantum dot. Under phonon irradiation, resonant inter-dot tunneling emerges at triplet resonance. Time-resolved charge sensing reveals that the resonant inter-dot tunneling is strongly modified by spin blockade. For weaker inter-dot coupling, the nonequilibrium phonon environment generates a unidirectional transport cycle along the phonon density gradient.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory for the Spin Hall effect in Dirac electron systems by analyzing how spin and magnetic moment accumulation coefficients scale with electric conductivity. Using advanced Green's function techniques, the authors identify three distinct transport regimes—superclean, moderately dirty, and dirty—and establish a unified framework that differs from previous anomalous Hall effect scaling laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the rigorous development of scaling laws and the need to define fundamental quantities (like spin current) in different scattering limits provides general theoretical tools applicable to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the scaling behavior of the Spin Hall (SH) effect for Dirac electrons, aiming to construct a unified theory that correctly defines spin current.

**Main result.** Three distinct transport regimes are found based on electric conductivity ($\sigma_{yy}$): superclean ($\propto \sigma_{yy}$), moderately dirty (constant), and dirty ($\propto (\sigma_{yy})^{0.6}$).

**Method.** The analysis employs linear response theory using Keldysh Green's functions and the self-consistent T-matrix approximation to account for short-range nonmagnetic impurities.

**Model / system.** The model is based on Dirac electrons, described by an anisotropic Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}(\mathbf{k})$), applied to systems like bismuth. Impurity scattering is treated via the T-matrix formalism.

**Key observables.** Spin Accumulation Coefficient (SAC) and Magnetic-Moment Accumulation Coefficient (MMAC); Spin Hall Conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}^{sz}$) and MMH conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}^{mz}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric conductivity ($\sigma_{yy}$), Fermi energy ($\epsilon_F$), impurity concentration, temperature ($T=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the T-matrix approximation for impurities and involves deriving transport coefficients using vertex corrections (VCs) in the linear response formalism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Density of States (DOS), Fermi-energy dependence of SAC/MMAC, and crucially, the scaling relationship between SAC/MMAC and $\sigma_{yy}$ across the three distinct scattering regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical formulas for various transport coefficients using Green's functions in the T-matrix approximation. It then analyzes how these coefficients scale with conductivity ($\sigma_{yy}$) by identifying three distinct physical regimes, culminating in a unified theory framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spin Hall (SH) effect for the Dirac electrons in terms of the spin and magnetic-moment accumulation coefficients $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy}$. We take short-range nonmagnetic impurities into account within the self-consistent $T$-matrix approximation. Similarly to the universal scaling for the anomalous Hall (AH) effect, we find three disctinct regimes by changing the electric conductivity $σ^{yy}$; the superclean regime with $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy} \propto σ^{yy}$ owing to the skew scattering, moderately dirty regime with almost constant $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy}$, and dirty regime with a new scaling relation $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy} \propto (σ^{yy})^{0.6}$ whose exponent differs from that of the AH conductivity. Our results construct a unified theory of the SH effect without any ambiguity of spin current.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that coating native oxide layers on superconducting Niobium films with Platinum and annealing them creates a stable $	ext{Nb-Pt}$ alloy surface. This alloying process effectively passivates the high-loss oxide interfaces, thereby offering a materials pathway to improve coherence in superconducting transmon qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses material science limitations in superconducting qubits by providing a chemically robust interface engineering solution, which is crucial for scaling up quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Dielectric loss from two-level systems (TLS) at surfaces and interfaces limits coherence in superconducting transmon qubits built on Niobium (Nb).

**Main result.** A thin Pt encapsulation layer, when annealed, forms a chemically stable metallic Nb-Pt alloy surface that suppresses further oxide growth and maintains high superconducting performance.

**Method.** The study combines advanced characterization techniques like VEXPS and EXAFS with first-principles DFT simulations to map the atomic and electronic structure changes at the interface.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting Nb film, which forms native oxides ($	ext{NbO}_x$), followed by Pt deposition and thermal annealing. The focus is on engineering a robust, low-loss $	ext{Nb-Pt}$ alloy interface.

**Key observables.** Superconducting Transition Temperature ($T_c = 9.30 \pm 0.10$ K), Residual Resistance Ratio (RRR > 63), chemical oxidation states ($	ext{Nb}^0, 	ext{Nb}^{5+}$), and the formation of $	ext{NbPt}_3$ alloy phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Annealing temperature ($750^\circ	ext{C}$), Pt layer thickness ($\approx 10 	ext{ nm}$), and specific binding energy shifts observed in VEXPS spectra.

**Assumptions / limitations.** VEXPS fitting requires assuming specific line shape models (Voigt/Skewed Voigt) for accurate deconvolution of overlapping chemical states. The structural analysis relies on matching EXAFS data to idealized alloy models ($	ext{NbPt}_3$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show TEM cross-sections confirming layer structure, VEXPS spectra detailing oxidation state changes, and EXAFS fits demonstrating the formation of a $	ext{Pt}$-rich $	ext{NbPt}_3$ interfacial alloy.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the loss mechanism (TLS in native oxides) to proposing Pt passivation. It then uses VEXPS/DFT to characterize oxide states, followed by EXAFS analysis to confirm the specific $	ext{Nb-Pt}$ alloy structure ($	ext{NbPt}_3$), and finally validates this protective layer using cross-sectional microscopy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dielectric loss arising from two-level systems (TLS) at surfaces and interfaces remains a primary limitation to coherence in superconducting transmon qubits. Niobium (Nb), a widely used material in superconducting quantum circuits, readily forms native oxides under ambient conditions, leading to lossy dielectric interfaces that degrade device performance. Here, a robust and scalable fabrication strategy is demonstrated for chemically stabilizing Nb surfaces and mitigating further oxidation, including protection of both surface and sidewall regions. High-purity Nb films were fabricated with bulk-like superconducting transition temperatures ($T_c = 9.30\pm0.10$) K. We demonstrate that a thin Pt encapsulation layer, deposited after native oxide formation, can be transformed via thermal annealing into a Nb-Pt alloy at the surface. Spectroscopic and microscopic analyses confirm the formation of a chemically stable metallic alloy layer and its ability to suppress further oxide growth. Ab initio simulations elucidate the atomic-scale rearrangement and electronic structure evolution associated with Pt incorporation on native niobium oxide, providing insight into the stabilization mechanism of the alloyed surface. This approach offers a materials pathway for engineering chemically robust Nb interfaces, including sidewalls, toward higher-coherence superconducting qubit architectures."

</details>

**Summary.** The research characterizes the formation and location of hydroxyl defects within the surface oxides of superconducting Tantalum. Using advanced depth profiling techniques, the authors show that these hydroxyls accumulate in suboxide regions, acting as a source of decoherence. They demonstrate that controlled surface treatments like CMP or plasma nitridization can effectively suppress this detrimental hydroxyl incorporation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses material science limitations for superconducting qubits by providing actionable surface engineering protocols to reduce dielectric loss mechanisms relevant to quantum coherence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Surface oxides on superconducting Tantalum (Ta) host Two-Level System (TLS) defects, which are a primary source of microwave loss and decoherence in quantum circuits.

**Main result.** Hydroxyl accumulation is found preferentially in the sub-stoichiometric oxide region ($	ext{TaO}_x$), and this incorporation can be suppressed by surface engineering techniques like Chemical Mechanical Planarization (CMP) or plasma nitridization.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (SIMS) depth profiling, Angle-resolved X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (ARXPS), and Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) to characterize the oxide layers.

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting Tantalum ($\alpha$-Ta) films whose surface oxides are analyzed. The focus is on the chemical structure and defect density within these native or engineered oxide layers, which act as lossy dielectrics in quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Depth profiles of $	ext{Ta}_2	ext{O}_5$, $	ext{TaOH}$, and metallic Ta; quantification of hydroxyl species ($	ext{TaOH}^-$) concentration across different surface treatments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Oxide thickness, relative concentrations of $	ext{Ta}$ oxidation states ($	ext{Ta}^{5+}$, $	ext{TaO}_x$), and the effectiveness of mitigation techniques (CMP, nitridization).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accumulation of hydroxyls in the suboxide region is hypothesized to be the molecular origin of TLS defects causing decoherence.

**Figures summary.** Figures show ARXPS analysis confirming a stratified oxide structure; SIMS depth profiles illustrate $	ext{TaOH}$ accumulation beneath the surface layer, and comparative figures quantify the reduction of this hydroxyl content after CMP or nitridization treatments.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem (TLS loss in Ta oxides) using ARXPS to map chemical states. It then uses SIMS depth profiling to pinpoint hydroxyl accumulation in suboxides. Finally, it compares native vs. engineered surfaces (CMP/nitrided) via quantitative measurements to demonstrate mitigation feasibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, tantalum (Ta) has gained attention in superconducting quantum circuits due to the longer coherence times achieved when replacing niobium (Nb) in capacitor pads. Previous literature shows that surface oxides that form upon ambient exposure on superconducting metals such as Ta, Al, and Nb host two-level system (TLS) defects, which are a leading source of microwave loss and decoherence. While the surface oxides of Nb and Al have been extensively studied, Ta oxides remain less well understood. Using secondary ion mass spectrometry of alpha-Ta films deposited at 300 mm wafer scale, we show for the first time that hydroxyls accumulate in the Ta suboxide region above the underlying Ta. Angle-resolved X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy shows that the surface region is dominated by Ta2O5, with sub-stoichiometric TaOx present in between the Ta2O5 and underlying Ta. The thickness of the tantalum oxide is confirmed by transmission electron microscopy. We demonstrate that [OH] incorporation can be suppressed by replacing the native oxide with an oxide formed during chemical mechanical planarization of alpha-Ta films. Our findings support the hypothesis that TLS defects are non-uniform within the oxide thickness and suggest hydroxyls as a probable molecular origin of these loss channels. Furthermore, we show the feasibility of plasma nitridization as a method to decrease hydroxyl loading on alpha-Ta surfaces. The modulation of hydroxyl content through surface engineering of alpha-Ta can enable the fabrication of more robust, high-coherence superconducting quantum circuits by addressing a potential TLS source.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate a universal logic circuit by utilizing gate-controlled switches based on superconducting nanowires operating at liquid helium temperatures. By controlling the supercurrent via an applied gate voltage, they create reliable switching elements capable of implementing all classical logic gates (AND, OR, NOT) and combinational circuits. This work establishes a functional, scalable building block for future hybrid superconducting computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware implementation for classical computation using superconducting electronics, providing a solid experimental platform that could be relevant for developing control logic in quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a superconducting building block for classical hybrid supercomputers due to scaling limitations and power consumption issues in conventional CMOS devices.

**Main result.** A universal logic circuit, realizing AND, OR, NOT, COPY gates, and the half-adder using only three gate-controlled superconducting nanowires was demonstrated.

**Method.** The functionality is achieved by exploiting the Gate-Controlled Supercurrent (GCS) effect in superconducting nanowires, which acts as a voltage-controlled switch between superconducting and normal states.

**Model / system.** The physical platform consists of superconducting nanowires (NbTiN) fabricated on silicon chips, controlled electrostatically via side-gate electrodes. Operation requires cryogenic temperatures ($	ext{T} \approx 4	ext{ K}$).

**Key observables.** Switching current ($I_{sw}$), critical current ($I_c$) vs. gate voltage ($V_{sg}$), and the resulting output voltage (high $\approx 2	ext{ V}$ in normal state, zero in superconducting state).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature ($	ext{LHe}$), gate voltages ($	ext{V}_{	ext{g,onset}} \approx 30	ext{ V}$, $	ext{V}_{	ext{g,offset}} \approx 40	ext{ V}$), and bias currents ($I_b$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The logic operation relies on setting the bias current below the retrapping current during switching events; minor scattering is attributed to thermal fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $V$-$I$ curves showing gate-controlled switching (SC $\leftrightarrow$ N states) and time-domain plots demonstrating the truth tables for AND, OR, NOT, XOR, etc., gates using specific input/output voltage levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically demonstrates the realization of basic logic gates (AND, OR, NOT, COPY) first, then expands to more complex combinational circuits like the half-adder and even a ring oscillator, proving the universality of the architecture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The observation of the gate-controlled supercurrent (GCS) effect in superconducting nanostructures initiated major research efforts toward the realisation of superconducting-based computing architectures. Here we introduce a universal logic circuit that can be a promising superconducting building block of classical hybrid supercomputers. We demonstrate a functionally complete set of logic gates by realising the AND, OR, NOT and COPY gates. The general layout and scalability of our device, combined with recent experiments demonstrating fast switching and small voltage signals, make it a functional candidate in superconducting electronics. Our device enables the realization of all classical logic gates and the half-adder combinational logic circuit using at most three nanowires, each uniquely configured with two side-gate electrodes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the Spin Seebeck Effect (SSE) as a definitive transport diagnostic tool to identify altermagnetic materials. By modeling magnon-phonon coupling, the authors show that altermagnets exhibit unique directional anisotropy and resonant features in the SSE that are absent or different in conventional antiferromagnets. This provides a clear pathway for experimental verification of novel magnetic orders.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of non-equilibrium Green's functions/Boltzmann theory for spin transport bridges concepts relevant to open quantum systems and energy dissipation mechanisms studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find experimentally accessible transport signatures that can distinguish altermagnets from conventional ferromagnets and antiferromagnets.

**Main result.** The Spin Seebeck Effect (SSE) exhibits pronounced, directionally anisotropic features and resonant peaks due to magnon-polaron coupling, providing a robust signature for altermagnetic order.

**Method.** Theoretical calculation using the Boltzmann transport equation applied to hybridized magnon-phonon modes derived from a minimal spin Hamiltonian model.

**Model / system.** The study uses a toy magnetic lattice model incorporating nearest and next-nearest neighbor couplings, distinguishing between altermagnets ($J_2 
eq J'_2$) and antiferromagnets ($J_2 = J'_2$). The analysis extends to include magnetoelastic interactions leading to magnon-polaron coupling.

**Key observables.** Spin Seebeck coefficient ($	ext{S}_{\alphaeta}$), directional anisotropy ($	ext{S}_{xx} 
eq 	ext{S}_{yy}$), and resonant peak positions in the SSE vs. magnetic field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field strength ($H$), coupling constants ($J_1, J_2, D$), and phonon/magnon frequencies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a minimal toy model and utilizes approximations like the spin-wave approximation and linear resonant coupling between magnons and phonons.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate magnon dispersion relations showing band splitting in altermagnets versus degeneracy in antiferromagnets, and compare directional SSE measurements for both types of magnets.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for a diagnostic probe (SSE) to developing the theoretical framework by modeling the Hamiltonian, calculating the coupled magnon-polaron dynamics, and finally demonstrating the unique anisotropic signatures of altermagnetism in transport coefficients.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets, distinguished by compensated antiparallel spins yet nondegenerate magnon spectra, bridge the gap between ferromagnets and antiferromagnets. Although several probes such as anisotropic transport and spectroscopic measurements have been proposed to identify altermagnetic order, experimentally accessible transport signatures remain highly desirable. Here, we show that in altermagnets subject to an external magnetic field, the spin Seebeck effect exhibits pronounced directional anisotropy. Specifically, the spin Seebeck coefficient differs significantly along in-plane directions, and this anisotropy increases with field strength. Magnetoelastic interactions further produce resonant peaks whose positions with respect to an applied magnetic field reflect the intrinsic magnon band anisotropy, and provide localized features that enhance the distinguishability of the altermagnetic response. The peaks provide a robust experimental signature of altermagnetic order. Our findings serve as signatures of altermagnetic order while laying the groundwork for their application in spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using arrays of electrostatically gated quantum dots in bilayer graphene to achieve controllable valley-dependent electron optics. By exploiting the valley-dependent mass term induced by gating, the QDs can act as precise switches: identical pairs split the current into two valleys, while oppositely gated pairs filter one valley out. This establishes a realistic platform for manipulating spin/valley polarization without external fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models coherent electron transport through engineered nanoscale potentials (QDs), which is highly relevant to open quantum systems dynamics and mesoscopic physics where boundary conditions dictate wave function evolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a tunable platform for valley-dependent electron optics in bilayer graphene (BLG) using electrostatically defined quantum dots (QDs).

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that QD arrays can function as controllable valley splitters or filters, enabling the generation and steering of highly valley-polarized currents with strong suppression of forward transmission.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis employing a four-band continuum model and generalized multiple-scattering formalism to calculate valley-resolved current densities ($\mathbf{j}_	au$).

**Model / system.** The system is Bernal-stacked BLG containing electrostatically defined QDs. The key mechanism relies on layer-antisymmetric gating, which induces a valley-dependent mass-like term in the Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Valley-resolved currents ($\mathbf{j}_K$ vs $\mathbf{j}_{K'}$), angular profiles, and valley polarization $P$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate asymmetries (e.g., 152 meV), dot radius ($R=10 	ext{ nm}$), Fermi energy ($\approx 19 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ballistic transport and uses approximations like truncating the angular momentum sum at a finite cutoff $M$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate far-field angular dependence of valley currents (plane-wave vs. Gaussian beam), current decomposition ($\mathbf{j}_{	ext{inc}} + \mathbf{j}_{	ext{sc}} + \mathbf{j}_{	ext{int}}$), and engineered patterns showing splitting or filtering effects in multi-dot arrays.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from single-dot scattering analysis to Gaussian beam excitation, then details the decomposition of total current into incident, scattered, and interference parts for multiple dots, culminating in demonstrating complex array functionalities like valley splitting and filtering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electrostatically defined quantum dots (QDs) with layer-antisymmetric gating in Bernal-stacked bilayer graphene (BLG) open a local gap and generate a mass-like term with opposite sign in the two valleys, producing strongly valley-dependent scattering without magnetic fields, strain, or spin-orbit coupling. Building on this mechanism, we propose a tunable platform based on such QDs for valley-dependent electron optics in BLG. Using a four-band continuum model and a generalized multiple-scattering formalism, we analyze scattering of Gaussian electron beams from single- and multi-dot architectures and compute valley-resolved currents and angular profiles. A single dot produces distinct valley-dependent deflection, while multi-dot configurations enable enhanced control: identical-dot arrays act as valley splitters, whereas oppositely gated pairs function as valley filters. Combining these elements yields tunable generation, steering, and filtering of highly valley-polarized currents with strong suppression of forward transmission. The required energy scales, gate asymmetries, and device dimensions are within experimentally accessible regimes for dual-gated BLG, establishing quantum-dot arrays as a realistic platform for controllable valley-resolved electron optics.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors developed a novel, source-free technique to measure the optical absorption of 2D TMD materials integrated into $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ photonic waveguides. By monitoring how the waveguide's internal photoluminescence is spectrally attenuated by the overlying flake, they achieve an accurate, in situ measurement of excitonic absorption. This method is crucial for designing next-generation, high-performance on-chip optoelectronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly relevant experimental methodology for quantum optics and open quantum systems research by characterizing material loss mechanisms (absorption) in solid-state devices that are critical for coupling light to quantum emitters or qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to characterize the optical absorption of 2D transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) in their operational, integrated environment.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrates a source-free, on-chip spectroscopy technique that accurately measures energy-dependent TMD absorption by monitoring spectral attenuation of guided photoluminescence.

**Method.** Evanescent absorption spectroscopy is performed by using the intrinsic defect-related photoluminescence from the $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ waveguide core as an internal probe source, which is then attenuated by the overlying TMD flake.

**Model / system.** The system involves integrating TMD flakes ($	ext{MoS}_2$, $	ext{WS}_2$) onto a silicon nitride ($	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$) photonic waveguide. The measurement probes the interaction between the guided optical mode and the material's excitonic transitions.

**Key observables.** Energy-dependent absorption spectrum ($	ext{dB/cm}$), local photoluminescence ($\mu$-PL) spectra, and spectral reshaping of propagating PL.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exciton binding energies (e.g., $\sim 1.83 	ext{ eV}$ for uniform $	ext{MoS}_2$), attenuation coefficients ($\alpha$ in $	ext{dB/cm}$), and flake coverage length ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurement assumes the broadband defect-related PL dominates the signal, allowing normalization against a reference waveguide; furthermore, near resonances, reported attenuation values are conservative lower bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare guided PL vs. local $\mu$-PL spectra, show extracted attenuation profiles, and illustrate the setup for both source-free spectroscopy and top-pumping excitation geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for in situ characterization, details the source-free evanescent absorption method using internal PL, validates this technique by comparing results to local $\mu$-PL and Raman data, and concludes by positioning the platform for future photodetector development.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

On-chip integration of two-dimensional transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) with photonic waveguides underpins a growing class of compact optoelectronic and sensing devices, whose performance depends on the optical absorption of the 2D material in this integrated environment. We demonstrate source-free, on-chip evanescent absorption spectroscopy of MoS$_2$ and WS$_2$ flakes integrated on Si$_3$N$_4$ waveguides, in which the broadband defect-related photoluminescence of the Si$_3$N$_4$ core itself acts as the internal probe. Light generated inside the core propagates under the TMD flake and is spectrally attenuated by evanescent interaction with the 2D material, and normalizing the transmitted spectrum to a reference waveguide without TMD yields the energy-dependent absorption response without any external illumination. The extracted guided absorption agrees with Raman and local micro-photoluminescence characterization and resolves differences between spatially uniform and non-uniform flakes. A complementary top-pumping scheme recovers guided WS$_2$ photoluminescence, whose spectral reshaping during propagation is quantitatively reproduced by the measured absorption. This source-free platform qualifies 2D materials in their integrated photonic environment and provides a direct route toward waveguide-integrated TMD photodetectors and sensors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using straintronic magnetic tunnel junctions (s-MTJs) as a tunable analog AC voltage amplifier. The device's conductance is controlled by strain via an applied gate voltage, allowing for linear operation in specific regimes. The key finding is that the amplification gain can be externally tuned by adjusting the bias supply voltage.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter/spintronics, the analysis of non-linear dynamic response (magnetization precession) to small signals, coupled with external circuit tuning, touches upon concepts relevant to open quantum systems and sensing mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate and analyze the potential of straintronic magnetic tunnel junctions (s-MTJs) as a novel analog AC voltage amplifier.

**Main result.** The s-MTJ can function as an analog amplifier whose gain is tunable by an external supply voltage, unlike traditional transistor amplifiers.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling involving the Landau–Lifshitz-Gilbert-Langevin (LLGL) equations to analyze magnetization dynamics under superimposed AC gate voltages.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a straintronic Magnetic Tunnel Junction (s-MTJ), whose conductance is continuously modulated by mechanical strain induced via an external gate voltage ($V_G$).

**Key observables.** Amplification factor ($	ilde{V}$ proportional to $v_g$), conductance swing, and the maximum allowed input signal amplitude before distortion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The amplification factor is proportional to the supply voltage ($V_s$); operating frequency is limited by magnetization dynamics (GHz range).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the AC gate voltage excursion remains within the analytically derived linear region of the s-MTJ's transfer characteristic.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the s-MTJ structure, its conductance vs. $V_G$ transfer curve highlighting linearity, and time-domain simulations showing signal amplification and frequency limitations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the s-MTJ as an analog platform, derives the linear conductance dependence on $V_G$, models the AC voltage amplification using LLGL dynamics, and discusses tunability and operational limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs) are known for their digital applications (memory and logic). A special class of them called "straintronic" magnetic tunnel junctions (s-MTJ) has lately emerged as a potential platform for analog applications because their conductance can be varied continuously with mechanical strain generated with a gate voltage. The conductance versus gate voltage (transfer) characteristic always has a linear region and that can be leveraged for a variety of analog applications. Here, we discuss one such application, namely analog voltage amplification. If the s-MTJ's gate voltage is fixed around the midpoint of the linear region and a small ac voltage is superimposed on it, then the ac voltage can be amplified without distortion as long as its amplitude is small enough to avoid gate voltage excursion beyond the linear region. Unlike a transistor-based voltage amplifier whose amplification is determined solely by the transistor's internal parameters - namely the transconductance and Early resistance - here the amplification can be varied by an external power supply voltage. We examine the maximum allowed amplitude and frequency of input signal for distortion-free amplification by modeling the magnetization dynamics and derive an expression for the amplification.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on methods to significantly enhance the anomalous Hall angle ($	heta_A$) in the magnetic Weyl semimetal $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$. By manipulating the material's resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity through doping or temperature changes, they achieved a large, tunable $	an	heta_A$ of up to 0.46. This breakthrough enables the fabrication of highly sensitive magnetic field detectors for spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter physics, the precise control over electronic transport coefficients in topological materials using external stimuli (doping, temperature) is relevant to understanding engineered quantum states and boundary effects that underpin many advanced quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of anomalous Hall sensors by modulating the anomalous Hall angle ($	heta_A$), which measures current conversion efficiency.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated modulation of $	an	heta_A$ up to a magnitude of 0.46 (an angle of $\approx 25^\circ$) in the magnetic Weyl semimetal $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$.

**Method.** The modulation is achieved by controlling the relationship between anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AH}$) and longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$), using techniques like chemical doping or temperature variation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$, a magnetic topological semimetal. The theoretical framework relates $	an	heta_A$ to the product of $ho_{xx}$ and $\sigma_{AH}$, allowing for systematic tuning via doping or structural changes.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall angle ($	heta_A$), anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AH}$), longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$), and magnetic field detectability ($23.5 	ext{ nT}/	ext{Hz}^{0.5}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The maximum modulation magnitude of $	an	heta_A$ (up to 0.46) is crucial, as it significantly surpasses the low angles typically found in magnetic materials.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that independent control over both $ho_{xx}$ and $\sigma_{AH}$ can be achieved through different experimental means (e.g., doping for one, temperature/thickness for the other).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $	an	heta_A$ on varying parameters like temperature or thickness, demonstrating how increasing $ho_{xx}$ and $\sigma_{AH}$ simultaneously enhances the angle.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (enhancing AHE sensitivity), develops a theoretical formulation linking $	an	heta_A$ to transport coefficients ($\sigma_{AH}, ho_{xx}$), presents experimental evidence showing modulation via doping/temperature, and concludes with high-performance sensor metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The anomalous Hall angle (θA) is a measure of the efficiency of converting a longitudinal driving current to a transverse spin-polarized Hall current. For anomalous Hall sensing, a large anomalous Hall angle can improve the sensitivity of magnetic field detection. However, modulation of this angle is challenging and magnetic materials typically have low angles of 0.1 to 3°. Here, we report modulation of the anomalous Hall angle in the magnetic Weyl semimetal Co3Sn2S2. We propose that the angle parameter tanθA can be formulated as a function of the product of electrical resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity. Our scheme was utilized to demonstrate the modulation of tanθA up to a magnitude of 0.46, corresponding to an angle of around 25°. Microfabricated anomalous Hall devices using Fe-doped Co3Sn2S2 single-crystalline nanoflakes exhibit a high Hall sensitivity of 7028 μΩucm/T and a magnetic field detectability of 23.5 nT/Hz0.5 at 1 Hz.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates the electrical control of magnon spin photocurrent in the magnetoelectric oxide $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$. Using nonlinear response theory, the authors show that an applied electric field modifies the spin current conductivity via the Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction. The results confirm that this material is a promising platform for electrically tunable optospintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from spintronics (spin currents), multiferroics (magnetoelectric coupling), and advanced many-body theory (nonlinear response/bosonic excitations), providing a theoretical framework for electrically controlling quantum magnetic responses.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how an applied electric field can electrically control the magnon spin photocurrent generated in magnetoelectric oxides.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that the Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction, induced by the electric field, significantly modifies the spin-current conductivity, confirming electrical tunability of this effect.

**Method.** Nonlinear response theory is employed to calculate the second-order nonlinear spin current conductivity ($\sigma^{(2)}$), decomposing it into one-magnon and two-magnon contributions.

**Model / system.** The physical system studied is $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$, modeled using 1D and 3D spin chain Hamiltonians that incorporate nearest-neighbor exchange, Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (DMI), and anisotropy terms.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear spin current conductivity ($\sigma^{(2)}$) components for different polarizations ($z_{xx}, z_{yy}$, etc.), resonance frequencies, and continuum spectrum features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied electric field ($E$), magnon frequency ($\Omega$), temperature (analyzed at $T=0$ in some parts), and exchange coupling constants ($J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the linear spin-wave approximation for the magnon Hamiltonian, and certain calculations assume zero temperature or slow variation of matrix elements near resonance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic models of spin chains with canting (Fig. 1), show $\sigma^{(2)}$ spectra for one-magnon and two-magnon processes in 1D (Fig. 2, Fig. 3), and present the magnon dispersion relations for $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general theory using nonlinear response formalism to specific calculations on 1D and 3D spin models, analyzing both one-magnon resonance peaks and the two-magnon continuum spectrum as functions of the external electric field.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling magnetism by electric field or current is a central topic in spintronics. In this work, we argue that the magnon spin photocurrent can also be controlled by the electric field in magnetoelectrics. Taking Cr$_2$O$_3$ as an example, we demonstrate how the spin current is modified by the electric field, using nonlinear response theory. We find that the Dzyaloshinsky--Moriya interaction induced by the applied field plays a key role in modifying spin-current conductivity, which exhibits pronounced anisotropy with respect to the light polarization. In particular, both the resonance frequency and the peak intensity show distinct dependences on the external electric field $E$, demonstrating electrical control of the spin photocurrent. In addition, we show that the two-magnon processes give rise to a continuum spectrum, a consequence of the field-induced spin canting. These results show that Cr$_2$O$_3$ is a promising platform for realizing electrically tunable spin photovoltaic effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes spin-polarized transport in the altermagnet $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$, demonstrating that charge ordering induces a real-space, atom-selective mechanism for spin current flow despite weak bulk magnetic splitting. By modeling an all-in-one tunnel junction, the authors show that controlling the atomic conduction channels—rather than just momentum overlap—allows for massive modulation of spin conductance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the concept of controlling quantum transport through engineered atomic connectivity and valence states has parallels with designing controlled coupling elements in superconducting or molecular quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate spin-polarized transport in altermagnets by moving beyond conventional momentum-space descriptions to focus on real-space electronic selectivity.

**Main result.** The charge-ordered altermagnet $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ exhibits atom-selective spin-polarized transport, allowing for orders-of-magnitude conductance modulation in a tunnel junction based on matching or mismatching atomic conduction channels.

**Method.** First-principles calculations (DFT) combined with quantum transport simulations using the Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) formalism.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$, a charge-ordered altermagnet characterized by inequivalent Fe$^{2+}$ and Fe$^{3+}$ sites within its sublattices, which dictates the spin-selective transport pathways.

**Key observables.** Spin-polarized current flow pathways, local conductance modulation ($\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{\sigma}$), and ON/OFF switching ratios in a tunnel junction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak altermagnetic spin splitting near $E_F$; doping energy ($E_{n}^s, E_{p}^s$); lattice structure details (puckered C-type stacking).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard momentum-space description fails to capture the crucial role of local and atomically-resolved electronic states in determining transport.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, band/DOS calculations showing site-resolved features, constant-energy surfaces confirming weak splitting, and real-space bond-resolved transmission ($\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{\sigma}$) for different doping configurations in a tunnel junction schematic.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the limitations of momentum-space analysis to demonstrating atom-selective transport via site-specific channels (Fe$^{3+}$ vs Fe$^{2+}$) and culminates in proposing an all-in-one tunnel junction where real-space channel matching controls conductance modulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets provide a promising platform for spin-polarized transport without net magnetization, but their transport properties are usually discussed in terms of momentum-space spin splitting. Here, using first-principles calculations and quantum transport simulations, we show that the charge-ordered altermagnet $α$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ exhibits a distinct form of real-space spin selectivity despite weak altermagnetic spin splitting near the Fermi level. The charge order creates inequivalent Fe$^{2+}$ and Fe$^{3+}$ sites within each sublattice, while the puckered C-type antiferromagnetic stacking suppresses inter-sublattice transport. As a result, electron and hole doping activate spin-polarized transport predominantly through Fe$^{3+}$- and Fe$^{2+}$-based channels, respectively. These atom-selective channels carry opposite spin polarizations on the two antiferromagnetic sublattices, giving rise to a globally compensated charge current with hidden Néel spin character. We further propose an all-in-one $α$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ tunnel junction, where matching or mismatching atom-selective conduction channels yields orders-of-magnitude conductance modulation. Our findings establish a real-space design principle for atomically controlled spin functionality and spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes how electrons scatter off skyrmion spin textures using an advanced Green's function formalism valid even in strong coupling. It predicts that this scattering generates distinct energy-dependent features like resonances and dips in the cross-section. These features are crucial for determining the material's topological and spin Hall conductivities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed quantum mechanical treatment of electron transport through emergent magnetic structures provides a rich platform for studying non-trivial topology in condensed matter systems, relevant to spintronics and topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates electron scattering from chiral spin textures, such as skyrmions, focusing on regimes where the exchange interaction is strong relative to the electron energy.

**Main result.** The full Green's function approach reveals new features in the scattering cross-section, including Ramsauer-Townsend minima and Landau-level resonances, which strongly influence the topological and spin Hall conductivities.

**Method.** The problem is solved using the Lippmann-Schwinger equation and evaluating the full Green's function formalism, applicable across all coupling strengths.

**Model / system.** It models itinerant electron scattering from a 2D skyrmion spin texture defined by $\hat{S}(r, 	heta)$. The Hamiltonian includes an exchange interaction term $J$ coupling to the electron spin ($oldsymbol{\sigma}$).

**Key observables.** Topological Hall conductivity ($\sigma_H$), Spin Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{SH}$), and the scattering cross section ($\sigma_{\alphaeta}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of exchange interaction strength ($J$) to electron energy ($E$); winding number ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism uses the full Green's function approach, avoiding approximations valid only in the weak-coupling limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate scattering cross sections ($\sigma_{\alphaeta}$) vs. energy and coupling strength, showing resonances and dips; other figures map out spin-resolved transverse currents ($J_{\alphaeta}^{tr}$) across different energy regimes relative to $J$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for a strong-coupling formalism (Lippmann-Schwinger/GF) to calculating scattering cross sections ($\sigma_{\alphaeta}$), and finally deriving how these cross-sections determine the measurable topological and spin Hall conductivities across various energy regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron scattering from chiral spin textures such as skyrmions is fundamental to the understanding of transport in more complex systems, including skyrmion crystals. Most of the previous studies have focused on the weak-coupling regime, where the exchange interaction is small compared with the electron energy. Real materials, however, often lie in the strong-coupling regime, which exhibits qualitatively different behavior. Using the Lippmann-Schwinger equation and Green's function formalism, valid for all coupling strengths, we uncover several new features in the scattering cross section, including Ramsauer-Townsend minima, pronounced intermediate-coupling resonances, and Landau-level resonances for skyrmions with larger winding numbers. These features strongly influence the topological and spin Hall conductivities, which depend sensitively on the incident electron energy. Our work provides important insights into the Hall transport in collective chiral spin textures such as the skyrmion crystal.

</details>
