**Summary.** The research introduces THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy to overcome the spatial resolution limits of traditional THz measurements in superconductors. By scanning a current-biased superconducting strip, the authors mapped localized photovoltage peaks that directly correlate with nanoscale material defects. This technique establishes a powerful new tool for investigating how disorder influences light-matter interactions and vortex dynamics in superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct, non-equilibrium optical probe into local superconducting order parameter suppression caused by material disorder, which is crucial for understanding high-Tc superconductors and moiré systems where inhomogeneity dominates physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is experimentally resolving nanoscale inhomogeneities (weak spots) within superconducting materials because their relevant energy scales are in the terahertz range, which far-field THz radiation cannot spatially resolve.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a technique to map these weak spots by observing localized photovoltage peaks associated with enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at material defects.

**Method.** They employed THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy using an AFM tip as a nanoantenna to locally probe the superconducting response while applying a DC bias current.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses superconducting films, such as NbN strips, which exhibit nanoscale inhomogeneities. The physics is modeled by considering vortex dynamics and pair-breaking effects induced by the THz near-field excitation.

**Key observables.** Photovoltage peaks ($V_{PV}$), Full Width at Half Maximum ($	ext{FWHM}$) of the peak, and the dependence of $V_{PV}$ on bias current ($I_b$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial resolution ($\sim 300 	ext{ nm}$), THz frequency ($2.52 	ext{ THz}$), and superconducting gap energy scale.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed photovoltage is attributed to enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at defect sites, which is supported by time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show local $V_{PV}$ peak maps varying with bias current and temperature, confirming localized peaks in the bulk due to defects. Comparisons are made between these measurements and DC transport characteristics ($	ext{d}V/	ext{d}I$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the limitations of far-field THz probing for nanoscale features. It then details the experimental setup, presenting results showing localized $V_{PV}$ peaks correlated with defects. Finally, it uses theoretical modeling (TDGL) to interpret the current and temperature dependence of these observed photovoltage signals.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanoscale inhomogeneities are a defining feature of many superconducting materials, yet their local electromagnetic response has remained difficult to access experimentally. This is because their relevant energy scale lies in the terahertz range, where wavelengths -- on the order of hundreds of microns -- are too large to spatially resolve nanoscopic variations. Here, we demonstrate the first application of THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy in a superconductor, achieving 300 nm spatial resolution at 2.52 THz. Scanning a current-biased NbN strip, we reveal photovoltage peaks within the bulk associated with nanoscopic defects of reduced superfluid density. The observed photovoltage follows the evolution of the vortex-dissipative state and is attributed to enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at defect sites. Together, these results open a direct route to probing how material inhomogeneities influence light-matter interactions in superconductors, with implications for superconducting devices and strongly inhomogeneous systems such as high-Tc and moiré materials.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes spin wave propagation in a ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer using magnetostatic theory. The key finding is that dipolar coupling induces significant frequency nonreciprocity, enabling the creation of unidirectional magnonic waves. This tunability—switching these directional properties via temperature or magnetic field—suggests applications for novel magnonic logic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnonics rather than quantum information directly, the concept of tunable, directional energy transport (unidirectional waves) is analogous to engineered quantum channels or non-Hermitian physics studied in AMO/quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To calculate and analyze the spectra of Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic modes (surface and bulk spin waves) in a ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer, focusing on how dipolar coupling modifies these spectra.

**Main result.** The dipolar coupling induces nonreciprocity in surface spin waves, allowing for the creation of unidirectional wave regimes that can be switched on or off by varying external magnetic fields or temperature near the Curie point.

**Method.** Analytical calculation using the magnetostatic approximation and solving coupled differential equations across the FM/PM interface.

**Model / system.** The system is an in-plane magnetized ferromagnet (FM) film placed on a paramagnet (PM) half-space. The PM's magnetic susceptibility follows the Curie-Weiss law, $\chi \propto 1/(T-T_C)$.

**Key observables.** Spin-wave frequency spectrum ($\Omega$), frequency nonreciprocity ($\Delta\Omega$), and group/phase velocities ($v_g, v_p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic permeability of the PM ($\mu$), external magnetic field ($H_0$ or $h$), and temperature ($T$) relative to the Curie temperature ($T_C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetostatic approximation is used, and the analysis assumes a thick FM film. The PM susceptibility follows the Curie-Weiss law.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of nonreciprocity ($\Delta\Omega$) on wave vector ($k$), showing unidirectional regimes; they also map out spectra for arbitrary propagation angles ($	heta$), revealing forbidden regions and mode splitting between MSSWs and BVMSWs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the governing equations using magnetostatic theory, analyzes the specific case of perpendicular propagation ($k \perp M_0$) to show nonreciprocity, then extends the analysis to arbitrary propagation angles ($	heta$), detailing how MSSW and BVMSW spectra interact and defining parameter regimes for wave confinement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the magnetostatic approximation, we calculate the spectra of bulk and surface spin waves in an in-plane magnetized ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer. Due to the dipolar coupling between the layers, the paramagnet becomes polarized, which in turn modifies the spectrum of the Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic modes. We assume that the paramagnet is characterized by a magnetic susceptibility, $χ\propto 1/(T-T_C)$, which reaches large values when the system temperature $T$ is close to the Curie temperature $T_C$. We find the conditions under which surface spin waves become unidirectional, i.e., capable of carrying energy in only one direction, and determine the magnitude of their frequency nonreciprocity. We demonstrate the possibility of switching the unidirectional wave regime on and off by varying the external magnetic field or temperature, making the ferromagnet/paramagnet system an attractive platform for tunable magnonic logic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically analyzes the stability of chiral d+id' superconductivity in twisted cuprate bilayers against quantum phase fluctuations. By incorporating these fluctuations into the free energy calculation, the authors show that the exotic chiral state is highly fragile, surviving only under extreme conditions. This work establishes quantum phase fluctuations as a critical constraint on realizing TRSB superconducting phases in low-dimensional materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how low-energy collective fluctuations can fundamentally constrain exotic superconducting orders like chiral superconductivity, providing a general theoretical framework applicable to other layered quantum materials where phase coherence is crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum phase fluctuations fundamentally alter the predicted chiral d+id' superconducting state in twisted cuprate bilayers, particularly concerning Time-Reversal Symmetry Breaking (TRSB).

**Main result.** Quantum phase fluctuations strongly suppress the stability of the chiral phase, restricting it to a narrow twist-angle window and ultra-low temperatures. This leads to a first-order transition into the regular d-wave state.

**Method.** The authors derived the free energy functional by self-consistently incorporating the renormalization of the relative interlayer phase due to dynamical quantum phase fluctuations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a continuum microscopic Hamiltonian for twisted cuprate bilayers, considering both d-wave and chiral d+id' superconducting orders. The analysis focuses on the collective behavior of the interlayer phase degree of freedom.

**Key observables.** Minimal superconducting gap (2\Delta_min) to distinguish between $d+id'$ ($>0$) and nodal $d$-wave ($=0$), and the Josephson gap ($\Delta_J$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), temperature ($T/T_{1L}^c$), and interlayer tunneling amplitude ($t$). The chiral phase is only stable at very low temperatures (e.g., $\lesssim 1.4 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a simplified local form for the interlayer tunneling $t_l(r) = t_l\delta(r)$ and relies on a derived free energy functional from companion work.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams ($	heta$–$T$) showing how quantum fluctuations narrow the region where the chiral state exists, contrasting with mean-field predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon theoretical proposals for chiral superconductivity by introducing phase fluctuation corrections to the free energy functional. It then maps out the resulting phase diagram in terms of twist angle and temperature, concluding with implications for Josephson coupling near quantum critical points.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Following theoretical proposals of chiral $d+id'$ superconductivity in twisted cuprate bilayers, experimental signatures of time-reversal symmetry breaking (TRSB) remain highly controversial. Here we demonstrate that quantum phase fluctuations fundamentally reshape the phase diagram of this proposed chiral state. Unlike regular superconducting orders, the chiral $d+id'$ state requires long-range coherence of an interlayer phase degree of freedom and is therefore intrinsically vulnerable to phase fluctuations. Incorporating these fluctuations nearly eliminates the chiral phase over most parts of the phase diagram, restricting it to a narrow twist-angle window and ultra-low temperatures. The fluctuation-driven destruction of chirality produces a first-order transition into the $d$-wave state, giving rise to coexistence and metastability. Meanwhile, Josephson phase locking is strongly weakened at the TRSB quantum critical point, which sits well within the superconducting regime. More broadly, our work establishes quantum phase fluctuations as a fundamental constraint on the emergence of TRSB phases in low-dimensional layered quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a reactive force field for GaN to simulate how defects move when subjected to high electric fields. The key finding is that the interaction is complex, causing anisotropic barrier changes due to charge redistribution and lattice distortion, rather than a simple linear shift. This provides critical insight into device reliability in extreme electrical environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state defects rather than quantum systems, the study's focus on complex, non-linear coupling between an external field (an electromagnetic perturbation) and the material's internal energy landscape shares conceptual parallels with understanding driven dynamics in open quantum systems or materials under extreme bias.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Assessing defect transport mechanisms in GaN under high operating electric fields is crucial for predicting device reliability in power electronics.

**Main result.** Electric fields modify defect migration barriers anisotropically through charge-lattice coupling, deviating significantly from simple linear dipole predictions.

**Method.** The study develops and applies a ReaxFF reactive force field trained on DFT data to simulate defect migration using CI-NEB methods under external electric fields.

**Model / system.** The system is Gallium Nitride (GaN) in its wurtzite structure, focusing on intrinsic point defects like vacancies ($	ext{V}_{	ext{Ga}}, 	ext{V}_{	ext{N}}$) and interstitials. The simulation incorporates external electric fields up to $40 	ext{ MV/cm}$.

**Key observables.** Defect formation energies, migration barriers ($E_F^B$, $E_R^B$), charge distribution variation, and the deviation from linear field dependence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric field strength (up to $0.04 	ext{ V}/	ext{\AA}$), defect type, and field orientation (parallel vs. perpendicular to migration path).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a ReaxFF force field with the QEq scheme implies that the results represent neutral defects and are a lower bound compared to fully charged states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate EOS comparisons, charge distribution changes during defect movement, and energy profiles showing the effect of external fields on migration barriers for different defect types.

**Paper structure.** The paper first validates the ReaxFF model against DFT results for structural properties. It then calculates zero-field migration barriers before systematically applying increasing electric fields to analyze the resulting modulation of forward and reverse barriers, comparing findings to linear models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A predictive understanding of defect transport in GaN under operating electric fields is critical for assessing device reliability in high-power and radiation environments. In this work, a ReaxFF reactive force field for GaN is developed using a density-functional-theory training set that includes structural, thermodynamic, and defect properties. The force field yields various properties such as lattice parameters, cohesive energies, and defect formation and migration energies in close agreement with prior first-principles and experimental results. Under externally applied electric fields, we find that migration barriers can be strongly modulated, with changes that depend on defect type and field orientation. Notably, the electric fields do not simply linearly bias defect motion in GaN, but can anisotropically modify migration barriers through charge-lattice coupling, leading to nonlinear transport behavior. The response arises from field-induced partial charge redistribution and local lattice distortion. These results demonstrate that electric fields can complexly modify the defect migration landscape, providing new insight into defect transport in GaN under high-field conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a group-theoretic framework to classify non-reciprocal current responses in quantum ring devices. Using symmetry analysis on $n$-vertex rings, the authors predict that observable non-reciprocity requires breaking both time-reversal and inversion symmetries simultaneously. The theory is validated using a three-site superconducting quantum dot model, confirming predictions for circulator and semi-circulator transport regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool—symmetry classification—to predict fundamental transport properties (non-reciprocity) in mesoscopic superconducting circuits, linking topology/geometry directly to measurable electrical response.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a symmetry-based framework to classify non-reciprocal responses occurring in multiterminal quantum ring devices.

**Main result.** Non-reciprocity requires the simultaneous breaking of both time-reversal (T) and spatial inversion (I) symmetries. The geometry dictates which specific circulator or semi-circulator responses are physically observable.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on group theory, specifically applying Burnside's Lemma to classify the $2^n$ possible current configurations into symmetry equivalence classes (orbits).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a ring of $n$ vertices with bonds characterized by preferred signal flow directions ($\pm 1$). The theoretical predictions are validated using a minimal toy model of three quantum dots coupled to superconducting baths.

**Key observables.** Non-reciprocal transmission ($S_{nr}$), the reactive component of the current-current response function ($	ext{Im}[\chi_{nr}]$), and the absolute non-reciprocal weight ($R_{nr}^{tot}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of terminals $n$ (especially $n=3$), geometric symmetry (equilateral vs. isosceles), and external fields like Zeeman field ($b_y$) or SOC ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the non-reciprocal response can be entirely classified by the underlying discrete symmetries of the ring geometry, even when microscopic details are involved.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $n$-vertex ring setup and show examples of circulator ($W=3$) and semi-circulator ($W=1$) configurations. Other figures plot non-reciprocal transmission spectra, reactive response functions ($	ext{Im}[\chi_{nr}]$), and energy level splittings as a function of symmetry breaking parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general group theory framework for $n$-rings to analyzing specific cases ($n=3$ equilateral vs. isosceles) using Burnside's lemma, culminating in validation via calculations on a minimal quantum dot toy model that predicts observable circulator and semi-circulator responses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a symmetry-based framework to classify the non-reciprocal responses of multiterminal ring quantum devices. The device is modeled as a ring of $n$ vertices, where a binary variable $e_k\in\{+1,-1\}$ on each bond encodes the preferred direction of signal flow between terminals. Non-reciprocity corresponds to a preferred current configuration on the ring, and the symmetry group of the device partitions all $2^n$ configurations into equivalence classes(orbits) characterized by a topological winding number $W$. Using the minimal non-trivial case $n=3$, we establish two results independent of microscopic details. First, lifting the degeneracy within an orbit generates non-reciprocal responses. For $n=3$ this requires simultaneous breaking of both time-reversal $T$ and spatial inversion $I$. Breaking either alone is insufficient. Second, the residual geometry symmetry after $T$ and $I$ are broken determines which responses are observable. For an isosceles triangular geometry, only two types of response are allowed: uniform circulation (all bonds carrying current in the same direction) and semi-circulation with the reversed bond on the geometrically distinct base. Semi-circulation with the reversed bond on either equal leg is symmetry-forbidden. Both predictions are validated using a minimal toy model of three quantum dots coupled to superconducting baths, which demonstrates a reactive quantum circulator response.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the existence of supercurrents confined specifically to certain facets of a weak topological insulator ($	ext{ZrTe}_5$). By using superconducting interferometry, the authors confirmed that this facet-selectivity and ballistic nature are direct consequences of the material's underlying bulk topology. This establishes WTIs as a promising platform for designing next-generation, topologically controlled superconducting devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides direct experimental evidence linking bulk topological invariants (WTI classification) to macroscopic quantum phenomena (supercurrent distribution), which is highly relevant for understanding engineered quantum materials interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to demonstrate and characterize facet-selective ballistic supercurrent flow in three-dimensional weak topological insulators (WTIs). This addresses the challenge of confining superconducting currents to specific crystal facets.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated SQUID-like critical current oscillations confined to specific crystallographic facets, confirming that the supercurrent is dictated by the underlying bulk topology of the WTI material ($	ext{ZrTe}_5$).

**Method.** Superconducting quantum interferometry was performed on Josephson junctions formed with $	ext{ZrTe}_5$, supplemented by STM/STS measurements and analysis of magnetic field dependence.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes Weak Topological Insulators ($	ext{ZrTe}_5$), which possess gapless topological surface states only on selected facets, contrasting with Strong TIs. Superconductivity is induced via proximity coupling in Josephson junctions.

**Key observables.** SQUID-like critical current oscillations ($I_c$ vs $B$), exponential temperature dependence of $I_c$, and distinct interference patterns dependent on the magnetic field orientation relative to crystal facets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The supercurrent is confined to specific side facets, exhibiting ballistic transport signatures characterized by an exponential temperature dependence ($\exp(-\pi^2 k_B T / \delta E)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurements assume that the observed superconducting current flow is dominated by the topological surface states rather than trivial bulk conduction channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SQUID-like $I$-$V$ curves and interference patterns (Fig. 4) confirming facet confinement, alongside STM/STS data showing a bulk gap and enhanced LDOS at step edges (Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical basis for WTI surface currents, details the experimental setup using Josephson junctions on $	ext{ZrTe}_5$, presents measurements confirming SQUID oscillations and field-orientation dependence, and concludes by establishing WTIs as a platform for facet-resolved superconducting devices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological superconductivity is widely pursued by inducing superconducting correlations in topologically protected boundary states. In two dimensions, this strategy has been realized using one-dimensional topological edge modes, but in three-dimensional crystals, spatially separated surface supercurrents confined to selected facets have not yet been achieved. Here we demonstrate facet-selective ballistic supercurrent in Josephson junctions based on the weak topological insulator ZrTe<sub>5</sub>. Superconducting quantum interferometry reveals SQUID-like critical current oscillations with flux-quantum periodicity, establishing that the supercurrent is spatially concentrated on specific crystallographic facets that host gapless topological surface states. Rotating the magnetic field yields markedly distinct interference patterns, linking the supercurrent distribution to the underlying bulk topology. The exponential temperature dependence of the critical current and triangular interference lobes provide signatures of ballistic transport due to high-transmission topological channels. These results establish weak topological insulators as a platform for facet-resolved superconducting devices and higher-order topological superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes the guided optical modes in WS2 nanobeams and quasi-bulk crystals using both near-field microscopy (s-SNOM) and cavity spectroscopy (iCEAS). The authors establish that s-SNOM is a robust tool for measuring relative modal dispersion across visible-NIR wavelengths, while also quantifying material absorption to improve the feasibility of integrating these 2D materials into photonic circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of advanced optical characterization techniques like s-SNOM and iCEAS to a 2D material platform provides crucial experimental benchmarks for designing next-generation integrated photonic circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the limited experimental understanding of wavelength-dependent modal confinement and attenuation in WS2 waveguides, complicated by ambiguities in material extinction coefficient values.

**Main result.** s-SNOM reliably captures relative modal trends for TE0 and TM0 modes across 800–1400 nm, confirming its utility as a diagnostic tool despite absolute loss dependence on measurement geometry.

**Method.** The authors employed hyperspectral cavity-enhanced imaging (iCEAS) to constrain material absorption and utilized scattering-type s-SNOM microscopy to probe guided modes in quasi-bulk and nanobeam WS2 waveguides.

**Model / system.** The physical system is WS2, a layered van der Waals semiconductor promising for nanophotonics due to its high refractive index and anisotropy. The analysis involves modeling TE0 and TM0 modes using Lumerical FDE simulations alongside experimental measurements on patterned nanobeams and bulk flakes.

**Key observables.** Effective indices (neff), modal confinement, propagation loss ($	ext{dB}/	ext{mm}$), birefringence ($\Delta n$), and the extinction coefficient ($\kappa$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavelength range of 800–1400 nm; waveguide thicknesses around 112 nm to 200 nm; nanobeam widths from 400 nm to 650 nm.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Absolute propagation losses measured by s-SNOM are influenced by measurement geometry and coupling factors, requiring relative trend analysis. Artifacts due to transverse interference in narrow beams must be accounted for.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate atomic structure, simulated effective index maps for TE0/TM0 modes, s-SNOM images showing fringe patterns across varying wavelengths, and plots correlating extracted neff and decay constants with mode filling factor or waveguide width.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically moves from characterizing the material properties (absorption via iCEAS) to simulating ideal modal behavior (FDE simulations), and finally performing detailed near-field measurements using s-SNOM on both quasi-bulk crystals and patterned nanobeams, analyzing mode dispersion and leakage effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The exceptionally high in-plane refractive index, low sub-bandgap absorption, and strong optical anisotropy of WS2 make it a promising material platform for next-generation integrated circuits for nanophotonics. Its layered van der Waals structure further enables heterogeneous integration with silicon photonics and emerging two-dimensional optoelectronic materials. However, despite increasing interest in the waveguiding properties of WS2, experimental studies of wavelength-dependent modal confinement and attenuation remain limited. Additionally, though the extinction coefficient of WS2 is expected to be near-negligible beneath the bandgap, reported values span orders of magnitude, leading to large uncertainty in predicted modal decay lengths and wafer-scale integration feasibility. To resolve these ambiguities we perform hyperspectral cavity-enhanced imaging, determining high-resolution upper and lower bounds on the extinction coefficient of WS2 within the visible-NIR edge. We further employ scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy (s-SNOM) to probe TE0, TM0, and higher-order modes in both quasi-bulk and nanobeam WS2 waveguides across the 800-1400 nm spectral range, enabling identification of mode-specific trends in wavevector dispersion and loss. This work simultaneously assesses s-SNOM as a probe of waveguide performance, and we find that while absolute loss values depend on measurement geometry, s-SNOM reliably captures relative modal trends and provides upper bounds on propagation loss, supporting its use as a diagnostic tool for anisotropic waveguides. We further identify significant artefacts in nanobeam measurements arising from transverse interference and spatial sampling effects when the structure size approaches the excitation wavelength, which can shift extracted effective indices by up to 0.25.

</details>

**Authors:** Yin Shi, Mengxian Zhao, Fei Yang, Miao Liu, Sheng Meng

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how low-energy phase fluctuations affect superconductivity in twisted d-wave bilayers, which are candidates for high-Tc chiral superconductors. By including all orders of Josephson coupling, the authors show that these fluctuations modify the stiffnesses of superconducting phases and induce a gap in the relative-phase mode. This indicates a breakdown of Josephson phase locking near quantum critical points.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of collective modes and the role of higher-order couplings in determining superconducting stability is highly relevant to understanding pairing mechanisms and quantum critical points in strongly correlated superconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes low-energy phase fluctuations in twisted d-wave superconducting bilayers, which are predicted to host high-temperature chiral superconductivity.

**Main result.** Phase fluctuations significantly modify the collective mode dynamics, suppressing relative-phase stiffness but enhancing overall-phase stiffness in the $d+id'$ phase. The relative-phase mode acquires a gap that vanishes near the quantum critical point.

**Method.** The authors employ a theoretical analysis using an effective action approach, systematically retaining Josephson coupling to all orders and expanding fluctuations to quadratic order.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as twisted cuprate bilayers exhibiting $d$-wave superconductivity, starting from a continuum microscopic model including interlayer tunneling and long-range Coulomb repulsion.

**Key observables.** Superfluid stiffnesses ($D_0, D_\pi$), the relative-phase excitation gap ($\Delta_J$), and the overall phase stiffness ($D_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle, temperature (low T regime), interlayer tunneling amplitude ($t$), and superconducting order parameters ($\Delta_l$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to low-energy, long-wavelength fluctuations, and the derivation relies on expanding fluctuation terms up to quadratic order.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate minimal superconducting gaps in the twist-angle–temperature plane, superfluid stiffnesses vs. temperature/twist angle, and Josephson gap dependence on parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving an effective action for phase fluctuations by integrating out fermionic fields to calculating specific observables like stiffnesses and gaps using mean-field theory corrections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted cuprate bilayers have been predicted to host high-temperature chiral $d+id'$ superconductivity, originating from higher-order Josephson coupling processes. In such two-dimensional superconducting systems, long-wavelength fluctuations in the phase of the superconducting order parameter constitute gapless collective modes and therefore remain significant even at zero temperature. Here, we perform a theoretical analysis of the low-energy phase fluctuations in twisted $d$-wave superconducting bilayers, systematically retaining Josephson coupling to all orders. We demonstrate that higher-order Josephson coupling processes lead to nontrivial modifications of the phase dynamics. In particular, the stiffness of the relative phase between the two layers is finite even in the normal state, which is a direct consequence of the intralayer U(1) symmetry breaking explicitly induced by interlayer tunneling. Phase fluctuations suppress the relative-phase stiffness but enhance the overall-phase stiffness in the $d+id'$ phase. Furthermore, the relative-phase fluctuations acquire a small but finite excitation gap, which is no longer equal to the Josephson plasma frequency and is strongly suppressed upon approaching the chiral quantum critical point, indicating the breakdown of Josephson phase locking within the superconducting state.

</details>
